Cluster Algebras and Scattering Diagrams Part II Cluster Patterns and Scattering Diagrams Thanks: This is the final manuscript of Part II of the monograph “Cluster Algebras and Scattering Diagrams”, MSJ Mem. 41 (2023) by the author.
Tomoki Nakanishi Graduate School of Mathematics, Nagoya University

Abstract
We review some important results by Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Kontsevich on cluster algebra theory, namely, the column sign-coherence of C C -matrices and the Laurent positivity , both of which were conjectured by Fomin and Zelevinsky. We digest and reconstruct the proofs of these conjectures by Gross et al. still based on their scattering diagram method, however, without relying on toric/birational geometry. At the same time, we also give a detailed account of the correspondence between the notions of cluster patterns and scattering diagrams. Most of the results in this part are found in or translated from the known results in the literature. However, the approach, the construction of logic and proofs, and the overall presentation are new. Also, as an application, we show that there is a one-to-one correspondence between g g -vectors and cluster variables in cluster patterns with arbitrary coefficients.



[page=1]

[depth=2]

0.0
Introduction to Part II

In this part we review some important results by Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Kontsevich
[
GHKK18
]
on
cluster algebra theory, namely, the
column sign-coherence of
C
-matrices

and the
Laurent positivity
, both of which were conjectured by Fomin and Zelevinsky
[
FZ07
,
FZ02
]
.

Let us start by recalling the background.
In the paper
[
FZ07
]
Fomin and Zelevinsky developed a
new method to study

cluster patterns
, which are the underlying algebraic and combinatorial
structure to define cluster algebras.
The basic ingredients of the method are
C
-matrices,
G
-matrices, and

F
-polynomials
associated with a given cluster pattern.
Through the
separation formulas
, the cluster pattern
is recovered from them.
Moreover,
one can regard
C
- and
G
-matrices as the
tropical part
of
the cluster pattern, while
F
-polynomials as the
nontropical part
,
so that
the separation formulas naturally separate these two parts.
In many applications of cluster algebras or cluster patterns, this separation
plays a central role.

In
[
FZ07
]
several conjectures on the properties of

C
- and
G
-matrices and
F
-polynomials were also proposed.
Among them, there are two prominent conjectures, namely,

•

The sign-coherence conjecture
:
Every
C
-matrix is column sign-coherent.

•

The Laurent positivity conjecture
:
Every
F
-polynomial has no negative coefficients.
(It is equivalent to the earlier conjecture in
[
FZ02
]

on the positivity of the coefficients in the Laurent polynomial expressions for cluster variables.)

Due to their importance,
these conjectures, together with other conjectures in
[
FZ07
]
, have been intensively
studied, and proved in some special cases by various methods.
Most notably, in the skew-symmetric case,
the sign-coherence conjecture was proved
by
[
DWZ10
,
Pla11
,
Nag13
]
,
and the Laurent positivity conjecture was proved by
[
LS15
,
Dav18
]
.
However, the extending these proofs to the most general case, i.e., the skew-symmetrizable case, seemed difficult and demanding some new ideas.

In
[
GHKK18
]
Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Kontsevich finally proved
both conjectures together in full generality by the
scattering diagram method
.
The scattering diagrams were originally introduced in
[
KS06
]

to study the toric degenerations of Calabi-Yau manifolds in homological mirror symmetry
in two dimensions,
and they were generalized to higher dimensions by

[
GS11
]
.

cluster patterns

scattering diagrams

G
-matrices

chambers

C
-matrices

normal vectors of walls

F
-polynomials

wall-crossing automorphisms

cluster variables

theta functions

Table 1:
Correspondence between
the notions of
cluster patterns and scattering diagrams

Roughly speaking, we have the correspondence between
the notions in
cluster patterns and scattering diagrams
as in Table
1
.
Let us admit this correspondence.
Then,
by the definition/construction of scattering diagrams,
any normal vector of a wall is either positive or negative.
This proves the sign-coherence conjecture.
On the other hand, any theta function has a
manifestly positive combinatorial description
by
broken lines

as shown in
[
GHKK18
]
.
This proves the Laurent positivity conjecture.

Therefore, establishing the above correspondence
is the crucial step to prove the conjectures.

In this part we digest and reconstruct the results
and the proofs of the above theorems
in
[
GHKK18
]

still based on their scattering diagram method,
however,

without relying on
toric/birational geometry therein
.
To be more specific,
we set the following guidelines while preparing the manuscript:

•

We assume that readers are familiar with basic concepts in
cluster algebras to some extent, for example, seeds and mutations, cluster variables,
coefficients, the Laurent phenomenon, the finite type classification, etc.

•

The goal of the part is to give reasonably self-contained proofs of the sign-coherence
and the Laurent positivity theorems
based on the formulation of cluster patterns by
[
FZ07
]

with the help of some key properties of scattering diagrams from
[
GHKK18
]
.

•

At the same time, we also give a detailed account of the correspondence
between the notions of cluster patterns
and scattering diagrams in Table
1
.

•

We employ the Fock-Goncharov decomposition
of mutations
with tropical sign

to work on
F
-polynomials.
This substitutes and avoids the toric/birational geometrical setting in
[
GHKK18
]
,
including cluster varieties,
which plays a key role at several points in their original proofs of the conjectures.

•

We rely crucially on some basic properties of scattering diagrams in
[
GHKK18
]

that are independent of the geometrical setting therein
.
The details and the proofs of these results will be given in Part III
of this monograph.

•

Our approach is entirely based on the formulation of cluster patterns
in
[
FZ07
]
.
However, after the paper
[
FZ07
]
,
considerable progress has been made in the subject.
So, we also take it into account and reorganize and/or streamline some part of the formulation.
Most notably, we do not rely on the notion of principal coefficients explicitly
except
for
the polynomial property of
F
-polynomials.

Most of the results in this part are found in or
translated from the known results
in
[
GHKK18
]
,
and/or
in other related literatures,
especially, a series of papers by Reading

[
RS16
,
Rea14
,
Rea20b
,
Rea20a
]
.
However, the approach, the construction of logic and proofs, and the overall presentation are
new.
For example, we prove the sign-coherence conjecture
inductively along the
n
-regular tree graph

together with
establishing
the correspondence in Table
1

for
C
- and
G
-matrices.
This approach is completely different from the toric geometrical argument in
[
GHKK18
]
.
On the other hand, it is close to the approach in
[
Rea14
,
Rea20b
]

using
mutation fans
therein.
In fact, many of our results can be also obtained from
the results on the mutations fans therein.
A subtle but important difference is that we work on
C
- and
G
-matrices
without referring to mutation fans at all.
Therefore, our approach is more direct.
To summarize, our presentation
is complementary to the above references,
and we believe it serves as an alternative guide for
the scattering diagram formalism for cluster algebras.

Finally let us mention that there are already several excellent applications
of the scattering diagram method to cluster algebra theory (e.g.,
[
Mul16
,
CGM+17
,
Bri17
,
CHL20
,
CL20
,
Zho20
,
Qin19
,
Mou19
,
DM21
]
).

0.1
Cluster patterns and separation formulas

In this section we recall several basic notions and facts on
cluster patterns

by following the formulation of Fomin and Zelevinsky especially in
[
FZ07
]
.
Most of the materials are taken or adapted from
[
FZ07
]
, and
we assume that the readers are familiar with them to some extent.
Thus, we skip the proofs of the results unless we think necessary.

0.1.1
Seeds and mutations

Below we fix a positive integer
n
,
which is called the
rank
of the forthcoming seeds, cluster patterns,
etc
.

Definition 0.1.1

(Skew-symmetrizable matrix)
.

An
n\times n
integer matrix
B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}

is said to be
skew-symmetrizable

if there is a positive rational diagonal matrix
D=(d_{i}\delta_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}

such that
DB
is skew-symmetric,
i.e,

\displaystyle d_{i}b_{ij}=-d_{j}b_{ji}

(0.1.1)

holds. The matrix
D
is called a
(left) skew-symmetrizer
of
B
.

Remark 0.1.2
.

Often a skew-symmetrizer
D
is assumed to be an
integer
matrix.
However, it is natural to use a rational one
in order to match with the convention in
[
GHKK18
]
.
See (
0.2.44
). So, we employ the condition here.

For a skew-symmetrizable matrix
B
,
its diagonal entries
b_{ii}
vanish
due to (
0.1.1
).
Also, the condition (
0.1.1
) is rephrased as

\displaystyle DBD^{-1}=-B^{T},

(0.1.2)

where for any matrix
M
,
M^{T}
stands for the transpose of
M
.

For our purpose,
we especially employ the following (nonstandard) definition of seeds.

Definition 0.1.3

(Seed)
.

Let

\mathcal{F}_{X}
and
\mathcal{F}_{Y}
be a pair of fields both of which are isomorphic to
the rational function field of
n
-variables
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}
.
A
(labeled) seed in
(\mathcal{F}_{X},\mathcal{F}_{Y})

is a triplet

\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
,
where

\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})

are
n
-tuples of free generating elements
in
\mathcal{F}_{X}
and
\mathcal{F}_{Y}
, respectively,
and
B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}

is an
n\times n

skew-symmetrizable (integer) matrix.
We call
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}
, and
B
,
respectively,
the
x
-variables
,
the
y
-variables
,
and the
exchange matrix
of
\Sigma
.
We also call the pair
(\mathcal{F}_{X},\mathcal{F}_{Y})
the
ambient fields

of a seed
\Sigma

and the forthcoming cluster patterns,
etc
.

Remark 0.1.4
.

In the standard terminology of
[
FZ07
]
,
the
x
-variables above are
cluster variables without coefficients
,
while the
y
-variables above are
coefficients in the
universal semifield

\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})\subset\mathcal{F}_{Y}
.
Here,
x
- and
y
-variables are on an equal footing and independent of each other.
In particular,

y
-variables do
not
serve as coefficients for
x
-variables.

For any seed
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
,
we attach an
n
-tuple

\hat{\mathbf{y}}=(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})

of elements in the ambient field
\mathcal{F}_{X}
defined by

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{i}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{b_{ji}}.

(0.1.3)

They play an important role in cluster algebra theory.
We call them
\hat{y}
-variables
.
They are algebraically independent if and only if
B
is nonsingular.

For any integer
a
,
we define

\displaystyle[a]_{+}:=\max(a,0).

(0.1.4)

We have a useful identity:

\displaystyle a=[a]_{+}-[-a]_{+}.

(0.1.5)

Definition 0.1.5

(Seed mutation)
.

For any seed
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)

and
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
we define a new seed

\Sigma^{\prime}=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},B^{\prime})

by the following rule:

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{k}^{-1}\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{[-b_{jk}]_{+}}\Biggr)(1+\hat{y}_{k})&i=k,\\
x_{i}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.1.6)

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k}^{-1}&i=k,\\
y_{i}y_{k}^{[b_{ki}]_{+}}(1+y_{k})^{-b_{ki}}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.1.7)

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-b_{ij}&\text{$i=k$ or $j=k$,}\\
b_{ij}+b_{ik}[b_{kj}]_{+}+[-b_{ik}]_{+}b_{kj}&i,j\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.1.8)

where
\hat{y}_{k}
in (
0.1.6
) is the
\hat{y}
-variable in (
0.1.3
).
The seed
\Sigma^{\prime}
is called the
mutation of
\Sigma
in direction
k
,
and denoted by
\mu_{k}(\Sigma)=\mu_{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
.

The following facts
ensure that
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is indeed a seed.

(a).

If
D
is a skew-symmetrizer of
B
,
then it is also a skew-symmetrizer of
B^{\prime}
.
In particular,
the matrix
B^{\prime}
is skew-symmetrizable.

(b).

Regard the mutation
\mu_{k}
as a map from the
set of seeds to itself. Then,
\mu_{k}
is an involution.
In particular,
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
are free
generating elements
in
\mathcal{F}_{X}
and
\mathcal{F}_{Y}
, respectively.

Also, one can easily verify that
|B^{\prime}|=|B|
,
so that
B^{\prime}
is nonsingular if and only if
B
is nonsingular.

The following fact can be easily verified.

Proposition 0.1.6

(
[
FZ07
, Prop. 3.9]
)
.

The
\hat{y}
-variables mutate as

\displaystyle\hat{y}^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\hat{y}_{k}^{-1}&i=k,\\
\hat{y}_{i}\hat{y}_{k}^{[b_{ki}]_{+}}(1+\hat{y}_{k})^{-b_{ki}}&i\neq k\end{cases}

(0.1.9)

in the ambient field
\mathcal{F}_{X}

by the same rule as (
0.1.7
).

There is some flexibility to write the mutation formulas
(
0.1.6
)–(
0.1.8
) as follows.
(The choice
\varepsilon=1
reduces to the one
in (
0.1.6
)–(
0.1.8
).)

Proposition 0.1.7

(
[
Nak12
, Eqs. (2.9), (2.10)]
,

[
BFZ05
, Eq. (3.1)]
)
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}
.
Then, the right hand sides of the
following equalities do not depend on the
choice of
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{k}^{-1}\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{[-\varepsilon b_{jk}]_{+}}\Biggr)(1+\hat{y}_{k}^{\varepsilon})&i=k,\\
x_{i}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.1.10)

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k}^{-1}&i=k,\\
y_{i}y_{k}^{[\varepsilon b_{ki}]_{+}}(1+y_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{-b_{ki}}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.1.11)

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-b_{ij}&\text{$i=k$ or $j=k$,}\\
b_{ij}+b_{ik}[\varepsilon b_{kj}]_{+}+[-\varepsilon b_{ik}]_{+}b_{kj}&i,j\neq k.\end{cases}

(0.1.12)

The claim can be easily checked with the identity (
0.1.5
).
We call the above formulas the
\varepsilon
-expressions
of mutations.

0.1.2
Cluster patterns

Let
\mathbb{T}_{n}
be the
n
-regular tree
graph,
that is, a graph without cycles such that exactly
n
-edges are attached
to each vertex.

We assume that the edges are labeled by
1
,…,
n

such that the edges attached to each vertex
have different labels.
By abusing the notation,
the set of vertices of
\mathbb{T}_{n}
is also denoted by

\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
We say that a pair of vertices in
t
and
t^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}

are (
k
-)
adjacent
, or
t^{\prime}
is (
k
-)
adjacent to
t
,
if they are connected with an edge labeled by
k
.

Definition 0.1.8

(Cluster pattern/
B
-pattern)
.

A collection of seeds
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\{\Sigma_{t}=(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
in
(\mathcal{F}_{X},\mathcal{F}_{Y})

indexed by
\mathbb{T}_{n}

is called a
cluster pattern
if,
for any vertices
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
that are
k
-adjacent,
the equality
\Sigma_{t^{\prime}}=\mu_{k}(\Sigma_{t})
holds.
The collection of exchange matrices

\mathbf{B}=\{B_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
extracted from a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is called the
B
-pattern

of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.

We arbitrary choose a distinguished
vertex (the
initial vertex
)
t_{0}
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
Any cluster pattern

\mathbf{\Sigma}=\{\Sigma_{t}=(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
is uniquely determined from
the
initial seed

\Sigma_{t_{0}}
at the initial vertex
t_{0}

by repeating mutations.

Note that one can take a common skew-symmetrizer
D
for all
B_{t}
in
\mathbf{B}
.
By (
0.1.2
), we have

\displaystyle DB_{t}D^{-1}=-B_{t}^{T}.

(0.1.13)

In particular, if
B_{t}
is skew-symmetric for some
t
, it is skew-symmetric for any
t
.
Such a cluster pattern or a
B
-pattern is said to be
skew-symmetric
.

Similarly, if
B_{t}
is nonsingular for some
t
, it is nonsingular for any
t
.
Such a cluster pattern or a
B
-pattern is said to be
nonsingular
.

For a seed
\Sigma_{t}=(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t},B_{t})
(
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
)
in a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
,
we use the notation

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t}=(x_{1;t},\dots,x_{n;t}),\quad\mathbf{y}_{t}=(y_{1;t},\dots,y_{n;t}),\quad B_{t}=(b_{ij;t})_{i,j=1}^{n}.

(0.1.14)

For the initial seed
\Sigma_{t_{0}}=(\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}},\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}},B_{t_{0}})
, we often drop the indices
t_{0}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),\quad\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}),\quad B_{t_{0}}=B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n},

(0.1.15)

if there is no confusion.
We also use similar notations for
\hat{y}
-variables
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{t}=(\hat{y}_{1;t},\dots,\hat{y}_{n;t})

and the initial

\hat{y}
-variables
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{0}}=\hat{\mathbf{y}}=(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})
.

We recall the definition of cluster algebras,
though
the focus of this part is
to study the properties of
cluster patterns

rather than cluster algebras.

Definition 0.1.9

(Cluster algebra)
.

For a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
,
the associated
cluster algebra

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})

is the
\mathbb{Z}
-subalgebra of the ambient field
\mathcal{F}_{X}

generated by all
x
-variables (cluster variables)
x_{i,t}
(
i=1,\dots,n;t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
).

0.1.3
C
-matrices,
G
-matrices, and
F
-polynomials

Following
[
FZ07
]
,
we introduce

C
-matrices,
G
-matrices, and
F
-polynomials.
They play a crucial role in the study of
cluster patterns.

Matrix notations

We start by introducing the following matrix notations (see, e.g.,
[
NZ12
]
).
For an
n\times n
matrix
A
, let
[A]_{+}
denote the matrix obtained from
A
by replacing the entry
a_{ij}

with
[a_{ij}]_{+}
.
Let
A^{\bullet k}

denote the matrix obtained from
A
by replacing the entry
a_{ij}
(
j\neq k
)
with
0
.
Similarly, let
A^{k\bullet}

denote the matrix obtained from
A
by replacing the entry
a_{ij}
(
i\neq k
)
with 0.

For any
n\times n
matrices,
the following facts are easily checked,
where for the last two equalities we use (
0.1.5
).

\displaystyle AB^{\bullet k}=(AB)^{\bullet k},\quad A^{k\bullet}B=(AB)^{k\bullet},

(0.1.16)

\displaystyle A^{\bullet k}B=A^{\bullet k}B^{k\bullet}=AB^{k\bullet},

(0.1.17)

\displaystyle A[B]_{+}+[-A]_{+}B=A[-B]_{+}+[A]_{+}B,

(0.1.18)

If
AB=CD
, then
A[B]_{+}-C[D]_{+}=A[-B]_{+}-C[-D]_{+}
.

(0.1.19)

Let
J_{k}
be the
n\times n
diagonal matrix obtained from the identity matrix by replacing
the
k
th diagonal entry with
-1
.
The following properties will be repeatedly used.

Lemma 0.1.10
.

Let
A
be any
n\times n
matrix whose diagonal entries are 0.
Let
P=J_{k}+A^{k\bullet}

or

J_{k}+A^{\bullet k}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle A^{\bullet k}J_{k}=-A^{\bullet k},\quad J_{k}A^{\bullet k}=A^{\bullet k},\quad A^{k\bullet}J_{k}=A^{k\bullet},\quad J_{k}A^{k\bullet}=-A^{k\bullet},

(0.1.20)

\displaystyle P^{2}=I,

(0.1.21)

\displaystyle|P|=-1.

(0.1.22)

Proof.

Let us prove (
0.1.21
) for
P=J_{k}+A^{k\bullet}
. By
a_{kk}=0
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}P^{2}&=J_{k}^{2}+J_{k}A^{k\bullet}+A^{k\bullet}J_{k}+A^{k\bullet}A^{k\bullet}\\
&=I-A^{k\bullet}+A^{k\bullet}=I.\end{split}

(0.1.23)

Also, we have
|P|=|J_{k}|=-1
.
∎

Example 0.1.11
.

The first case of the matrix mutation (
0.1.8
)
is written as

\displaystyle(B^{\prime})^{k\bullet}=-B^{k\bullet},\quad(B^{\prime})^{\bullet k}=-B^{\bullet k}.

(0.1.24)

Also, the entire mutation (
0.1.8
) is written as

[
BFZ05
, Eq. (3.2)]

\displaystyle B^{\prime}=(J_{k}+[-B]_{+}^{\bullet k})B(J_{k}+[B]_{+}^{k\bullet}).

(0.1.25)

By (
0.1.21
), we have the relation

\displaystyle(J_{k}+[-B]_{+}^{\bullet k})B^{\prime}=B(J_{k}+[B]_{+}^{k\bullet}).

(0.1.26)

C
-matrices

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be any cluster pattern,
and let
\mathbf{B}
be the
B
-pattern of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.
Let
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be a given initial vertex.

We introduce a collection of
matrices
\mathbf{C}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{C}=\{C_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}

called a
C
-pattern
,
which is uniquely determined from
\mathbf{B}
and
t_{0}
, therefore, eventually only from
t_{0}
and
B_{t_{0}}
.

Definition 0.1.12

(
C
-matrices)
.

The
C
-matrices
C_{t}=(c_{ij;t})_{i,j=1}^{n}
(
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
)
of a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
(or a
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}
) with an initial vertex
t_{0}

are
n\times n

integer matrices
that are uniquely determined
by the following initial condition
and the mutation rule:

\displaystyle C_{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=I,

(0.1.27)

\displaystyle C_{t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=C_{t}J_{k}+C_{t}[B_{t}]_{+}^{k\bullet}+[-C_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k}B_{t},

(0.1.28)

or more explicitly,

\displaystyle c_{ij;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-c_{ik;t}&j=k,\\
c_{ij;t}+c_{ik;t}[b_{kj;t}]_{+}+[-c_{ik;t}]_{+}b_{kj;t}&j\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.1.29)

where
t
and
t^{\prime}
are
k
-adjacent.
Each column vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}=(c_{ji;t})_{j=1}^{n}
of a matrix
C_{t}
is called
a
c
-vector
.

Remark 0.1.13
.

The
C
-matrices appeared implicitly as the lower halves of the extended
exchange matrices for the principal coefficients in
[
FZ07
, Def. 3.1]
.

The mutation (
0.1.28
), in particular, implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{k;t^{\prime}}=-\mathbf{c}_{k;t},

(0.1.30)

or equivalently,

\displaystyle C_{t^{\prime}}^{\bullet k}=-C_{t}^{\bullet k}.

(0.1.31)

One can easily check that the mutation (
0.1.29
) is involutive
using (
0.1.5
), (
0.1.8
), and (
0.1.30
).
Alternatively, in the matrix form,
by multiplying
J_{k}
from the right to (
0.1.28
), we have

\displaystyle C_{t^{\prime}}J_{k}

\displaystyle=C_{t}+C_{t}^{\bullet k}[B_{t}]_{+}^{k\bullet}+[-C_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k}B_{t}^{k\bullet}.

(0.1.32)

By (
0.1.24
) and (
0.1.31
),
this can be written as

\displaystyle C_{t}

\displaystyle=C_{t^{\prime}}J_{k}+C_{t^{\prime}}[-B_{t^{\prime}}]_{+}^{k\bullet}+[C_{t^{\prime}}]_{+}^{\bullet k}B_{t^{\prime}}.

(0.1.33)

Then,
by (
0.1.18
),
we have

\displaystyle C_{t}

\displaystyle=C_{t^{\prime}}J_{k}+C_{t^{\prime}}[B_{t^{\prime}}]_{+}^{k\bullet}+[-C_{t^{\prime}}]_{+}^{\bullet k}B_{t^{\prime}},

(0.1.34)

which is the desired result.
Moreover, by (
0.1.18
) again,
we have
the following
\varepsilon
-expression of (
0.1.28
):

Proposition 0.1.14

(
[
NZ12
, Eq. (2.4)]
)
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}
.
Then, the right hand side of the
following equality does not depend on the
choice of
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle C_{t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=C_{t}J_{k}+C_{t}[\varepsilon B_{t}]_{+}^{k\bullet}+[-\varepsilon C_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k}B_{t}.

(0.1.35)

G
-matrices

Under the same assumption of
C
-matrices,
we introduce another collection of
matrices
\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{G}=\{G_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}

called a
G
-pattern
,
which is uniquely determined from
\mathbf{B}
,
t_{0}
,
and
\mathbf{C}^{t_{0}}
defined above.

Definition 0.1.15

(
G
-matrices)
.

The
G
-matrices
G_{t}=(g_{ij;t})_{i,j=1}^{n}
(
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
)
of a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
(or a
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}
) with an initial vertex
t_{0}

are
n\times n

integer matrices
that are uniquely determined
by the following initial condition
and the mutation rule:

\displaystyle G_{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=I,

(0.1.36)

\displaystyle G_{t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=G_{t}J_{k}+G_{t}[-B_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k}-B_{t_{0}}[-C_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k},

(0.1.37)

or more explicitly,

\displaystyle g_{ij;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle-g_{ik;t}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}g_{i\ell;t}[-b_{\ell k;t}]_{+}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}b_{i\ell;t_{0}}[-c_{\ell k;t}]_{+}&j=k,\\
g_{ij;t}&j\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.1.38)

where
t
and
t^{\prime}
are
k
-adjacent.
Each column vector
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}=(g_{ji;t})_{j=1}^{n}
of a matrix
G_{t}
is called
a
g
-vector
.

Remark 0.1.16
.

The
g
-vectors were originally introduced as
the degree vectors of cluster variables with principal coefficients in
[
FZ07
]
.

The first nontrivial property so far is the following relation
between
C
- and
G
-matrices.

Proposition 0.1.17

(
[
FZ07
, Eq. (6.14)]
)
.

The following equality holds for any
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
.

(First duality)

\displaystyle G_{t}B_{t}=B_{t_{0}}C_{t}.

(0.1.39)

The proof in
[
FZ07
, Eq. (6.14)]

relied on the fact that
g
-vectors are the degree vectors of cluster variables
with principal coefficients.
We give an alternative proof based on the definitions
of
C
- and
G
-matrices here.
Let
d(t,t^{\prime})
denote the distance in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
between two vertices
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
.

Proof.

We prove it by
the induction on
t
along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
from
t_{0}
.
For
t=t_{0}
, the equality (
0.1.39
) holds
because
C_{t_{0}}=G_{t_{0}}=I
.
Suppose that the equality (
0.1.39
) holds for
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
such that

d(t_{0},t)=d
. Let
t^{\prime}
be the vertex that is
k
-adjacent to
t
such that
d(t_{0},t^{\prime})=d+1
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{t^{\prime}}B_{t^{\prime}}&=G_{t}(J_{k}+[-B_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k})B_{t^{\prime}}-B_{t_{0}}[-C_{t}]^{\bullet k}B_{t^{\prime}}^{k\bullet}\\
&=G_{t}B_{t}(J_{k}+[B_{t}]_{+}^{k\bullet})+B_{t_{0}}[-C_{t}]^{\bullet k}B_{t}^{k\bullet}\\
&=B_{t_{0}}C_{t}(J_{k}+[B_{t}]_{+}^{k\bullet})+B_{t_{0}}[-C_{t}]^{\bullet k}B_{t}\\
&=B_{t_{0}}C_{t^{\prime}},\end{split}

(0.1.40)

where in the second equality we used
(
0.1.24
) and (
0.1.26
).
∎

Remark 0.1.18
.

There has been no name on this equality so far in the literature; however, due to
its importance, we name it as above,
where the duality means a relation between
C
- and
G
-matrices.
(Later we will have the second and the third dualities,
which are more nontrivial.)

Thanks to
(
0.1.19
) and (
0.1.39
),
one can easily check that the mutation (
0.1.37
) is involutive
as before.
Moreover,
by (
0.1.19
) and (
0.1.39
) again,
we have
the following
\varepsilon
-expression of (
0.1.37
).

Proposition 0.1.19

(
[
FZ07
, Eqs. (6.12), (6.13)]
)
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}
.
Then, the right hand side of the
following equality does not depend on the
choice of
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle G_{t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=G_{t}J_{k}+G_{t}[-\varepsilon B_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k}-B_{t_{0}}[-\varepsilon C_{t}]^{\bullet k}.

(0.1.41)

F
-polynomials

Let

\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of formal variables.
Although it is not necessary at this moment,
it is natural to identify them with the initial
y
-variables
\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}

of
\mathbf{\Sigma}

as we will see soon.
Under the same assumption of
C
-matrices,
we introduce a collection of
rational functions

\mathbf{F}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{F}=\{F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})\}_{i=1,\dots,n;\,t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}

in
\mathbf{y}

called a
F
-pattern
,
which is uniquely determined from
\mathbf{B}
,
t_{0}
,
and
\mathbf{C}^{t_{0}}
as follows.

Definition 0.1.20

(
F
-polynomials)
.

The
F
-polynomials
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})
(
i=1,\dots,n
;
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
)
of a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
(or a
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}
) with an initial vertex
t_{0}

are rational functions in

\mathbf{y}

with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}

that are uniquely determined
by the following initial condition
and the mutation rule:

\displaystyle F_{i;t_{0}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=1,

(0.1.42)

\displaystyle F_{i;t^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\displaystyle M_{k;t}(\mathbf{y})}{\displaystyle F_{k;t}(\mathbf{y})}&i=k,\\
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.1.43)

where
t
and
t^{\prime}
are
k
-adjacent,
and
M_{k,t}(\mathbf{y})
is a rational function in
\mathbf{y}

defined as follows:

\displaystyle M_{k;t}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{[c_{jk;t}]_{+}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}(\mathbf{y})^{[b_{jk;t}]_{+}}+\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{[-c_{jk;t}]_{+}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}(\mathbf{y})^{[-b_{jk;t}]_{+}}.

(0.1.44)

In the situation in (
0.1.43
),
we have

\displaystyle M_{k;t}(\mathbf{y})=M_{k;t^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y}).

(0.1.45)

Therefore,
the mutation
(
0.1.43
) is involutive.
We do not discuss
the
\varepsilon
-expression for (
0.1.43
),
because we do not use it here.

The
F
-polynomials were originally introduced as the specialized
x
-variables
with principal coefficients under the specialization
x_{1}=\dots=x_{n}=1

in
[
FZ07
]
.
The Laurent phenomenon applied to them yields the following important fact,
which justifies the name
F
-polynomials
.

Theorem 0.1.21

(
[
FZ07
, Prop. 3.6]
)
.

For any
i=1,\dots,n
and
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
the rational function
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})
is a polynomial in
\mathbf{y}

with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 0.1.22
.

In case we need to clarify the choice of the initial vertex
t_{0}
,
we write
C
-matrices and others as,
C^{t_{0}}_{t}=(c^{t_{0}}_{ij;t})_{i,j=1}^{n}
,

\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}
,

G^{t_{0}}_{t}=(g^{t_{0}}_{ij;t})_{i,j=1}^{n}
,

\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}
,

F_{i;t}^{t_{0}}(\mathbf{y})
.
Conversely,
if there is no confusion, we suppress the superscript
t_{0}
as above for simplicity.

0.1.4
Separation formulas and tropicalization

Let us present one of the most fundamental
results on cluster patterns in
[
FZ07
]
.

Theorem 0.1.23

(Separation Formulas
[
FZ07
, Prop. 3.13, Cor. 6.3]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be any cluster pattern,
and let
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be a given initial vertex.
Let
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{G}
, and
\mathbf{F}
be
the
C
-,
G
-, and
F
-patterns
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
with the initial vertex
t_{0}
.
Let
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}
, and
\hat{\mathbf{y}}

be the initial
x
-,
y
-variables,
and
\hat{y}
-variables
for the initial seed
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
.
Then,
the following formulas hold.

\displaystyle x_{i;t}

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{g_{ji;t}}\Biggr)F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}),

(0.1.46)

\displaystyle y_{i;t}

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{c_{ji;t}}\Biggr)\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}(\mathbf{y})^{b_{ji;t}}.

(0.1.47)

Proof.

Here is an alternative proof to the one in
[
FZ07
]
.
Using the mutations of
C
- and
G
-matrices, and
F
-polynomials,
one can easily verify
(
0.1.46
) and (
0.1.47
)
by the induction on
t
along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
from
t_{0}

with the help of (
0.1.39
).
We leave the details as an exercise to the readers.
∎

Since
\hat{y}
-variables mutate exactly in the same manner as
y
-variables,
a parallel formula holds for them:

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{i;t}

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}\hat{y}_{j}^{c_{ji;t}}\Biggr)\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{b_{ji;t}}.

(0.1.48)

Alternatively, one can derive it from (
0.1.46
) with the help of
the duality
(
0.1.39
).

Thank to the separation formulas, the study of cluster patterns reduces to
the study of
C
- and
G
-matrices, and
F
-polynomials.
Moreover,
the formulas play a central role
in the interplay between the cluster algebraic structure
and various subjects.

The original meaning of the separation formula in
[
FZ07
, Cor. 6.3]
is that
a general version of the formula (
0.1.46
) for
x
-variables
with coefficients
naturally “separates” the additions of
x
-variables and
coefficients.
Unfortunately, we cannot observe it in (
0.1.46
), because our
x
-variables
are
without coefficients
.
Below we attach an additional meaning of “separation” to the formulas in view of the

tropicalization
.

First, we briefly recall the notion of tropicalization in the context of semifields
[
FZ07
]
.

Definition 0.1.24
.

A
semifield

\mathbb{P}
is
a multiplicative abelian group
equipped with
a binary operation

\oplus
on
\mathbb{P}

that is commutative, associative, and distributive,
i.e.,
(a\oplus b)c=ac\oplus bc
.
The operation
\oplus
is called the
addition
in
\mathbb{P}
.

Here we exclusively use the following two semifields.

Example 0.1.25
.

(a).
Universal semifield

\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{u})
.
Let
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of
commutative variables.
We say that a
rational function
f(\mathbf{u})\in\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{u})
in
u

has a
subtraction-free expression

if it is expressed as

f(\mathbf{u})=p(\mathbf{u})/q(\mathbf{u})
, where
p(\mathbf{u})
and
q(\mathbf{u})

are nonzero polynomials in
\mathbf{u}
whose coefficients
are nonnegative.
Let
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{u})

be the set of all rational functions in
\mathbf{u}

having subtraction-free expressions.
Then,
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{u})

is a semifield by the usual
multiplication and addition in
\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{u})
.

(b).
Tropical semifield

\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{u})
.
Let
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of
commutative variables.
Let
\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{u})
be the set of all
Laurent monomials of
\mathbf{u}
with coefficient 1,
which is a multiplicative abelian group by the usual
multiplication.
We define the addition
\oplus
by

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}^{a_{i}}\oplus\prod_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}^{b_{i}}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}^{\min(a_{i},b_{i})}.

(0.1.49)

Then,
\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{u})
becomes a semifield.
The addition
\oplus
is called the
tropical sum
.

Consider
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{u})
and

\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{u})
with common generating variables

\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
.
Then,
we have a unique semifield homomorphism

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}:\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{u})\rightarrow\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{u})

(0.1.50)

such that
\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}(u_{i})=u_{i}
for any
i=1,\dots,n
.
We call it the
tropicalization homomorphism
.

Roughly speaking, it extracts the “leading monomial” of
a rational function
f(\mathbf{u})
in
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{u})
.

Example 0.1.26
.

For
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
,

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}\left(\frac{3u_{1}u_{2}^{2}u_{3}^{2}+2u_{1}^{2}u_{2}u_{3}}{3u_{2}^{2}+u_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{2}+u_{1}u_{2}^{3}u_{3}}\right)=\frac{u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}}{u_{2}^{2}}=u_{1}u_{2}^{-1}u_{3}.

(0.1.51)

Now let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be any cluster pattern, and let
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}

be a given initial vertex.
Let
\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{y}
be the initial
y
-variables in the ambient field
\mathcal{F}_{Y}
.
Then, the semifield generated by
\mathbf{y}
in
\mathcal{F}_{Y}
(by the usual multiplication and addition)
is identified with
the universal semifield

\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
.
Moreover, any
y
-variable
y_{i;t}
belongs to
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})

because the mutation (
0.1.7
) is a subtraction-free operation.
Let us apply the tropicalization homomorphism
\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}^{t_{0}}:\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})\rightarrow\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{y})

to
y
-variables.

Definition 0.1.27

(Tropical
y
-variable)
.

We call the image
\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}^{t_{0}}(y_{i;t})
a

tropical
y
-variable
with respect to the initial vertex
t_{0}
.

The following fact
can be easily deduced from (
0.1.43
)
by the induction on
t
along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
from
t_{0}
.

Proposition 0.1.28

(
[
FZ07
, Eq. (5.5)]
)
.

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}^{t_{0}}(F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y}))=1.

(0.1.52)

Remark 0.1.29
.

The equality (
0.1.52
) does not mean the polynomial

F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})
has a nonzero constant term.
For example,
\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}(y_{1}+y_{2})=1
.

Tropical
y
-variables are naturally identified with
c
-vectors as follows:

Proposition 0.1.30

(
[
FZ07
, Eq. (3.14)]
)
.

We have

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}^{t_{0}}(y_{i;t})=y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}:=\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{c_{ji;t}}.

(0.1.53)

Proof.

This follows from the separation formula (
0.1.47
)
and (
0.1.52
).
∎

Remark 0.1.31
.

In fact, (
0.1.53
) is the definition of
c
-vectors
in
[
FZ07
, Eq. (5.8)]
,
where the tropical
y
-variables are identified with

principal coefficients
.

Now looking back the separation formula (
0.1.47
) for
y
-variables,
we see that it naturally “separates” the
tropical
and the
nontropical
parts of

y
-variables.

On the other hand, the tropicalization of
x
-variables
with respect to the initial
x
-variables
\mathbf{x}
does not yield
g
-vectors;
rather, it yields the so-called
d
-vectors
(
denominator vectors
in
[
FZ07
]
),

which are the leading exponents of the Laurent polynomial expression of
x
-variables.
However, if we regard the operation of setting all
F
-polynomials
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})
to 1
also as “a kind of tropicalization” for
x
-variables,
then
the separation formula (
0.1.46
) for
x
-variables
can be viewed again as separating the “tropical” and the “nontropical” parts of

x
-variables,
where
a
g
-vector
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}=(g_{ji;t})_{j=1}^{n}

is identified with a
tropical
x
-variable
defined by

\displaystyle x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}:=\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{g_{ji;t}}.

(0.1.54)

In the same token, we call

\displaystyle\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}:=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\hat{y}_{j}^{c_{ji;t}}

(0.1.55)

a
tropical
\hat{y}
-variable
with respect to the initial vertex
t_{0}
.

Thanks to the duality (
0.1.39
), we see that the notion is compatible with a tropical

x
-variable as

\displaystyle\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{j;t}})^{b_{ji;t}}.

(0.1.56)

Alternatively, based on the duality (
0.1.39
),
we introduce a
\hat{c}
-vector

\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}

by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}

\displaystyle=B_{t_{0}}\mathbf{c}_{i;t}=G_{t}\mathbf{b}_{i;t},

(0.1.57)

where
\mathbf{b}_{i;t}
is the
i
th column vector of
B_{t}
.
In other words, it is the
i
th column vector of a
\hat{C}
-matrix

defined by

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{t}:=B_{t_{0}}C_{t}=G_{t}B_{t}.

(0.1.58)

Then,
the tropical
\hat{y}
-variable in (
0.1.56
)
is expressed also as

\displaystyle\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}={x}^{\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}}.

(0.1.59)

These expressions and notions play a major role in the scattering diagram method later.

0.1.5
Sign-coherence of
C
-matrices and Laurent positivity

In the paper
[
FZ07
]
several conjectures on
the properties of cluster patterns were proposed.
Here we recall especially two (three, precisely speaking) conjectures among them,
which are the theme of this part.

Let
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{F}
be
the
C
- and
F
-patterns
of
any cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
with a given initial point
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
.

The first conjecture is the following:

Conjecture 0.1.32

(Unit constant property
[
FZ07
, Conj. 5.4]
)
.

Every
F
-polynomial
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})
has constant term 1.

This seemingly innocent conjecture turned out to be difficult to prove
simply by the induction on
t
along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
However, it was noticed by
[
FZ07
]
that Conjecture
0.1.32
is equivalent to another remarkable conjecture on
C
-matrices and
c
-vectors.

Definition 0.1.33

(Row/Column sign-coherence)
.

We say that a vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is
positive

(resp.
negative
)

if
it is nonzero vector and
all nonzero components are positive (resp. negative).
We say that a matrix
M
is
column sign-coherent

(resp.
row sign-coherent
) if each column vector (resp. row vector) of
M

is either positive or negative.

Conjecture 0.1.34

(Column sign-coherence of
C
-matrices
[
FZ07
, Prop. 5.6]
)
.

Every
C
-matrix
C_{t}
is column sign-coherent.
Equivalently, every
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}

is either positive or negative.

The equivalence between Conjectures
0.1.32
and
0.1.34

easily follows from the mutation of
F
-polynomials (
0.1.43
)

[
FZ07
, Prop. 5.6]
.

The sign-coherence conjecture was proved in the skew-symmetric case
by

[
DWZ10
,
Pla11
,
Nag13
]
with the representation/categorical methods,
and in general by
[
GHKK18
]
with the scattering diagram method.

Theorem 0.1.35

(
[
GHKK18
, Cor. 5.5]
)
.

Conjectures
0.1.32
and
0.1.34
hold
for any
\mathbf{\Sigma}
and
t_{0}
.

The second conjecture we consider is the following:

Conjecture 0.1.36

(Laurent positivity
[
FZ07
, §3]
,
[
FZ02
, §3]
)
.

Every
F
-polynomial
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{y}]
has no negative
coefficients.

Through the separation formula, this is equivalent to the earlier conjecture in
[
FZ02
]
on the positivity of the coefficients
in the Laurent polynomial expressions for
x
-variables.
This conjecture was proved for surface type
by
[
MSW11
]
, for acyclic type by
[
KQ14
]
,
in the skew-symmetric case by
[
LS15
,
Dav18
]
,
with various methods,
and in general by
[
GHKK18
]
with the scattering diagram method.

Theorem 0.1.37

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 4.10]
)
.

Conjecture
0.1.36
holds
for any
\mathbf{\Sigma}
and
t_{0}
.

From these conjectures (now theorems)
many other conjectures
in
[
FZ07
]
and new results follow (e.g.,
[
NZ12
,
CHL20
,
CL20
,
Nak21
]
).
Therefore, they are considered to be in the heart of cluster algebra theory.

As mentioned in Introduction,
the purpose of the part is
to digest and reconstruct the proofs of these conjectures in
[
GHKK18
]
.
Therefore, we temporally forget Theorems

0.1.35
and
0.1.37
,
and keep Conjectures
0.1.32
,

0.1.34
, and
0.1.36

as conjectures until we prove them here.

Remark 0.1.38
.

Theorem
0.1.35

was proved in

[
GHKK18
]
with some toric geometrical setting.
In contrast,
we prove Conjecture
0.1.34

without any toric geometrical setting.

0.2
More about
C
- and
G
-matrices

In this section we study more about
C
- and
G
-matrices,
namely, the tropical part of a cluster pattern.
Here we only consider a
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}
, and we do not refer to a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.
Therefore, we regard that
C
- and
G
-matrices are directly associated with
a
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}
.

0.2.1
Mutations and second duality

Let
\mathbf{B}
be any
B
-pattern, and let

\mathbf{C}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{G}

are the
C
- and
G
-patterns of
\mathbf{B}
with
a given initial vertex
t_{0}
.
In view of Proposition
0.1.30
, we introduce the following notion.

Definition 0.2.1

(Tropical sign)
.

Suppose that
a
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{c}_{i;t}
is either positive or negative.
Then, the common sign
\varepsilon_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\varepsilon_{i;t}\in\{1,-1\}

of all nonzero components of
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}

is called the
tropical sign
of
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}
.

Example 0.2.2
.

(a).
For the initial vertex
t_{0}
, we have
C_{t_{0}}=I
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i;t_{0}}=1,\quad(i=1,\dots,n).

(0.2.1)

(b). Let
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be vertices that are
k
-adjacent.
Then, by (
0.1.30
),
we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k;t^{\prime}}=-\varepsilon_{k;t}.

(0.2.2)

Proposition 0.2.3
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then, the following facts hold.

(a).
For any
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
, we have

(Unimodularity
[
NZ12
, Prop. 4.2]
)

\displaystyle|C_{t}|=|G_{t}|\in\{1,-1\},

(0.2.3)

(Second duality
[
NZ12
, Eq. (3.11)]
)

\displaystyle D^{-1}G_{t}^{T}DC_{t}=I,

(0.2.4)

or, equivalently,

\displaystyle D^{-1}C_{t}^{T}DG_{t}=I,

(0.2.5)

where

D
is a common skew-symmetrizer of
\mathbf{B}
.

(b).
For any
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
that are
k
-adjacent,
we have

(Mutations
[
NZ12
, Prop. 1.3]
)

\displaystyle C_{t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=C_{t}(J_{k}+[\varepsilon_{k;t}B_{t}]_{+}^{k\bullet}),

(0.2.6)

\displaystyle G_{t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=G_{t}(J_{k}+[-\varepsilon_{k;t}B_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k}).

(0.2.7)

Since we need some details of the proof later,
we present it below.

Proof.

We first prove (b).
By the column sign-coherence of
C_{t}
,
the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{k;t}
is defined
for any
k
.
Then,
we have

\displaystyle[-\varepsilon_{k;t}C_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k}=O.

(0.2.8)

We set
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{k;t}

in the
\varepsilon
-expressions of mutations (
0.1.35
) and (
0.1.41
).
The resulting relations
are
(
0.2.6
) and (
0.2.7
).

We prove (a) by the induction on
t

along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
starting from
t_{0}
.
Let us introduce the following statement:

(a)_{d}
.

The claim (a) holds for any
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
such that
d(t_{0},t)=d
.

First,
(a)_{0}
holds because
C_{t_{0}}=G_{t_{0}}=I
.
Next, suppose that
(a)_{d}
holds for some
d
.
Let
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be vertices that are
k
-adjacent such that

d(t_{0},t)=d
and
d(t_{0},t^{\prime})=d+1
.
Let us temporally set the matrices in (
0.2.6
) and (
0.2.7
) as

\displaystyle P=J_{k}+[\varepsilon_{k;t}B_{t}]_{+}^{k\bullet},\quad Q=J_{k}+[-\varepsilon_{k;t}B_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k}.

Then, by Lemma
0.1.10
, we have

\displaystyle P^{2}=I,\quad|P|=|Q|=-1.

(0.2.9)

Thus, by the induction assumption
|C_{t}|=|G_{t}|=\pm 1
, we have

|C_{t^{\prime}}|=|G_{t^{\prime}}|=\mp 1
.
Also, by
(
0.1.13
),
we have

\displaystyle DPD^{-1}=Q^{T}.

(0.2.10)

Using the induction assumption
D^{-1}(G_{t})^{T}DC_{t}=I
, we have

\displaystyle D^{-1}G_{t^{\prime}}^{T}DC_{t^{\prime}}=D^{-1}(Q^{T}G_{t}^{T})D(C_{t}P)=P(D^{-1}G_{t}^{T}DC_{t})P=P^{2}=I.

(0.2.11)

Therefore,
(a)_{d+1}
holds.
∎

Remark 0.2.4
.

For the later use,
we record the following facts in the above proof.

•

To prove (b), we only use the sign-coherence of
C_{t}
for
t
therein.

•

To prove
(a)_{d}\Longrightarrow(a)_{d+1}
,
we only use the sign-coherence of
C_{t}
such that
d(t_{0},t)=d
.

The following expressions of formulas
(
0.2.6
) and (
0.2.7
)
by
c
- and
g
-vectors are also useful.

Proposition 0.2.5
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{i;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle-\mathbf{c}_{k;t}&i=k,\\
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}+[\varepsilon_{k;t}b_{ki;t}]_{+}\mathbf{c}_{k;t}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.2.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{i;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle-\mathbf{g}_{k;t}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}[-\varepsilon_{k;t}b_{jk;t}]_{+}\mathbf{g}_{j;t}&i=k,\\
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}&i\neq k.\end{cases}

(0.2.13)

We note that
the matrices
C_{t}
and
B_{t_{0}}
in turn determine
B_{t}
.

Proposition 0.2.6

(
[
NZ12
, Eq. (2.9)]
)
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then, the following equality holds:

\displaystyle C_{t}^{T}(DB_{t_{0}})C_{t}=DB_{t}.

(0.2.14)

Proof.

By the dualities (
0.1.39
) and (
0.2.5
), we have

\displaystyle C_{t}^{T}DB_{t_{0}}C_{t}=C_{t}^{T}DG_{t}B_{t}=D(D^{-1}C_{t}^{T}DG_{t})B_{t}=DB_{t}.

(0.2.15)

∎

0.2.2
Dual mutations and third duality

Let
\mathbf{B}=\{B_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
be any
B
-pattern,
and let
t_{0}
be a given initial vertex.
Since we now vary the initial vertex below,
we write the corresponding
C
- and
G
-matrices
as
C_{t}^{t_{0}}
and
G_{t}^{t_{0}}

as in Remark

0.1.22
.

We note that the mutation (
0.1.12
)
is compatible with the matrix transpose.
Namely,
if
B^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(B)
, then
B^{\prime}{}^{T}=\mu_{k}(B^{T})
.
Therefore,
{\mathbf{B}}^{T}:=\{B_{t}^{T}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}

is also a
B
-pattern,
which is called the
transpose of
\mathbf{B}
.
We temporarily write the associated
C
- and
G
-matrices
as
\tilde{C}_{t}^{t_{0}}
,
\tilde{G}_{t}^{t_{0}}
.

We borrow the following notation from
[
FG19
]
:
For any matrix
M
,
\varepsilon_{k\bullet}(M)
(resp.
\varepsilon_{\bullet k}(M)
)
stands for
the common sign of the
k
th row (resp.
k
th column) assuming that it is
either positive or negative.
For example, the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{i;t}
in
Definition
0.2.1

is written as
\varepsilon_{\bullet i}(C^{t_{0}}_{t})
.

Proposition 0.2.7
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then, the following facts hold:

(a). For any
t_{0},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
, we have

(Third duality
[
NZ12
, Eq. (1.13)]
)

\displaystyle C_{t}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{G}_{t_{0}}^{t})^{T},

(0.2.16)

\displaystyle G_{t}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{C}_{t_{0}}^{t})^{T}.

(0.2.17)

In particular, each
G
-matrix
G_{t}^{t_{0}}
is
row sign-coherent, and the equality

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k\bullet}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})=\varepsilon_{\bullet k}(\tilde{C}_{t_{0}}^{t})

(0.2.18)

holds.

(b).
For any
t_{0},t_{1},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
such that
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
are
k
-adjacent,
we have

(Dual mutations
[
NZ12
, Prop. 1.4, Eq. (4.2)]
,
[
FG19
, Prop. 3.6]
)

\displaystyle C^{t_{1}}_{t}

\displaystyle=(J_{k}+[-{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{k\bullet})C^{t_{0}}_{t},

(0.2.19)

\displaystyle G^{t_{1}}_{t}

\displaystyle=(J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})G^{t_{0}}_{t}.

(0.2.20)

Here,
{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})

is well-defined thanks to (a).

Again, we need some details of the proof later.
Furthermore, the statement and the proof in
[
NZ12
]
are a little indirect
in the present context.
Thus, we give a more transparent proof,
still in the spirit of
[
NZ12
]
.

Proof.

We prove the claims by the induction on the distance

d(t_{0},t)
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
, where we vary
t_{0}
for (a) and
t
for (b).

Let us introduce the following claims:

(a)_{d}
.

The claim (a) holds for any
t_{0},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
such that
d(t_{0},t)=d
.

(b)_{d}
.

The claim (b) holds for any
t_{0},t_{1},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
such that
d(t_{0},t)=d

and
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
are
k
-adjacent.

We prove the claims in the following order,

\displaystyle(a)_{0}\Longrightarrow(b)_{0}\Longrightarrow(a)_{1}\Longrightarrow(b)_{1}\Longrightarrow(a)_{2}\Longrightarrow\cdots,

(0.2.21)

assuming all preceding claims.

First, we show that
(a)_{0}
and
(b)_{0}
hold.
The claim
(a)_{0}
, where
t_{0}=t
, trivially holds, because all relevant matrices are the identity matrix
I
.
Let us prove
(b)_{0}
, where
t_{0}=t
.
By assumption,
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
are
k
-adjacent.
Then, by Proposition
0.2.3
(b) and (
0.1.24
),
we have

\displaystyle C^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=C^{t_{1}}_{t_{1}}(J_{k}+[B_{t_{1}}]^{k\bullet}_{+})=(J_{k}+[-B_{t_{0}}]^{k\bullet}_{+})C^{t_{0}}_{t_{0}},

(0.2.22)

\displaystyle G^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=G^{t_{1}}_{t_{1}}(J_{k}+[-B_{t_{1}}]^{\bullet k}_{+})=(J_{k}+[B_{t_{0}}]^{\bullet k}_{+})G^{t_{0}}_{t_{0}}.

(0.2.23)

Thus,
(b)_{0}
holds.

Next, assuming the claims in (
0.2.21
) up to
(b)_{d}
, we show
(a)_{d+1}
.
Assume
d(t_{0},t)=d
and
d(t_{1},t)=d+1
in (
0.2.19
). Take the transpose of (
0.2.19
).
Then, we apply (
0.2.16
),
which is valid thanks to
(a)_{d}
,
to its right hand side.
We have

\displaystyle(C^{t_{1}}_{t})^{T}=\tilde{G}^{t}_{t_{0}}(J_{k}+[-\varepsilon_{\bullet k}(\tilde{C}^{t}_{t_{0}})B_{t_{0}}^{T}]_{+}^{\bullet k}).

(0.2.24)

The right hand side is
\tilde{G}^{t}_{t_{1}}
by (
0.2.7
). Thus, we have
(
0.2.19
). The other case is similar.

Finally, assuming the claims in (
0.2.21
) up to
(a)_{d+1}
, we show
(b)_{d+1}
.
This is the nontrivial part.
It is enough to concentrate on the following situation.
Let
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
,
t
,
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be vertices such that

d(t_{0},t)=d
,
d(t_{0},t^{\prime})=d+1
,
where

t_{1}
and
t_{0}
are
k
-adjacent,
while

t
and
t^{\prime}
are
\ell
-adjacent.
They are depicted as follows, where

t_{1}
could be between
t_{0}
and
t
.

By (
0.2.6
) and (
0.2.19
) with
(b)_{d}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}C^{t_{1}}_{t^{\prime}}&=C^{t_{1}}_{t}(J_{\ell}+[\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{1}}_{t})B_{t}]_{+}^{\ell\bullet})\\
&=(J_{k}+[-{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{k\bullet})C^{t_{0}}_{t}(J_{\ell}+[\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{1}}_{t})B_{t}]_{+}^{\ell\bullet}).\end{split}

(0.2.25)

By
(a)_{d+1}
,
the sign
{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t^{\prime}})
is well-defined.
Let us temporally assume the identity:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ (J_{k}+[-{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{k\bullet})C^{t_{0}}_{t}(J_{\ell}+[\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{1}}_{t})B_{t}]_{+}^{\ell\bullet})\\
=&\ (J_{k}+[-{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t^{\prime}})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{k\bullet})C^{t_{0}}_{t}(J_{\ell}+[\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t}]_{+}^{\ell\bullet}).\end{split}

(0.2.26)

Then, the right hand side of (
0.2.25
)
reduces to

(J_{k}+[-{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t^{\prime}})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{k\bullet})C^{t_{0}}_{t^{\prime}}
,
which is the desired result for (
0.2.19
) of
(b)_{d+1}
.
Similarly, from the identity

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ (J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})G^{t_{0}}_{t}(J_{\ell}+[-\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{1}}_{t})B_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet\ell})\\
=&\ (J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t^{\prime}})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})G^{t_{0}}_{t}(J_{\ell}+[-\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet\ell}),\end{split}

(0.2.27)

we obtain (
0.2.20
) of
(b)_{d+1}
.
It remains to prove (
0.2.26
) and (
0.2.27
).
To do it, we need to know a precise relation between signs

{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})
and
{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t^{\prime}})
,
and also
\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{1}}_{t})
and
\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{0}}_{t})
.

The following facts hold:

(i).

By (
0.2.7
),
G^{t_{0}}_{t}
and
G^{t_{0}}_{t^{\prime}}
differ only in the
\ell
th column.

(ii).

By
(b)_{d}
,
C^{t_{0}}_{t}
and
C^{t_{1}}_{t}
differ only in the
k
th row.

(iii).

By the duality (
0.2.4
),
the
k
th row of
G^{t_{0}}_{t}
has only a unique component
\varepsilon
at
\ell
th column
if and only
the
\ell
th column of
C^{t_{0}}_{t}
has a unique nonzero component
\varepsilon^{\prime}
at
k
th row.
Furthermore, if it happens,
\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\{1,-1\}
,
thanks to the unimodularity (
0.2.3
).

Now we consider two cases.

Case 1.
Suppose that the
k
th row of
G^{t_{0}}_{t}
has a nonzero component at some column
other than the
\ell
th column. Then, from Facts (i)–(iii) above and the row and column sign-coherence
of
G
- and
C
-matrices in
(a)_{d+1}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t^{\prime}})={\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t}),\quad\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{1}}_{t})=\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{0}}_{t}).

(0.2.28)

Therefore, (
0.2.26
) and (
0.2.27
) hold trivially.

Case 2.

Suppose that the
k
th row of
G^{t_{0}}_{t}
has only one nonzero component that is at the
\ell
th column.
Let us write this component as
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}
.
As mentioned in Fact (iii),

\varepsilon
coincides with
the only one nonzero component
in the
\ell
th column of
C^{t_{0}}_{t}
that is at the
k
th row.
Then, by (
0.2.7
) and
(b)_{d}
, we have

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t^{\prime}})=-\varepsilon,\quad{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})=\varepsilon,\quad\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{1}}_{t})=-\varepsilon,\quad\varepsilon_{\bullet\ell}(C^{t_{0}}_{t})=\varepsilon.

(0.2.29)

Let us prove (
0.2.26
), which is now written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ (J_{k}+[-{\varepsilon}B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{k\bullet})C^{t_{0}}_{t}(J_{\ell}+[-{\varepsilon}B_{t}]_{+}^{\ell\bullet})\\
=&\ (J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{k\bullet})C^{t_{0}}_{t}(J_{\ell}+[\varepsilon B_{t}]_{+}^{\ell\bullet}).\end{split}

(0.2.30)

By (
0.1.21
),
it is rewritten as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ (J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{k\bullet})(J_{k}+[-{\varepsilon}B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{k\bullet})C^{t_{0}}_{t}\\
=&\ C^{t_{0}}_{t}(J_{\ell}+[\varepsilon B_{t}]_{+}^{\ell\bullet})(J_{\ell}+[-{\varepsilon}B_{t}]_{+}^{\ell\bullet}).\end{split}

(0.2.31)

After some manipulation,
it reduces to

\displaystyle(B_{t_{0}}C_{t}^{t_{0}})^{k\bullet}=C_{t}^{t_{0}}(B_{t})^{\ell\bullet}.

(0.2.32)

By the assumption, we have

(C_{t}^{t_{0}})^{\bullet\ell}=(G_{t}^{t_{0}})^{k\bullet}=\varepsilon E_{k\ell}
, where
E_{k\ell}
is the matrix whose entries
are zero except for the
(k,\ell)
-entry that is 1.
Then,
the right hand side of (
0.2.32
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle(C_{t}^{t_{0}})^{\bullet\ell}B_{t}=\varepsilon E_{k\ell}B_{t}=(G_{t}^{t_{0}})^{k\bullet}B_{t}=(G_{t}^{t_{0}}B_{t})^{k\bullet}.

(0.2.33)

Thus, (
0.2.32
) reduces to
the duality (
0.1.39
).
Therefore, (
0.2.26
) is proved.
Similarly, skipping details a little, (
0.2.27
) reduces to

\displaystyle(B_{t_{0}})^{\bullet k}G_{t}^{t_{0}}=(G_{t}^{t_{0}}B_{t})^{\bullet\ell}.

(0.2.34)

Then, by repeating a similar argument,
again (
0.2.34
) reduces to the duality (
0.1.39
).
Therefore, (
0.2.27
) is proved.
∎

Remark 0.2.8
.

For the later use we record the following facts in the above proof.

•

To prove
(a)_{d}
,
(b)_{d}

\Longrightarrow(a)_{d+1}
, we only use the sign-coherence of
\tilde{C}_{t_{0}}^{t}
such that
d(t_{0},t)=d
.

•

To prove
(b)_{d}
,
(a)_{d+1}

\Longrightarrow(b)_{d+1}
,
we only use the sign-coherence of
C_{t}^{t_{0}}
,

C_{t}^{t_{1}}
,
\tilde{C}_{t_{0}}^{t}
,
\tilde{C}_{t_{0}}^{t^{\prime}}
such that
d(t_{0},t)=d
,

d(t_{0},t^{\prime})=d+1
,
d(t_{1},t)=d\pm 1
.

Remark 0.2.9
.

Combining three dualities (
0.1.39
), (
0.2.4
), (
0.2.16
),
we have various relations among
C
- and
G
-matrices therein.
For example, by (
0.1.39
) and (
0.2.16
), we have

\displaystyle B_{t_{0}}C_{t}^{t_{0}}=(B_{t}^{T}\tilde{C}_{t_{0}}^{t})^{T},\quad G_{t}^{t_{0}}B_{t_{0}}=(\tilde{G}_{t_{0}}^{t}B_{t}^{T})^{T}.

(0.2.35)

The
\hat{C}
-matrices in (
0.1.58
) mutate as a “hybrid” of

C
- and
G
-matrices in the following sense.

Proposition 0.2.10
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Let
\hat{C}_{t}^{t_{0}}=B_{t_{0}}{C}_{t}^{t_{0}}
and
\hat{C}_{t}^{t_{1}}=B_{t_{1}}{C}_{t}^{t_{1}}
.
Then,
for any
t_{0},t_{1},t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
such that
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
,
and also,
t
and
t^{\prime}
are
k
-adjacent, respectively,
we have

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=\hat{C}_{t}^{t_{0}}(J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}_{\bullet k}(C^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t}]_{+}^{k\bullet}),

(0.2.36)

\displaystyle\hat{C}^{t_{1}}_{t}

\displaystyle=(J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})\hat{C}^{t_{0}}_{t}.

(0.2.37)

Proof.

The first formula is obtained from (
0.1.58
)
by multiplying
B_{t_{0}}
from the left.
The second one is obtained from (
0.2.20
)
by multiplying
B_{t}
from the right.
∎

0.2.3
Principal extension of
B
-patterns

For any
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}
of rank
n
and a given initial vertex
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
we introduce a
2n\times 2n
skew-symmetrizable matrix

\displaystyle\overline{B}_{t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{t_{0}}&-I\\
I&O\\
\end{pmatrix},

(0.2.38)

which is called the
principal extension of
B_{t_{0}}
.
It is important that
\overline{B}_{t_{0}}
is nonsingular.
Choose any point
\overline{t}_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{2n}
.
We naturally identify
\mathbb{T}_{n}
as a subtree of
\mathbb{T}_{2n}

by identifying
t_{0}
with
\overline{t}_{0}
, and also
the edges with
labels
k=1,\dots,n
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
and
\mathbb{T}_{2n}
.
Consider a
B
-pattern

\overline{\mathbf{B}}=\{\overline{B}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{2n}}
of rank
2n
generated
by
\overline{B}_{t_{0}}
,
which is called
the principal extension of
\mathbf{B}
with
the initial vertex
t_{0}
.

The following stability properties have repeatedly appeared
in the literatures in various forms.
Here, we quote from
[
FG19
]
.

Proposition 0.2.11

(e.g.,
[
FG19
, Theorem 4.2, Remark 4.4]
)
.

Let
C_{t}
,
G_{t}
,
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})
be
C
- and
G
-matrices, and
F
-polynomials
of
\mathbf{B}
with the initial point
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
Let
\overline{C}_{t}
,
\overline{G}_{t}
,
\overline{F}_{i;t}(\overline{\mathbf{y}})
be
C
- and
G
-matrices, and
F
-polynomials
of
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
with the same initial point
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}\subset\mathbb{T}_{2n}
,
where
\overline{\mathbf{y}}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{2n})
.
Then, the following relations hold for any
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}\subset\mathbb{T}_{2n}
:

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{t}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}C_{t}&Z_{t}\\
O&I\end{pmatrix},

(0.2.39)

\displaystyle\overline{G}_{t}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}G_{t}&O\\
O&I\end{pmatrix},

(0.2.40)

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{i;t}(\overline{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})&i=1,\dots,n,\\
1&i=n+1,\dots,2n,\end{cases}

(0.2.41)

where
Z_{t}
is a certain matrix. Moreover,

Z_{t}=O
if the sign-coherence of
C
-matrices for
\mathbf{B}
holds.

These equalities can be proved by the induction on
t
along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
from
t_{0}

[
FG19
, Theorem 4.2]
.

By Proposition
0.2.11
, any information
on
C_{t}
,
G_{t}
,
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})

can be extracted from the one on

\overline{C}_{t}
,
\overline{G}_{t}
,
\overline{F}_{i;t}(\overline{\mathbf{y}})
.

0.2.4
G
-cones and
G
-fans

From now on,
we study the geometric aspect of
G
-matrices.

We recall some notions from convex geometry.

Definition 0.2.12

(Cone)
.

Let
M\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be a lattice of rank
n
.
Let
M_{\mathbb{R}}=M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the extension to an
n
-dimensional
vector space over
\mathbb{R}
.
Let
N=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M,\mathbb{Z})

and
N_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(M_{\mathbb{R}},\mathbb{R})

be their duals.
Let
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
be the canonical paring

N_{\mathbb{R}}\times M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

•

For given elements
a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\in M\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
,
the subset of
M_{\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\displaystyle\sigma(a_{1},\dots,a_{r}):=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}a_{1}+\cdots\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}a_{r}

(0.2.42)

is called a
convex rational polyhedral cone
, or simply, a
cone

generated by
a_{1},\dots,a_{r}
.

The set
\sigma(\emptyset):=\{0\}
is also a cone.

•

A cone
\sigma
is said to be
strongly convex
if

\sigma\cap(-\sigma)=\{0\}
.

•

A cone is
simplicial
if it is generated
by
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent elements in
M
.
Thus, a simplicial cone is strongly convex.

•

A cone is
nonsingular
if it is generated
by a subset of a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis in
M
.
Thus, a nonsingular cone is simplicial.

•

The
dimension of a cone
\sigma
is the dimension of the subspace
of
M_{\mathbb{R}}
spanned by
\sigma
.

•

The
dual cone

\sigma^{\vee}
of a cone
\sigma
is defined by

\sigma^{\vee}=\{u\in N_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle u,a\rangle\geq 0\ \text{for any $a\in\sigma$})\},

which is a cone in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

A subset
\tau
of a cone
\sigma
is called a
face
of
\sigma

if there is some
u\in\sigma^{\vee}
such that
\tau=\sigma\cap u^{\perp}
,
where
u^{\perp}=\{a\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle u,a\rangle=0\}
.
In particular,
for a simplicial cone
\sigma

with a
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent
generating set
S
,
any face of
\sigma

is
a cone generated by a subset of
S
.

Example 0.2.13
.

For
M=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
M_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{1})
and

\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2})
are strongly convex cones,
while
\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{1},-\mathbf{e}_{1})=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{e}_{1}
and

\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{1},-\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2},-\mathbf{e}_{2})=\mathbb{R}^{2}
are cones
that are not strongly convex.
The cone
\sigma=\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2})

has four faces
\sigma
,
\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{1})
,
\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{2})
,
\{0\}
,
while
the cone
\sigma=\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{1},-\mathbf{e}_{1})

has the unique face, which is
\sigma
.

Definition 0.2.14

(Fan)
.

•

A
fan

\Delta
in
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a nonempty (possibly infinite) set of strongly convex cones in
M_{\mathbb{R}}

satisfying the following conditions:

(i).

If
\tau
is a face of
\sigma\in\Delta
, then
\tau\in\Delta
.

(ii).

If
\sigma,\tau\in\Delta
, then
\sigma\cap\tau\in\Delta
.

•

A subset
|\Delta|=\bigcup_{\sigma\in\Delta}\sigma
of
M_{\mathbb{R}}
is called the
support

of a fan
\Delta
.

•

A fan
\Delta
is
simplicial
if all cones of
\Delta
are simplicial.

•

A fan
\Delta
is
nonsingular
if all cones of
\Delta
are nonsingular.
Thus, a nonsingular fan is simplicial.

•

A fan
\Delta
is
complete
if
|\Delta|=M_{\mathbb{R}}
.

We apply these geometric notions to
G
-matrices following
[
RS16
,
Rea14
]
.
In the rest of this section

we temporarily assume the sign-coherence of
C
-matrices

and study some basic properties of
G
-cones.

Let
\mathbf{B}
be any
B
-pattern,
and let

\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{G}
be the
G
-pattern of
\mathbf{B}
with
a given initial point
t_{0}
.

Definition 0.2.15

(
G
-cone)
.

For each
G
-matrix
G_{t}
,
the cone spanned by its
g
-vectors
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle\sigma(G_{t}):=\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{1;t},\dots,\mathbf{g}_{n;t})

(0.2.43)

is called a
G
-cone
.
The interior of
\sigma(G_{t})
is denoted by

\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t})
.
Each face of
\sigma(G_{t})
spanned by

\mathbf{g}_{j;t}
’s excluding the
i
th
g
-vector

\mathbf{g}_{i;t}

is denoted by
\sigma_{i}(G_{t})
for
i=1,\dots,n
.

By the unimodularity
(
0.2.3
),
every
G
-cone
\sigma(G_{t})

is a nonsingular cone,
and its faces
\sigma_{i}(G_{t})
(
i=1,\dots,n
)
are cones of codimension one.

From now on, let us especially take a common skew-symmetrizer

D
of the
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}

in the following form:

\displaystyle D=\mathrm{diag}({\delta}_{1}^{-1}\dots,{\delta}_{n}^{-1}),

(0.2.44)

where
\delta_{1}
, …,
\delta_{n}
are positive integers.
(This seemingly awkward choice is suitable to the formulation
of scattering diagrams in
[
GHKK18
]
we use later. See Example
0.5.4
.)
Accordingly, we introduce the following nonstandard inner product

(\ ,\ )_{D}

in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})_{D}=\mathbf{u}^{T}D\mathbf{v}\quad(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(0.2.45)

Below, for a given
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}
,
we always assume this inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{D}

in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
For
\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mathbf{n}^{\perp}

is a hyperplane defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}^{\perp}:=\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{v})_{D}=0\}.

(0.2.46)

We say that a normal vector
\mathbf{n}
of a face

\sigma_{i}(G_{t})
of a cone
\sigma(G_{t})
is
inward for
\sigma(G_{t})

if
(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{g}_{i;t})_{D}>0
.

In this geometrical setting,
the duality (
0.2.4
)/(
0.2.5
) can be rephrased in the following manner.

Proposition 0.2.16
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then, the following dualities of vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
hold:

\displaystyle(\delta_{i}\mathbf{c}_{i;t},\mathbf{g}_{j;t})_{D}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij},

(0.2.47)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{c}_{i;t},\delta_{j}\mathbf{g}_{j;t})_{D}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}.

(0.2.48)

In particular, each
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}
is a normal vector of
the face
\sigma_{i}(G_{t})
of the cone
\sigma(G_{t})

with respect to the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{D}
.
Moreover, it is inward for
\sigma(G_{t})
.

Let
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})=\Delta(\mathbf{G})
be the set of all faces of all
G
-cones

\sigma(G_{t})
(
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
) in the
G
-pattern
\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
.
Let
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be vertices that are
k
-adjacent.
Then, by (
0.2.13
),
two cones
\sigma(G_{t})
and
\sigma(G_{t^{\prime}})
intersect
each other
in their common face
\sigma_{k}(G_{t})=\sigma_{k}(G{{}_{t^{\prime}}})
with codimension one.
However, for a cone
\sigma(G_{t^{\prime\prime}})
obtained from
\sigma(G_{t})
after several mutations,
it is not clear at all that
\sigma(G_{t^{\prime\prime}})
intersects
\sigma(G_{t})
only in their common face so that the set
\Delta(\mathbf{G})
forms a fan.
It was conjectured by
[
RS16
, Conj. 3.14]
that this always happens.
Then, it was shown to be true by
[
Rea14
]

under the sign-coherence conjecture.

Theorem 0.2.17

(
[
Rea14
, Theorem 8.7]
)
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then,
for any
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}
and a given initial vertex
t_{0}
,
the set of cones
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
is a fan in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

In
[
Rea14
]
this was proved in a more general setting of
mutation fans
therein.
We provide a self-contained proof of Theorem
0.2.17

in
Section
0.2.7
without referring to mutation fans,
because the tools and techniques we use therein are also relevant to
our main purpose.

The resulting fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
,
which is nonsingular by (
0.2.3
), is
called the
g
-vector fan

in
[
RS16
]
.
To simplify a little,
we call it the
G
-fan
of
\mathbf{B}
(or
\mathbf{\Sigma}
) with the initial vertex
t_{0}
.

Let
\#\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
be the number of
distinct

G
-matrices in
\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
,
which can be finite or infinite.

Proposition 0.2.18
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then, the
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
is complete
if and only if
\#\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
is finite.

Proof.

This is true by the following topological reason.
For each subset
T
of
\mathbb{T}_{n}
, we attach a
subset
\Delta_{T}
of
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
consisting of the faces
of all
\sigma(G_{t})
with
t\in T
.
Let
|\Delta_{T}|^{c}
be the complement of the support
|\Delta_{T}|

in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let
S^{n-1}
be the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let
D_{T}=|\Delta_{T}|^{c}\cap S^{n-1}
.
For
T=\{t_{0}\}
,
D_{T}
is topologically a
(n-1)
-dimensional open disk.
We consider a strongly increasing sequence of subsets of
\mathbb{T}_{n}
,

T_{1}=\{t_{0}\}\subsetneq T_{2}\subsetneq T_{3}\subsetneq\cdots

with
|T_{s}|=s
.
Correspondingly, we have a weakly decreasing sequence of subsets of
S^{n-1}
,

D_{T_{1}}\supset D_{T_{2}}\supset D_{T_{3}}\supset\cdots
.
Then,
D_{T_{s}}
is a disjoint union of a finite number of open disks,
such that
at each process of increasing
T_{s}
to
T_{s+1}
, one of the followings occurs
(by adding a
G
-cone):

•

D_{T_{s+1}}=D_{T_{s}}
.

•

An open disk shrinks.

•

An open disk splits into two open disks.

•

An open disk disappears.

If
\#\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
is finite, the decreasing process of
D_{T_{s}}
should terminate at some
s
.
This occurs only when
D_{T_{s}}=\emptyset
. Thus,
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
is complete.
If
\#\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
is infinite, the process never terminates.
Then, when
T_{s}
converges to
\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
D_{T_{s}}
converges to the (possibly infinite)
union of points and (not open, but not necessarily closed) disks.
Therefore,
|\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})|^{c}
is nonempty.
∎

Remark 0.2.19
.

We say that a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is
of finite type

if there are only finitely many distinct seeds in
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.
It is known, for example, by

[
Nak21
, Theorem 5.2]
,
that
a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is of finite type
if and only if
\#\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
is finite for
\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
of

\mathbf{\Sigma}
.
Then, combining it with Proposition
0.2.18
,
we have that
a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is of finite type
if and only if the
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
is complete
for
\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
of

\mathbf{\Sigma}
.
Also, we note that the proof of
[
Nak21
, Theorem 5.2]

is based on the sign-coherence of
C
-matrices and the Laurent positivity.

0.2.5
Rank 2 examples of
G
-fans

In the rank 2 case, one can confirm Theorem

0.2.17
and Proposition
0.2.18

by explicitly calculating
G
-matrices.

Let us arrange a
B
-pattern of rank 2 in the following way:

\displaystyle\cdots\ \mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{\mu_{2}}\ B_{t_{-2}}\ \mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{\mu_{1}}\ B_{t_{-1}}\ \mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{\mu_{2}}\ B_{t_{0}}\ \mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{\mu_{1}}\ B_{t_{1}}\ \mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{\mu_{2}}\ B_{t_{2}}\ \mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{\mu_{1}}\ \cdots.

(0.2.49)

We take
B_{t_{0}}
as the initial exchange matrix.
Let us present the associated
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G})

in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
explicitly.
Below the type of a
B
-pattern refers to the type of
the Cartan matrix
A(B)=(a_{ij})
associated with
B=B_{t_{0}}
in the convention of
[
Kac90
]
, where

\displaystyle a_{ij}=\begin{cases}2&i=j,\\
-|b_{ij}|&i\neq j.\end{cases}

(0.2.50)

(I). Finite type.
The
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G})
is complete
as stated in Proposition
0.2.18
.

(a). Type
A_{2}
: Let

\displaystyle B_{t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(0.2.51)

Along the mutation sequence
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
,
\cdots
,
the
G
-matrices are explicitly calculated as

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{t_{0}}&=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad G_{t_{1}}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad G_{t_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\\
G_{t_{3}}&=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1&-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad G_{t_{4}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad G_{t_{5}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

(0.2.52)

Both
G_{t_{5}}
and
G_{t_{0}}
define the same cone,
corresponding to
the celebrated
pentagon periodicity
.
The
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G})
is depicted in Figure
1
(a).

(b). Type
B_{2}
: Let

\displaystyle B_{t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
2&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(0.2.53)

We start from the initial
G
-matrix
G_{t_{0}}=I
.
Along the mutation sequence
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
,
\cdots
,
the following
g
-vectors appear in this order, showing periodicity:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-2\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(0.2.54)

The
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G})
is depicted in Figure
1
(b).

(c). Type
G_{2}
: Let

\displaystyle B_{t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
3&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(0.2.55)

We start from the initial
G
-matrix
G_{t_{0}}=I
.
Along the mutation sequence
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
,
\cdots
,
the following
g
-vectors appear in this order, showing periodicity:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-3\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\

\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}2\\
-3\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\

\end{pmatrix}.

(0.2.56)

The
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G})
is depicted in Figure

(c).

(a)
A_{2}

Figure 1:

-fans of rank 2.

(II). Infinite type.
The
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G})
is not complete.

(d). Type
A_{1}^{(1)}
: Let

\displaystyle B_{t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\\
2&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(0.2.57)

We start from the initial
G
-matrix
G_{t_{0}}=I
.
Along the mutation sequence
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
,
\cdots
,
the following
g
-vectors appear in this order:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-2\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}2\\
-3\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}3\\
-4\\
\end{pmatrix},\cdots.

(0.2.58)

On the other hand,
along the mutation sequence
\mu_{2}
,
\mu_{1}
,
\cdots
,
the following
g
-vectors appear in this order:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}2\\
-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}3\\
-2\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}4\\
-3\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}5\\
-4\\
\end{pmatrix},\cdots.

(0.2.59)

The
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G})
is depicted in Figure
1
(d).
It covers the region excluding the half line

\mathbb{R}_{+}(1,-1)
.

(e). Type
A_{2}^{(2)}
: Let

\displaystyle B_{t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
4&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(0.2.60)

We start from the initial
G
-matrix
G_{t_{0}}=I
.
Along the mutation sequence
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
,
\cdots
,
the following
g
-vectors appear in this order:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-4\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-3\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}3\\
-8\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}2\\
-5\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}5\\
-12\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}3\\
-7\\
\end{pmatrix},\cdots.

(0.2.61)

On the other hand,
along the mutation sequence
\mu_{2}
,
\mu_{1}
,
\cdots
,
the following
g
-vectors appear in this order:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}3\\
-4\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}2\\
-3\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}5\\
-8\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}3\\
-5\\
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}7\\
-12\\
\end{pmatrix},\cdots.

(0.2.62)

The
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G})
is depicted in Figure
1
(e).
It covers the region excluding the half line

\mathbb{R}_{+}(1,-2)
.

(f). Non-affine type: Let

\displaystyle B_{t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-c\\
b&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad(b,c>0,\ bc\geq 5).

(0.2.63)

We start from the initial
G
-matrix
G_{t_{0}}=I
.
Along the mutation sequence
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
,
\cdots
,
the following
g
-vectors appear in this order:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{g}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{g}_{3},\ \mathbf{g}_{4},\cdots,

(0.2.64)

where
\mathbf{g}_{i}
’s obey the recursion,

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{i+2}=\begin{cases}-\mathbf{g}_{i}+b\mathbf{g}_{i+1}&\text{$i$ odd},\\
-\mathbf{g}_{i}+c\mathbf{g}_{i+1}&\text{$i$ even}.\end{cases}

(0.2.65)

On the other hand,
along the mutation sequence
\mu_{2}
,
\mu_{1}
,
\cdots

from the initial
G
-matrix,
the following
g
-vectors appear in this order:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}c\\
-1\\
\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}bc-1\\
-b\\
\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{3},\ \mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{4},\cdots,

(0.2.66)

where
\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{i}
’s obey the recursion,

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{i+2}=\begin{cases}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{i}+c\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{i+1}&\text{$i$ odd},\\
-\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{i}+b\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{i+1}&\text{$i$ even}.\end{cases}

(0.2.67)

It is known
[
Rea14
, Eqs. (9.11), (9.12)]

that,
in the limit
i\rightarrow\infty
,
the rays
\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i})

and
\sigma(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{i})

monotonically converge to

\sigma(\mathbf{v})
and
\sigma(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
, respectively,
where

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}=\begin{pmatrix}bc-\sqrt{bc(bc-4)}\\
-2b\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}bc+\sqrt{bc(bc-4)}\\
-2b\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(0.2.68)

The
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G})
is depicted in Figure
1
(f).
It covers the region excluding
\sigma(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\setminus\{0\}

(the hatched region
in the figure).

0.2.6
Piecewise linear isomorphisms between
G
-fans

In this subsection
we study a canonical bijection between the sets
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})

and
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{1}})
for any
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
The idea comes from
[
GHKK18
]
,
but
this is independent of their results.
Also, the result of this and the next subsections has considerable overlap
with the results in
[
Rea14
,
Rea20b
]
,
in which
G
-fans were studied as a part of
mutation fans.

Let
\mathbf{B}
be any
B
pattern.
Let us start with the following consequence of the sign-coherence.

Proposition 0.2.20
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then, for any
t_{0},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
, the cone
\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})
intersects
the hyperplanes
\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\perp}

(i=1,\dots,n)
only in the boundary
of
\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})
.

Proof.

Suppose that a cone
\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})

intersects with
\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\perp}
for some
i
in its interior

\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})
.
This means that the
i
th row of the
G
-matrix
G_{t}^{t_{0}}

has both positive and negative components.
This contradicts the row sign-coherence of
G_{t}^{t_{0}}

in Proposition
0.2.7
.
∎

Let
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be vertices that are
k
-adjacent.
Recall the formula
(
0.2.20
),
which is valid under the assumption of the sign-coherence of
C
-matrices.
It is written in the vector form as follows.

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{1}}=(J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})\mathbf{g}^{t_{0}}_{i;t},\quad(i=1,\dots,n).

(0.2.69)

Let us introduce the half spaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,+}=\{\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\mid v_{k}\geq 0\},\quad\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,-}=\{\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\mid v_{k}\leq 0\}.

(0.2.70)

The matrix
(J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})

in (
0.2.69
)
is regarded as a piecewise linear map

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}:&\mathbb{R}^{n}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
&\mathbf{v}&\mapsto&\begin{cases}(J_{k}+[B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})\mathbf{v}&\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,+},\\
(J_{k}+[-B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})\mathbf{v}&\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,-},\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(0.2.71)

where we note that
\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathbf{v})=\mathbf{v}
if
v_{k}=0
.
We have, by (
0.2.69
) and (
0.2.71
),
for any
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
and
i=1,\dots,k
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathbf{g}^{t_{0}}_{i;t})=\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{1}}.

(0.2.72)

The map
\varphi_{t_{1}}^{t_{0}}
is also defined in the same way
by replacing
B_{t_{0}}
with
B_{t_{1}}
.

Proposition 0.2.21
.

The following relation holds:

\displaystyle\varphi_{t_{1}}^{t_{0}}\circ\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\circ\varphi_{t_{1}}^{t_{0}}=\mathrm{id}.

(0.2.73)

Proof.

Note that, for
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathbf{v})
,
we have

v^{\prime}_{k}=-v_{k}
due to
b_{kk}^{t_{0}}=0
.
We also recall that
(B_{t_{1}})^{\bullet k}=-(B_{t_{0}})^{\bullet k}
.
Then, for
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,+}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{t_{1}}^{t_{0}}\circ\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathbf{v})&=(J_{k}+[-B_{t_{1}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})(J_{k}+[B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})\mathbf{v}\\
&=(J_{k}+[B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})^{2}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}\end{split}

(0.2.74)

thanks to Lemma
