0.1.10
.
The other case is similar.

∎

Proposition 0.2.22

(cf.
[
Rea14
, Prop. 8.13]
)
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}}))

\displaystyle={\sigma}(G_{t}^{t_{1}}),\quad(t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}).

(0.2.75)

Therefore,
the correspondence

\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}:\mathbf{g}^{t_{0}}_{i;t}\mapsto\mathbf{g}^{t_{1}}_{i;t}

gives a bijection
between
{\Delta}(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
and
{\Delta}(\mathbf{G}^{t_{1}})

preserving
the intersection and the inclusion of cones.

Proof.

We only need to prove
(
0.2.75
).
By Proposition
0.2.20
, each cone
\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})

belongs to either
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,+}
or
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,-}
,
so that it is
linearly
transformed under
\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
.
Therefore, by (
0.2.72
),
its image is
\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{1}})
.
∎

Remark 0.2.23
.

For the later use,
we record the following fact in the above proof.

•

To prove (
0.2.75
), we only use the formula
(
0.2.69
) for
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
and
t
therein,
and the row sign-coherence
of
G_{t}^{t_{0}}
therein.

Example 0.2.24
.

Let us clarify
Proposition

0.2.22
explicitly for type
A_{2}

based on the convention in Section
0.2.5
.
The fans
{\Delta}(\mathbf{G}^{t_{-1}})
,
{\Delta}(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
,
{\Delta}(\mathbf{G}^{t_{1}})

are depicted in Figure
2
.
Also, the piecewise linear maps
\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
and
\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{-1}}

are given by combining the following linear maps:

\displaystyle J_{1}+[B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet 1}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad J_{1}+[-B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet 1}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

(0.2.76)

\displaystyle J_{2}+[B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet 2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad J_{2}+[-B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet 2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(0.2.77)

Then, the correspondence in Proposition

0.2.22
can be easily confirmed
in Figure
2
.

\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{-1}}
\longleftarrow
{\Delta}(\mathbf{G}^{t_{-1}})
G_{t_{4}}^{t_{-1}}=G_{t_{-1}}^{t_{-1}}
G_{t_{0}}^{t_{-1}}
G_{t_{1}}^{t_{-1}}
G_{t_{3}}^{t_{-1}}
G_{t_{2}}^{t_{-1}}
\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
\longrightarrow
{\Delta}(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
G_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}
G_{t_{1}}^{t_{0}}
G_{t_{2}}^{t_{0}}
G_{t_{3}}^{t_{0}}
G_{t_{4}}^{t_{0}}
{\Delta}(\mathbf{G}^{t_{1}})
G_{t_{1}}^{t_{1}}
G_{t_{2}}^{t_{1}}
G_{t_{3}}^{t_{1}}
G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
G_{t_{4}}^{t_{1}}

Figure 2:
Bijections between
G
-fans for type
A_{2}
.

By applying the above isomorphism repeatedly along the tree
\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
we obtain the following important fact.

Proposition 0.2.25
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then,
for any vertices
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
which are not necessarily adjacent,
the correspondence

\mathbf{g}^{t_{0}}_{i;t}\mapsto\mathbf{g}^{t_{1}}_{i;t}

gives a bijection
between
{\Delta}(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
and
{\Delta}(\mathbf{G}^{t_{1}})

preserving
the intersection and the inclusion
of cones.

For the later use, we also describe the relation between

\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
here and
a certain piecewise linear isomorphism in
[
GHKK18
]
.

Again, let
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be vertices that are
k
-adjacent.
First, for
B_{t_{0}}
and
k
, we introduce a basis
\mathbf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{n}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{i}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle-\mathbf{e}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}[-b_{jk;t_{0}}]_{+}\mathbf{e}_{j}&i=k,\\
\mathbf{e}_{i}&i\neq k.\end{cases}

(0.2.78)

In fact,
by specializing the formula in (
0.2.13
)
with
t=t_{0}
,
and applying the fact
\varepsilon_{k;t_{0}}=1
therein,
we see that

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{i}=\mathbf{g}_{i;t_{1}}^{t_{0}}.

(0.2.79)

The change of a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

from
\mathbf{e}_{1}
, …,
\mathbf{e}_{n}

to
\mathbf{a}_{1}
, …,
\mathbf{a}_{n}

induces the following linear isomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}:&\mathbb{R}^{n}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
&\mathbf{v}&\mapsto&(J_{k}+[-B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})\mathbf{v}\end{matrix}

(0.2.80)

such that
\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathbf{a}_{i})=\mathbf{e}_{i}
.

Next, for the same
B_{t_{0}}
and
k
,
we define a piecewise linear map
T_{k;t_{0}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

following
[
GHKK18
]

as

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}T_{k;t_{0}}:&\mathbb{R}^{n}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
&\mathbf{v}&\mapsto&\begin{cases}(I+(B_{t_{0}})^{\bullet k})\mathbf{v}&\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,+},\\
\mathbf{v}&\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,-}.\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(0.2.81)

The following fact appeared in
[
Mul16
,
Rea20b
]
.

Proposition 0.2.26

(
[
Mul16
, Lemma 5.2.1]
,
[
Rea20b
, Cor. 4.4]
)
.

The above maps are related as follows:

\displaystyle\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}=\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\circ T_{k;t_{0}}.

(0.2.82)

Proof.

By the definitions of these maps,

\displaystyle\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\circ T_{k;t_{0}}(\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(J_{k}+[-B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})(I+(B_{t_{0}})^{\bullet k})\mathbf{v}&\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,+},\\
(J_{k}+[-B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})\mathbf{v}&\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,-}.\end{cases}

(0.2.83)

Thanks to (
0.1.5
), we have

\displaystyle(J_{k}+[-B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})(I+(B_{t_{0}})^{\bullet k})=J_{k}+(B_{t_{0}})^{\bullet k}+[-B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k}=J_{k}+[B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k}.

∎

0.2.7
Proof of Theorem
0.2.17

Let us prove Theorem
0.2.17

under the assumption of the sign-coherence of
C
-matrices.

We recall that
the condition for
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
to form a fan
is stated as follows:

Condition 0.2.27
.

For any
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
the cones
\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})

and

\sigma(G_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}})
intersect in their common face.

Thanks to Proposition
0.2.22
,
Condition
0.2.27
is rephrased as follows,
where we used the bijection between
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
and
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t})

therein.

Condition 0.2.28
.

For any
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
the cones
\sigma(G_{t^{\prime}}^{t})

and

\sigma(I)
intersect in their common face,
where
\sigma(I)=\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{n})
.

By changing the notation, we may prove Condition
0.2.28
for
\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})

instead of
\sigma(G_{t^{\prime}}^{t})
therein.
Then, the condition can be further rephrased as follows:

Claim.

For any
t_{0},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
if a
g
-vector
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}
is contained in
\sigma(I)
, then
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{e}_{\ell}
for some
\ell
.

Let us prove the claim.
For
J\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
,
let
J^{c}=\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus J
be its compliment,
and let
\sigma_{J}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})
be the face of

\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})
generated by
\mathbf{g}_{j;t}^{t_{0}}
’s (
j\in J^{c}
).
For example, we have
\sigma_{\{1,\dots,n\}}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})=\{0\}
,

\sigma_{\{i\}^{c}}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})=\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}})
.

Thanks to Proposition
0.2.16
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{J}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})=\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})\cap\Biggl(\,\bigcap_{j\in J}({\mathbf{c}}_{j;t}^{t_{0}})^{\perp}\Biggr).

(0.2.84)

On the other hand,
since
{\mathbf{c}}_{j;t}^{t_{0}}
is either positive or negative,
we have

\displaystyle\sigma(I)\cap({\mathbf{c}}_{j;t}^{t_{0}})^{\perp}=\sigma(I)\cap\bigcap_{k\in K[j]}{(\mathbf{e}_{k})}^{\perp}=\sigma_{K[j]}(I),

(0.2.85)

where
K[j]
is the set of
k
such that
the
k
th component of
{\mathbf{c}}_{j;t}^{t_{0}}
is nonzero.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{J}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})\cap\sigma(I)=\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})\cap\Biggl(\,\bigcap_{j\in J}\sigma_{K[j]}(I)\Biggr)=\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})\cap\sigma_{K[J]}(I),

(0.2.86)

where
K[J]
is the set of
k
such that
the
k
th component of
{\mathbf{c}}_{j;t}^{t_{0}}
is nonzero
for some
j\in J
.
Now we set
J=\{i\}^{c}
.
Then, we have

\sigma_{\{i\}^{c}}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})=\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}})
and

K[J]=\{1,\dots,n\}
or
\{\ell\}^{c}
for some
\ell
.
In the former case,
we see from (
0.2.86
) that

\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}})

intersects
\sigma(I)
trivially.
In the latter case,
we have

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}})\cap\sigma(I)=\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}})\cap\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{\ell}).

(0.2.87)

Therefore,
if
\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}})\cap\sigma(I)\neq\{0\}
, we have
\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}})=\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{\ell})
.
Then, by the unimodularity (
0.2.3
),
we have
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{e}_{\ell}
as desired.
This
completes the proof of Theorem
0.2.17
.

0.2.8
Properties of
\hat{c}
-vectors

In this subsection we observe some properties
of
\hat{c}
-vectors that are relevant to
the scattering diagram method.

The following properties do not depend on
the sign-coherence conjecture.

Proposition 0.2.29
.

The following facts hold:

(a).
Any
\hat{c}
-vector
\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}
is orthogonal to the

{c}
-vector
{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}
.

(b).
Any
\hat{c}
-vector
\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}

is in the subspace in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
spanned by

\sigma_{i}(G_{t})
.

Proof.

(a).
Since
DB_{t_{0}}
is skew-symmetric, we have

\displaystyle({\mathbf{c}}_{i;t},\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t})_{D}=({\mathbf{c}}_{i;t})^{T}DB_{t_{0}}{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}=0.

(0.2.88)

(b).
By the second expression of
\hat{c}
-vectors
in (
0.1.57
),
we have

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji;t}\mathbf{g}_{j;t},

(0.2.89)

where we recall that
b_{ii;t}=0
.
∎

Definition 0.2.30
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Let
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}
be a
c
-vector, and
let
\varepsilon_{i;t}
be its tropical sign
in Definition
0.2.1
.
We call the vectors
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}=\varepsilon_{i;t}\mathbf{c}_{i;t}

and
\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{+}=\varepsilon_{i;t}\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}

a

c^{+}
-vector
and a

\hat{c}^{+}
-vector
,
respectively.

Remark 0.2.31
.

Any
c^{+}
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}
is
positive
due to the sign-coherence
assumption,
while a
\hat{c}^{+}
-vector
\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{+}
is not so at all in general.

The following fact is related to the
incoming/outgoing property

of walls appearing later in Definition
0.5.14
.

Proposition 0.2.32
.

Suppose that the sign-coherence conjecture holds.
Then, if

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{+}\in\sigma_{i}(G_{t}),

(0.2.90)

then
{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{+}=\mathbf{e}_{\ell}
for some
\ell
.

Proof.

In view of (
0.2.89
),
the condition (
0.2.90
)
is equivalent to the following condition:

The vector

\varepsilon_{i;t}\mathbf{b}_{i;t}
is either positive or zero.

(0.2.91)

Suppose that (
0.2.91
) happens, where we replace
i
with
k
therein
for the sake of convenience.
Let
t^{\prime}
be the vertex that is

k
-adjacent to
t
.
Then, the mutation (
0.2.13
) is simplified by (
0.2.91
)
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{i;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle-\mathbf{g}_{k;t}&i=k,\\
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}&i\neq k.\end{cases}

(0.2.92)

Now we recall the row sign-coherence of
G
-matrices
in Proposition
0.2.7
.
Then,
(
0.2.92
) happens only when
the following condition is satisfied:

•

For each
\ell=1,\dots,n
,
if the
\ell
th element of
\mathbf{g}_{k;t}
is nonzero,
then the
\ell
th element of other
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}
(
i\neq k
) vanishes.

Thanks to the unimodularity (
0.2.3
) of
G_{t}
,
one can sharpen the condition as follows:

•

There is some
\ell
such that

\mathbf{g}_{k;t}=\pm\mathbf{e}_{\ell}
,
and the
\ell
th element of other
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}
(
i\neq k
) vanishes.

Then, by the duality (
0.2.4
),

\mathbf{c}_{k;t}=\pm\mathbf{e}_{\ell}
.
Thus,
\mathbf{c}_{k;t}^{+}=\mathbf{e}_{\ell}
.
∎

0.3
Scattering diagrams and
C
- and
G
-matrices

In this section we present a proof of the sign-coherence conjecture
based on the results on scattering diagrams in
[
GHKK18
]
.
At the same time we also establish the correspondence
between a
G
-fan and the support of a scattering diagram.

0.3.1
Supports of scattering diagrams

To write down a precise definition of scattering diagrams in
[
GHKK18
]
requires considerable pages,
and it will be postponed in Section
0.5
.
Here we summarize the minimal information and results
[
GHKK18
]

that are needed to prove the sign-coherence conjecture.

Informally speaking,
for a certain vector space
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
a
scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}=\{\mathbf{w}_{\lambda}=(\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},f_{{\lambda}})\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
in
M_{\mathbb{R}}

is a finite or countably infinite
collection of
walls
,
where
\Lambda
is an index set,
and
each wall
\mathbf{w}_{\lambda}=(\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},f_{{\lambda}})
is a pair such that

\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
is a (not necessarily strongly convex) cone of codimension one in
M_{\mathbb{R}}

called the
support
of
\mathbf{w}_{\lambda}
,
and
f_{{\lambda}}
is a certain formal power series called the
wall function
of
\mathbf{w}_{\lambda}
.
The union of the supports of walls

\displaystyle\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}

(0.3.1)

is called the
support of a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
.

For any
nonsingular
skew-symmetrizable matrix
B
,
one can construct a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

called a
cluster scattering diagram
,
where
\mathfrak{s}
is a
seed data
in
[
GHKK18
]
corresponding to
B
.
There is a canonical isomorphism
\phi_{\mathfrak{s}}:M_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{\sim}}}\mathbb{R}^{n}

such that the image of
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
only depends on
B
.
We write this image as
\mathfrak{S}(B)
,
and we call it the
support diagram
for
B
, for simplicity.
A connected component of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathfrak{S}(B)

is called a
chamber
of
\mathfrak{S}(B)
.

From the viewpoint of cluster patterns,
the supports and the wall functions of walls
contain the information of the tropical and the nontropical part
of a cluster pattern,
respectively.
Since at this moment we are interested only in the tropical part, i.e.,
C
- and
G
-matrices,
we may safely forget wall functions, and concentrate on the support diagram

\mathfrak{S}(B)
.

Let
D
be a skew-symmetrizer of
B
in the form
(
0.2.44
),
and let

(\ ,\ )_{D}
be the inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined in (
0.2.45
).
The following facts are direct consequences of
the definition/theorem of the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
in
[
GHKK18
, Theorems 1.12 &1.28]
, which will appear later as
Theorem
0.5.27
.

Proposition 0.3.1

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorems 1.12 &1.28]
)
.

For any nonsingular skew-symmetrizable matrix
B
,
the support diagram
\mathfrak{S}(B)
has the following properties.

(a). The support
\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
of each wall of
\mathfrak{S}(B)
has a positive normal vector

\mathbf{n}_{\lambda}
with respect to the inner product
(\ ,\ )_{D}
.

(b).
Each hyperplane
\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\perp}
(
i=1,\dots,n
)
with respect to the inner product
(\ ,\ )_{D}

is a subset of
\mathfrak{S}(B)
.

(c).
The interior
\sigma^{\circ}(I)
of the
cone
\sigma(I)=\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{n})
is a
chamber
of

\mathfrak{S}(B)
.
In other words,
\sigma^{\circ}(I)
does not intersect
\mathfrak{S}(B)
,
while
its boundary belongs to
\mathfrak{S}(B)
.
(This is a consequence of (a) and (b).)

Also, the following property of the support diagram
\mathfrak{S}(B)

is obtained from
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.24]
,
which will appear later as
Theorem
0.6.7
,
together with
Proposition
0.2.26
.
See the explanation after Theorem
0.6.7

for details.

Proposition 0.3.2

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.24]
,

[
Mul16
, Lemma 5.2.1]
,
[
Rea20b
, Cor. 4.4]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{B}
be any nonsingular
B
-pattern.
Let
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be vertices that are
k
-adjacent.
Let

\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}

be
the map
defined in (
0.2.71
).
Then, the support diagrams
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}})

and
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{1}})
are related by

\displaystyle\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}}))

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{1}}).

(0.3.2)

This is clearly a parallel result for
G
-fans in Proposition

0.2.22
, which was proved
under the assumption of the sign-coherence of
C
-matrices.

0.3.2
Proof of sign-coherence conjecture

Let us present a proof of Conjecture
0.1.34

relying on Propositions
0.3.1
and
0.3.2
.
Our strategy is to establish both

•

the identification of
the interior
\sigma^{\circ}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})
of each
G
-cone
as a chamber in
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}})
,

•

the column sign-coherence of
C
-matrices
C_{t}^{t_{0}}
,

simultaneously
by the induction
on the distance
d(t_{0},t)
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
where both
t_{0}
and
t
vary.

Now we prove the following theorem.
See
[
Rea20b
, Theorem 4.10]
for a closely related result for mutation fans.

Theorem 0.3.3
.

Let
\mathbf{B}=\{B_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
be any nonsingular
B
-pattern.
Then, for any
t_{0},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
, the following facts hold:

(a). The set

\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})
is a chamber of
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}})
.

(b). Each
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}

is a normal vector of the support of some wall of
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}})
.

(c). The column sign-coherence holds for the
C
-matrix
C_{t}^{t_{0}}
.

Proof.

As in Section
0.2.2
,
we introduce the transpose
\mathbf{B}^{T}=\{(B_{t})^{T}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
of
\mathbf{B}
.
Let
\tilde{C}_{t}^{t_{0}}
,
\tilde{G}_{t}^{t_{0}}

be
C
- and
G
-matrices of
\mathbf{B}^{T}
.

Consider the following claims
(a)_{d}
,
(b)_{d}
,
(c)_{d}
for
d=0,1,2
, ….

(a)_{d}
.
For any
t_{0},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
such that
d(t_{0},t)=d
, the following facts hold:

\displaystyle|C_{t}^{t_{0}}|

\displaystyle=|G_{t}^{t_{0}}|\in\{1,-1\},

(0.3.3)

\displaystyle D^{-1}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})^{T}DC_{t}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=I,

(0.3.4)

\displaystyle C_{t}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{G}_{t_{0}}^{t})^{T},

(0.3.5)

\displaystyle G_{t}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{C}_{t_{0}}^{t})^{T}.

(0.3.6)

(b)_{d}
.
For any
t_{0},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
such that
d(t_{0},t)=d
, the following facts hold:

(i).

The set

\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})
is a chamber of
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}})
.

(ii).

Each
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}

is a normal vector of the support of a wall of
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}})
.

(iii).

The column sign-coherence holds for
C_{t}^{t_{0}}
.

(iv).

The row sign-coherence holds for
G_{t}^{t_{0}}
.

(c)_{d}
.
For any
t_{0},t_{1},t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
such that

•

d(t_{0},t)=d
,

•

t_{0}
and
t_{1}
are
k
-adjacent,

•

t
and
t^{\prime}
are
k
-adjacent,

the following facts hold:

\displaystyle C_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=C_{t}^{t_{0}}(J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}_{\bullet k}(C^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t}]_{+}^{k\bullet}),

(0.3.7)

\displaystyle G_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=G_{t}^{t_{0}}(J_{k}+[-{\varepsilon}_{\bullet k}(C^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t}]_{+}^{\bullet k}),

(0.3.8)

\displaystyle C^{t_{1}}_{t}

\displaystyle=(J_{k}+[-{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{k\bullet})C^{t_{0}}_{t},

(0.3.9)

\displaystyle G^{t_{1}}_{t}

\displaystyle=(J_{k}+[{\varepsilon}_{k\bullet}(G^{t_{0}}_{t})B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})G^{t_{0}}_{t}.

(0.3.10)

We prove the claims in the following order,

\displaystyle(a)_{0}\Longrightarrow(b)_{0}\Longrightarrow(c)_{0}\Longrightarrow(a)_{1}\Longrightarrow(b)_{1}\Longrightarrow(c)_{1}\Longrightarrow(a)_{2}\Longrightarrow\cdots,

(0.3.11)

assuming all preceding claims.
Moreover, we run the same induction procedure for

C
- and
G
-matrices

\tilde{C}_{t}^{t_{0}}
,
\tilde{G}_{t}^{t_{0}}
of
\mathbf{B}^{T}
in the background.

We first prove
(a)_{0}
,
(b)_{0}
, and
(c)_{0}
.
The claims
(a)_{0}
and
(c)_{0}
were proved in Propositions
0.2.3
and
0.2.7
.
The claim
(b)_{0}
holds because
C_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}=G_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}=I
,
and Proposition
0.3.1
(c),
where we have already received an important input from
scattering diagrams.

Now, assuming the claims in
(
0.3.11
) up to
(c)_{d}
, we prove
(a)_{d+1}
.
This was already proved in Propositions
0.2.3
and
0.2.7

by taking account of Remarks
0.2.4
and
0.2.8
.

Next, assuming the claims in
(
0.3.11
) up to
(a)_{d+1}
,
we prove
(b)_{d+1}
.
(This is the highlights of the proof of the sign-coherence conjecture.)
Let
t_{0},t_{1},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}

be vertices such that
d(t_{0},t)=d
,
d(t_{1},t)=d+1
, and

t_{0}
and
t_{1}
are
k
-adjacent.
By
(b)_{d}
,

\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})
is a chamber in
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}})
.
By
(a)_{d}
,
(c)_{d}
, Proposition
0.2.22
,
and Remark
0.2.23
,
we have
\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{1}})=\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\sigma(G_{t}^{t_{0}}))
.
Meanwhile,
by Proposition

0.3.2
,
the piecewise linear isomorphism
\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
,
which is homeomorphism,
maps a chamber in
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}})

to a chamber in
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{1}})
.
Therefore,
\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t}^{t_{1}})
is a chamber in
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{1}})
.
This proves (i).
By (i),
each face

\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{1}})
(
i=1,\dots,n
),
which is a cone of codimension one by
(a)_{d+1}
,
is a subset of the union of the supports of some walls of
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{1}})
.
Meanwhile,
by
(a)_{d+1}
,
each
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{1}}
is a normal vector of
the face
\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{1}})

with respect to the inner product
(\ ,\ )_{D}
.
Therefore,
each
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{1}}

is a normal vector of the support of some wall of
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{1}})
.
See Proposition
0.2.16
.
This proves (ii).
Then, by Proposition
0.3.1
(a),
the
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{1}}
is either positive or negative.
This proves (iii), i.e., the column sign-coherence of the
C
-matrix
C_{t}^{t_{1}}
.
The same result is proved in the background for the
C
-matrix
\tilde{C}_{t_{1}}^{t}

of the
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}^{T}
.
Thus, by
(a)_{d+1}
, the
G
-matrix
G_{t}^{t_{1}}
is row sign-coherent.
This proves (iv).

Finally,
assuming the claims in
(
0.3.11
) up to
(b)_{d+1}
,
we prove
(c)_{d+1}
.
This was already proved in Propositions
0.2.3
and
0.2.7

by taking account of Remarks
0.2.4
and
0.2.8
.
∎

We may roughly describe the results in Theorem
0.3.3
(a)
that
the
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
is embedded in the corresponding cluster scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.

We immediately extend Theorem
0.3.3
(c)
for any (possibly singular)
B
-pattern by
Proposition
0.2.11
,

Theorem 0.3.4
.

Let
\mathbf{B}=\{B_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
be any (possibly singular)
B
-pattern.
Then, for any
t_{0},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
the column sign-coherence for the
C
-matrix
C_{t}^{t_{0}}
holds.

Proof.

Let
\overline{C}_{t}
and
\overline{G}_{t}

be
C
- and
G
-matrices
of the principal extension
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
of
\mathbf{B}
with the initial vertex
t_{0}

in Section
0.2.3
.
The matrix
\overline{C}_{t}
is column sign-coherent by Theorem
0.3.3
(c).
Then, the claim follows from (
0.2.39
).
∎

This completes the proof of Conjecture
0.1.34
.

From now on, we use the column sign-coherence of
C
-matrices
not as an assumption but as a theorem.

Remark 0.3.5
.

In Part III we show that Propositions
0.3.1
and
0.3.2

actually
holds for any (possibly singular)
B
-pattern.
Thus, Theorem
0.3.3
also holds for any (possibly singular)
B
-pattern
by the same proof.
This gives a direct proof of the sign-coherence without using
Proposition
0.2.11
.

0.4
More about
F
-polynomials

Let us turn to study
F
-polynomials,
namely, the nontropical part of a cluster pattern.

0.4.1
Fock-Goncharov decomposition

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be any cluster pattern.
We come back to the mutations of
x
-
and
y
-variables, especially in the
\varepsilon
-expression
(
0.1.10
) and (
0.1.11
).
Let
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be vertices that are
k
-adjacent.
One can regard
mutations (
0.1.10
) and (
0.1.11
)
as isomorphisms between rational function fields as follows,
where
\mathbf{x}_{t}
,
\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}
,
\mathbf{y}_{t}
,
\mathbf{y}_{t^{\prime}}
are
n
-tuple of formal variables:

\displaystyle\mu_{k;t}:\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t}),\ \mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t^{\prime}})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t}),

(0.4.1)

\displaystyle\mu_{k;t}(x_{i;t^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{k;t}^{-1}\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j;t}^{[-\varepsilon b_{jk;t}]_{+}}\Biggr)(1+\hat{y}_{k;t}^{\varepsilon})&i=k,\\
x_{i;t}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.4.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{k;t}(y_{i;t^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k;t}^{-1}&i=k,\\
y_{i;t}y_{k;t}^{[\varepsilon b_{ki;t}]_{+}}(1+y_{k;t}^{\varepsilon})^{-b_{ki;t}}&i\neq k.\end{cases}

(0.4.3)

Note that the map is in the opposite direction, namely, from
t^{\prime}
to
t
.
Here and below, we use common symbols for maps for

x
- and
y
-variables as above in view of the parallelism between them.

To make use of the flexibility of the choice of
sign
\varepsilon
, we first choose a given
initial vertex
t_{0}
.
Since we have already established the sign-coherence
of
C
-matrices
C_{t}^{t_{0}}=C_{t}
,
the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\varepsilon_{i;t}
in
Definition
0.2.1
is defined for any
t
and
i
.
Now we set
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{k;t}

in (
0.4.2
) and (
0.4.3
).

Then, modifying the idea of
[
FG09
, §2.1]
with tropical signs,
we consider the following decompositions of the maps
\mu_{k;t}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{k;t}=\rho_{k;t}\circ\tau_{k;t}.

(0.4.4)

Here,
\tau_{k;t}^{t_{0}}=\tau_{k;t}
are the following isomorphisms,

\displaystyle\tau_{k;t}:\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t}),\ \mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t^{\prime}})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t}),

(0.4.5)

\displaystyle\tau_{k;t}(x_{i;t^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{k;t}^{-1}\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j;t}^{[-\varepsilon_{k;t}b_{jk;t}]_{+}}&i=k,\\
x_{i;t}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.4.6)

\displaystyle\tau_{k;t}(y_{i;t^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k;t}^{-1}&i=k,\\
y_{i;t}y_{k;t}^{[\varepsilon_{k;t}b_{ki;t}]_{+}}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.4.7)

while
\rho_{k;t}^{t_{0}}=\rho_{k;t}
are the following automorphisms,

\displaystyle\rho_{k;t}:\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t}),\ \mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t}),

(0.4.8)

\displaystyle\rho_{k;t}(x_{i;t})=x_{i;t}(1+\hat{y}_{k;t}^{\varepsilon_{k;t}})^{-\delta_{ik}},

(0.4.9)

\displaystyle\rho_{k;t}(y_{i;t})=y_{i;t}(1+y_{k;t}^{\varepsilon_{k;t}})^{-b_{ki;t}}.

(0.4.10)

Note that the maps
\tau_{k;t}
and
\rho_{k;t}
depend on the choice
of the initial vertex
t_{0}

through tropical signs.
Also, they are defined only after establishing the sign-coherence of
C
-matrices.
We call the decomposition (
0.4.4
) the
Fock-Goncharov decomposition

of a mutation
{\mu}_{k;t}
(
with tropical sign
) with respect to the initial vertex
t_{0}
.
We also call
\tau_{k;t}
and
\rho_{k;t}
the
tropical part

and the
nontropical part
of
{\mu}_{k;t}
, respectively.
Indeed,
it is clear that (
0.4.6
) and (
0.4.7
) are the exponential form
of the mutations of
g
-vectors (
0.2.13
) and
c
-vectors (
0.2.12
), respectively.

Remark 0.4.1
.

The decomposition (
0.4.4
) was introduced in
[
FG09
]

for
\varepsilon=1
.
The signed version here were used, for example, in the application to
the Stokes automorphisms

[
IN14
]
and
dilogarithm identities

[
GNR17
]
.

Proposition 0.4.2
.

Let
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be vertices that are
k
-adjacent.
Then, the following relations hold.

\displaystyle\mu_{k;t^{\prime}}\circ\mu_{k;t}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id},

(0.4.11)

\displaystyle\tau_{k;t^{\prime}}\circ\tau_{k;t}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}.

(0.4.12)

Proof.

The equality (
0.4.11
) is nothing but the involution of the mutations of
x
- and
y
-variables.
The equality (
0.4.12
) for both
x
- and
y
-variables can be verified by the fact,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k;t^{\prime}}=-\varepsilon_{k;t},\quad b_{ki;t^{\prime}}=-b_{ki;t},\quad b_{jk;t^{\prime}}=-b_{jk;t}.

(0.4.13)

∎

For any
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
consider a sequence of vertices
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
, …,
t_{r+1}=t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}

such that
they are sequentially adjacent with edges labeled by
k_{0}
, …,
k_{r}
.
Then, we define isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle:=\mu_{k_{0};t_{0}}\circ\mu_{k_{1};t_{1}}\circ\dots\circ\mu_{k_{r};t_{r}}:\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}),\ \mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}),

(0.4.14)

\displaystyle\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle:=\tau_{k_{0};t_{0}}\circ\tau_{k_{1};t_{1}}\circ\dots\circ\tau_{k_{r};t_{r}}:\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}),\ \mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}),

(0.4.15)

where we set
\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{y}
.
Thanks to Proposition
0.4.2
,
\mu_{t}^{t_{0}}
and

\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}
depend only on
t_{0}
and
t
.
Namely, we do not have to care about the redundancy
k_{s+1}=k_{s}

in the sequence
k_{0},\dots,k_{r}
.

Recall the notions of tropical
x
- and
y
-variables in
(
0.1.54
) and (
0.1.53
).
The following proposition tells
that the tropical parts
\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}
are nothing but the
mutations of tropical
x
- and
y
-variables.

Proposition 0.4.3
.

The following formulas hold:

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{t_{0}}(x_{i;t})

\displaystyle=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}),

(0.4.16)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{t_{0}}(y_{i;t})

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}(\mathbf{y})^{b_{ji;t}},

(0.4.17)

\displaystyle\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}(x_{i;t})

\displaystyle=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}},

(0.4.18)

\displaystyle\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}(y_{i;t})

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}.

(0.4.19)

Proof.

The first two equalities are the separation formulas
in Theorem
0.1.23
.
Let us prove the equality (
0.4.18
) for
x
-variables by the induction
on
t

along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
starting from
t_{0}
.
For
t=t_{0}
,
x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t_{0}}}=x^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}=x_{i}
.
Therefore, (
0.4.18
) holds.
Suppose that it is true for
t
with
d(t_{0},t)=d
.
Let
t^{\prime}
be the vertex that is

k
-adjacent to
t

such that
d(t_{0},t^{\prime})=d+1
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \tau_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}(x_{i;t^{\prime}})=(\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}\circ\tau_{k;t})(x_{i;t^{\prime}})\\
&=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}\Biggl(x_{k;t}^{-1}\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j;t}^{[-\varepsilon_{k;t}b_{jk;t}]_{+}}\Biggr)=x^{-\mathbf{g}_{k;t}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}[-\varepsilon_{k;t}b_{jk;t}]_{+}\mathbf{g}_{j;t}}&i=k,\\
\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}(x_{i;t})=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}&i\neq k\end{cases}\\
&=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t^{\prime}}},\end{split}

(0.4.20)

where we used (
0.2.13
) in the last equality.
Therefore, (
0.4.18
) holds for
t^{\prime}
.
Similarly,
the equality (
0.4.19
) for
y
-variables follows from (
0.2.12
).
∎

Under these maps,
\hat{y}
-variables transform in the same way as
y
-variables as expected.

Proposition 0.4.4
.

The following formulas hold:

\displaystyle\tau_{k;t}(\hat{y}_{i;t^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\hat{y}_{k;t}^{-1}&i=k,\\
\hat{y}_{i;t}\hat{y}_{k;t}^{[\varepsilon_{k;t}b_{ki;t}]_{+}}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.4.21)

\displaystyle\rho_{k;t}(\hat{y}_{i;t})

\displaystyle=\hat{y}_{i;t}(1+\hat{y}_{k;t}^{\varepsilon_{k;t}})^{-b_{ki;t}},

(0.4.22)

\displaystyle\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}(\hat{y}_{i;t})

\displaystyle=\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}=x^{\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}},

(0.4.23)

where
\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}=B_{t_{0}}\mathbf{c}_{i;t}
is a
\hat{c}
-vector in
(
0.1.57
).

Proof.

The equality (
0.4.21
)
follows from
(
0.4.6
) and (
0.1.12
).
The equality (
0.4.22
)
follows from (
0.4.9
).
The equality (
0.4.23
)
follows from (
0.4.18
) and (
0.1.56
),

∎

0.4.2
Nontropical parts and
F
-polynomials

Observing Proposition
0.4.3
,
it is clear that the nontropical parts
\rho_{k;t}
are responsible to
generate and
mutate
F
-polynomials.
Let us make this statement more manifest.

In addition to the inner product
(\ ,\ )_{D}

in (
0.2.45
),
we introduce a skew-symmetric form

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}_{DB}:=\mathbf{u}^{T}DB\mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{u},B\mathbf{v})_{D},

(0.4.24)

where
B=B_{t_{0}}
.
Then, we introduce the following automorphisms
\mathfrak{q}_{k;t}
for the initial
x
- and
y
-variables,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{k;t}:\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}),\ \mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}),

(0.4.25)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{k;t}(x^{\mathbf{m}})

\displaystyle=x^{\mathbf{m}}(1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{k;t}^{+}})^{-(\delta_{k}\mathbf{c}_{k;t},\mathbf{m})_{D}}

(0.4.26)

\displaystyle=x^{\mathbf{m}}(1+x^{\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{k;t}^{+}})^{-(\delta_{k}\mathbf{c}_{k;t},\mathbf{m})_{D}},

(0.4.27)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{k;t}(y^{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{{\mathbf{c}}_{k;t}^{+}})^{-\{\delta_{k}\mathbf{c}_{k;t},\mathbf{n}\}_{DB}},

(0.4.28)

where
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, and

\mathbf{c}_{k;t}^{+}=\varepsilon_{k;t}\mathbf{c}_{k;t}
and

\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{k;t}^{+}=\varepsilon_{k;t}\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{k;t}

are a
c^{+}
-vector
and
a
\hat{c}^{+}
-vector,
respectively,
in Definition
0.2.30
.

The seemingly asymmetric definitions in
(
0.4.26
) and (
0.4.28
)
are justified by the following properties.

Proposition 0.4.5
.

The following facts hold:

(a). We have the formulas

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{k;t}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}(1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{k;t}^{+}})^{-\delta_{ik}},

(0.4.29)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{k;t}(y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}})=y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}(1+y^{{\mathbf{c}}_{k;t}^{+}})^{-b_{ki;t}}.

(0.4.30)

(b). The following relation holds:

\displaystyle\tau^{t_{0}}_{t}\circ\rho_{k;t}=\mathfrak{q}_{k;t}\circ\tau^{t_{0}}_{t}.

(0.4.31)

(c). If
t^{\prime}
and
t
are
k
-adjacent, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{k;t^{\prime}}\circ\mathfrak{q}_{k;t}=\mathrm{id}.

(0.4.32)

Proof.

(a).
The claim follows from the following equalities:

\displaystyle(\delta_{k}\mathbf{c}_{k;t},\mathbf{g}_{i;t})_{D}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ki},

(0.4.33)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{k}\mathbf{c}_{k;t},\mathbf{c}_{i;t}\}_{DB}

\displaystyle=b_{ki;t}.

(0.4.34)

The equality (
0.4.33
) is
the duality in (
0.2.47
).
The equality (
0.4.34
) follows from
(
0.2.14
) as follows:

\displaystyle D^{-1}(C_{t}^{T}DB_{t_{0}}C_{t})=D^{-1}(DB_{t})=B_{t}.

(0.4.35)

(b).
Thanks to (
0.4.29
) and Propositions
0.4.3
and
0.4.4
,
we have

\displaystyle x_{i;t}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\rho_{k;t}}}}\ x_{i;t}(1+\hat{y}_{k;t}^{\varepsilon_{k;t}})^{-\delta_{ik}}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}}}}\ x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}(1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{k;t}^{+}})^{-\delta_{ik}},

(0.4.36)

\displaystyle x_{i;t}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}}}}\ x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\mathfrak{q}_{k;t}}}}\ x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}(1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{k;t}^{+}})^{-\delta_{ik}}.

(0.4.37)

Therefore, the equality (
0.4.31
) holds for
x
-variables. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle y_{i;t}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\rho_{k;t}}}}\ y_{i;t}(1+{y}_{k;t}^{\varepsilon_{k;t}})^{-b_{ki;t}}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}}}}\ y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}(1+{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{k;t}^{+}})^{-b_{ki;t}},

(0.4.38)

\displaystyle y_{i;t}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}}}}\ y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\mathfrak{q}_{k;t}}}}\ y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}(1+{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{k;t}^{+}})^{-b_{ki;t}}.

(0.4.39)

Therefore, the equality (
0.4.31
) holds also for
y
-variables.

(c).
This follows from
\mathbf{c}_{k;t^{\prime}}=-\mathbf{c}_{k;t}
.
∎

For the same

t_{0}
,
t_{1}
, …,
t_{r+1}=t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}

for
\mu_{t}^{t_{0}}
and
\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}
in
(
0.4.14
) and (
0.4.15
),
we define the automorphism

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{q}_{k_{0};t_{0}}\circ\mathfrak{q}_{k_{1};t_{1}}\circ\dots\circ\mathfrak{q}_{k_{r};t_{r}}:\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}),\ \mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}).

(0.4.40)

Again, by Proposition
0.4.5
(c),
it depends only on
t_{0}
and
t
.

One can separate the tropical and the nontropical
parts of
\mu^{t_{0}}_{t}

as follows:

Proposition 0.4.6
.

The following decomposition holds:

\displaystyle\mu^{t_{0}}_{t}=\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}\circ\tau^{t_{0}}_{t}:\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}),\ \mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}).

(0.4.41)

Proof.

First, we note that

\displaystyle\rho_{k_{0};t_{0}}(x_{i})=x_{i}(1+\hat{y}_{k_{0}})^{-\delta_{ik_{0}}}=\mathfrak{q}_{k_{0};t_{0}}(x_{i}),

(0.4.42)

\displaystyle\rho_{k_{0};t_{0}}(y_{i})=y_{i}(1+y_{k_{0}})^{-b_{k_{0}i;t_{0}}}=\mathfrak{q}_{k_{0};t_{0}}(y_{i}).

(0.4.43)

For
x
-variables, for example,
by Proposition
0.4.5
(b),
we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{r}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{r}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{r-1}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{2}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}})}
\tau_{k_{r};t_{r}}
\tau^{t_{0}}_{t_{r}}
\tau^{t_{0}}_{t_{r-1}}
\tau_{k_{r-1};t_{r-1}}
\tau_{k_{1};t_{1}}
\tau_{k_{0};t_{0}}
\tau_{k_{0};t_{0}}
\mu_{k_{r};t_{r}}
\mu_{k_{1};t_{1}}
\mu_{k_{0};t_{0}}
\rho_{k_{r};t_{r}}
\rho_{k_{1};t_{1}}
\mathfrak{q}_{k_{r};t_{r}}
\mathfrak{q}_{k_{1};t_{1}}
\rho_{k_{0};t_{0}}=\mathfrak{q}_{k_{0};t_{0}}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\ddots}

(0.4.44)

Therefore, the claim holds.
∎

We conclude
that the automorphisms
\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}
generate
the nontropical parts of

x
- and
y
-variables
in the following manner.

Theorem 0.4.7
.

The following formulas hold:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})

\displaystyle=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}),

(0.4.45)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}(y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}})

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}(\mathbf{y})^{b_{ji;t}},

(0.4.46)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}(\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}})

\displaystyle=\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{b_{ji;t}}.

(0.4.47)

Proof.

The equalities (
0.4.45
) and (
0.4.46
)
follow from Propositions

0.4.3
and
0.4.6
.
For example,
for
x
-variables,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})=\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}(\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}(x_{i;t}))=\mu_{t}^{t_{0}}(x_{i;t})=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}).

(0.4.48)

Similarly,
the equality (
0.4.47
)
follows from Propositions

0.4.4
and
0.4.6
.
∎

0.4.3
Detropicalization

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be any cluster pattern of rank
n
,
and let
t_{0}
be a given initial vertex.
Let
S_{n}
be the symmetric group of degree
n
.
We define the (left) action of a permutation
\nu\in S_{n}

on
x
- and
y
-variables, etc., as follows.

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t},

\displaystyle\quad x^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=x_{\nu^{-1}(i);t},

(0.4.49)

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t},

\displaystyle\quad y^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=y_{\nu^{-1}(i);t},

(0.4.50)

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu B_{t},

\displaystyle\quad b^{\prime}_{ij}

\displaystyle=b_{\nu^{-1}(i)\nu^{-1}(j);t},

(0.4.51)

\displaystyle C^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu C_{t},

\displaystyle\quad c^{\prime}_{ij}

\displaystyle=c_{i\nu^{-1}(j);t},

(0.4.52)

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu G_{t},

\displaystyle\quad g^{\prime}_{ij}

\displaystyle=g_{i\nu^{-1}(j);t}.

(0.4.53)

Note that these actions are compatible with the separation formulas
in Theorem
0.1.23
.

For any
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
and any permutation
\nu\in S_{n}
,
we define the following isomorphisms.

\displaystyle\nu_{t}^{t^{\prime}}:\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}),\ \mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{y}_{t^{\prime}}),

(0.4.54)

\displaystyle\nu_{t}^{t^{\prime}}(x_{i;t})=x_{\nu^{-1}(i);t^{\prime}},

(0.4.55)

\displaystyle\nu_{t}^{t^{\prime}}(y_{i;t})=y_{\nu^{-1}(i);t^{\prime}}.

(0.4.56)

Then,
by
Proposition
0.4.3
,
one can rephrase the
periodicity condition

for
G
-matrices and
x
-variables as follows:

\displaystyle G_{t}^{t_{0}}=\nu G_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\tau_{t}^{t_{0}}=\tau_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}\circ\nu_{t}^{t^{\prime}}\ \mbox{(for $x$-variables)},

(0.4.57)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mu_{t}^{t_{0}}=\mu_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}\circ\nu_{t}^{t^{\prime}}\ \mbox{(for $x$-variables)}.

(0.4.58)

Also, the parallel statement holds for
C
-matrices and
y
-variables.

Proposition 0.4.8
.

Let
t_{0},t,t^{\prime}
be any vertices in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
Suppose that

\displaystyle G_{t}^{t_{0}}=\nu G_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}

(0.4.59)

holds.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}\quad

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{q}_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}\ \mbox{\rm(for $x$-variables)}.

(0.4.60)

Also, the parallel statement holds for
C
-matrices and
y
-variables.

Proof.

The claim follows from (
0.4.57
), (
0.4.58
),
and
Proposition
0.4.6
.
∎

Note that the permutation
\nu_{t}^{t^{\prime}}
drops off in the condition for
\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}

in (
0.4.60
).

The properties

\displaystyle G_{t}^{t_{0}}=\nu G_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathbf{x}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}},

(0.4.61)

\displaystyle C_{t}^{t_{0}}=\nu C_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathbf{y}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{y}_{t^{\prime}}

(0.4.62)

are called the
detropicalization

of
x
- and
y
-variables, respectively.
They are the core of the
synchronicity phenomenon
occurring
in cluster patterns
systematically studied in
[
Nak21
]
.

Remark 0.4.9
.

The implications (
0.4.61
) and (
0.4.62
)
were proved for
\nu=\mathrm{id}

by
[
CHL20
, Lemma 2.4 & Theorem 2.5]

and for general
\nu
by
[
Nak21
, Theorem 5.2]
.
However, the proofs therein depend on the Laurent positivity in
Theorem
0.1.37
, which we temporarily dismiss.
We come back to the point later
in Section
0.7.1
.

0.5
Scattering diagrams

In this section we present a precise formulation of scattering diagrams
following
[
GHK15
,
GHKK18
]
.
To do this, we set the following guideline.

(a).

Since in our approach we do not need
scattering diagrams with
frozen variables

(corresponding to
coefficients of geometrical type
in
[
FZ07
]
),
we skip the relevant formulation.
This makes a considerable simplification
of the formulation.

(b).

We omit the description of the underlying Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and the group

G
in
[
GHKK18
]
for scattering diagrams.
They are crucial for the construction of
scattering diagrams, but we can skip them for our purpose.

(c).

We clarify and establish the connection between cluster patterns
and scattering diagrams in detail by step-by-step examples.

(d).

We mostly follow the notations and the conventions in
[
GHKK18
]
.
However, we take the
transpose
of the skew-symmetric forms therein
to match the convention of the exchange matrices in
[
FZ07
]
.

0.5.1
Fixed data and seed

Definition 0.5.1

(Fixed data)
.

A
fixed data

\Gamma
consists of the following:

•

A lattice
N\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{n}
of rank
n
with
a skew-symmetric bilinear form

\displaystyle\{\cdot,\cdot\}:N\times N\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}.

(0.5.1)

•

A sublattice
N^{\circ}\subset N
of finite index (equivalently, of rank
n
) such that

\displaystyle\{N^{\circ},N\}\subset\mathbb{Z}.

(0.5.2)

•

Positive integers
\delta_{1}
, …,
\delta_{n}

such that
there is a basis
(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
of
N
,
where

(\delta_{1}e_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}e_{n})
is a basis of
N^{\circ}
.

•

M=\mathrm{Hom}(N,\mathbb{Z})
,
M^{\circ}=\mathrm{Hom}(N^{\circ},\mathbb{Z})
,

M_{\mathbb{R}}=M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
,
and we regard

\displaystyle M\subset M^{\circ}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}.

(0.5.3)

For a given
N^{\circ}\subset N
of finite index,
the above
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}
always exist; for example, take the
elementary divisors of the embedding
N^{\circ}\hookrightarrow N
.
However, other choices are equally good.

Let
\langle n,m\rangle:N^{\circ}\times M^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be
the canonical paring.
We also write its linear extension

N\times M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

by the same symbol
\langle n,m\rangle
.
(Though it is a little confusing, the symbol
n
is used to denote both the rank and an element in
N
.)

Remark 0.5.2
.

(a).
In (
0.5.2
) we already took the transpose of the form in
[
GHKK18
]

as mentioned in the above guideline (d).

(b).
In
[
GHKK18
]

it is assumed that
the numbers
\delta_{1}
, …,
\delta_{n}
are coprime.
Here we do not require it.
See also Remark
0.5.29
.

Definition 0.5.3

(Seed)
.

A
seed

\mathfrak{s}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
for a fixed data
\Gamma

is a basis of
N
such that

(\delta_{1}e_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}e_{n})
is a basis of
N^{\circ}
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
be a seed for a fixed data
\Gamma
,
and
let
(e^{*}_{1},\dots,e^{*}_{n})
be the dual basis of
M
.
Let
f_{i}=\delta_{i}^{-1}e_{i}^{*}
(
i=1,\dots,n
).
Then,

(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
is a basis of
M^{\circ}
.

Accordingly,
we define isomorphisms, all of which are denoted by the same symbol for simplicity,

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\phi_{\mathfrak{s}}:&N&\rightarrow&\mathbb{Z}^{n},\\
&e_{i}&\mapsto&\mathbf{e}_{i},\end{matrix}

(0.5.4)

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\phi_{\mathfrak{s}}:&M^{\circ}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{Z}^{n},&M_{\mathbb{R}}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
&f_{i}&\mapsto&\mathbf{e}_{i}.\end{matrix}

(0.5.5)

The identifications under the above isomorphisms are written as,

N\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,

M^{\circ}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
respectively.

Let us identify

N\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
M^{\circ}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
For
n\in N^{\circ}\subset N
and
m\in M^{\circ}
,
let
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be the corresponding vectors.
Then,
the canonical paring
\langle n,m\rangle

for
N^{\circ}\times M^{\circ}
is given by

\displaystyle\langle n,m\rangle=(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m})_{D}:=\mathbf{n}^{T}D\mathbf{m},\quad D=\mathrm{diag}(\delta_{1}^{-1},\dots,\delta_{n}^{-1}).

(0.5.6)

This symmetric bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{D}
agrees with the one
in (
0.2.45
).
We also regard it
as a pairing

N\times M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, depending on the context.

Example 0.5.4
.

A seed
\mathfrak{s}
for a fixed data
\Gamma
is identified
with a seed
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
(in the sense of Definition
0.1.3
) in the following way:
Let
x^{m}
(
m\in M^{\circ})
and
y^{n}
(
n\in N)
be the monomial expressions
with formal symbols
x
and
y

such that
x^{m+m^{\prime}}=x^{m}x^{m^{\prime}}
,
y^{n+n^{\prime}}=y^{n}y^{n^{\prime}}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{X}
and
\mathcal{F}_{Y}
be the rational function fields generated by
all formal exponentials

x^{m}
and
y^{n}
, respectively.
Then, a seed
(\mathbf{x}_{\mathfrak{s}},\mathbf{y}_{\mathfrak{s}},B_{\mathfrak{s}})=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
in
(\mathcal{F}_{X},\mathcal{F}_{Y})
is defined by

\displaystyle x_{i}=x^{f_{i}},\quad y_{i}=y^{e_{i}},\quad b_{ij}=\{\delta_{i}e_{i},e_{j}\}.

(0.5.7)

In particular,
the matrix
D
in (
0.5.6
)
is a (left) skew-symmetrizer of
B=(b_{ij})
.
Moreover,
under the identification
N\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, the skew-symmetric bilinear form
in (
0.5.1
) is given by

\displaystyle\{n,n^{\prime}\}=\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{DB}:=\mathbf{n}^{T}DB\mathbf{n}^{\prime}.

(0.5.8)

This skew-symmetric bilinear form agrees with the one
in (
0.4.24
).
This is the reason why we take
D
as
(
0.2.44
) throughout.
For a given seed
\mathfrak{s}
(the
initial seed
) for a fixed data
\Gamma

and a given initial vertex
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
we associate a cluster pattern

\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}}

whose initial seed
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
is given
by the seed
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
in (
0.5.7
).
Below we consider the

C
-,
G
-, and
F
-patterns
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}}
with the initial vertex
t_{0}

unless otherwise mentioned.

Remark 0.5.5
.

In the scattering diagram formalism,
it is natural to consider the diagonal matrix
with positive integer diagonals

\Delta=\mathrm{diag}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n})

and the skew-symmetric rational matrix

\Omega=(\omega_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}
,

\omega_{ij}=\{e_{i},e_{j}\}
.
Then,
the matrices in (
0.5.6
)–(
0.5.8
)
are given by

\displaystyle D=\Delta^{-1},\quad B=\Delta\Omega,\quad DB=\Omega.

(0.5.9)

0.5.2
Walls

Let us introduce the homomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}p^{*}:&N&\rightarrow&M^{\circ}\\
&n&\mapsto&\{\cdot,n\}\end{matrix}.

(0.5.10)

Example 0.5.6

(continued)
.

Using the matrix
B
in
(
0.5.7
),
we have

\displaystyle p^{*}(e_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{ij}f_{i}.

(0.5.11)

Therefore,
under our identification
N\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
M^{\circ}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
the matrix representation of
p^{*}
is just given by
B
.
Also, again by (
0.5.7
) and (
0.5.11
),

\displaystyle x^{p^{*}(e_{i})}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{b_{ji}}=\hat{y}_{i}.

(0.5.12)

From now on we assume the following condition for a fixed data
\Gamma
:

Assumption 0.5.7

(Injectivity Assumption)
.

The map
p^{*}
is injective.

Example 0.5.8

(continued.)
.

Injectivity Assumption
is equivalent to assume that
the skew-symmetric form
\{\cdot,\cdot\}

in (
0.5.1
) is nondegenerate.
Thus,

B
in (
0.5.7
) is nonsingular,
and
\hat{y}_{1}
, …,
\hat{y}_{n}

in (
0.5.12
)
are algebraically independent.

Define

\displaystyle N^{+}=N_{\mathfrak{s}}^{+}:=\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}e_{i}\biggm|a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\neq 0\biggr\},

(0.5.13)

\displaystyle P=P_{\mathfrak{s}}:=\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}p^{*}(e_{i})\biggm|a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\biggr\}=p^{*}(N^{+})\sqcup\{0\}\subset M^{\circ}.

(0.5.14)

We view
P
as a monoid.

Example 0.5.9

(continued.)
.

Under the identification
M^{\circ}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
we have

\displaystyle P=\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathbf{b}_{i}\biggm|a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\biggr\},

(0.5.15)

where
\mathbf{b}_{i}
is the
i
th column of
B
.

Let
\mathbbm{k}
be any field of characteristic zero,
e.g.,
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{Q}
.
Let
\mathbbm{k}[P]
be the monoid algebra of
P
over
\mathbbm{k}
.
Let
J
be the maximal ideal of
\mathbbm{k}[P]

generated by
P\setminus\{0\}
,
and let

\displaystyle\mathbbm{k}[[P]]=\lim_{\longleftarrow}\mathbbm{k}[P]/J^{\ell}

(0.5.16)

be the completion with respect to
J
.
Using the formal exponentials
x^{m}
(
m\in P
),
an element of
\mathbbm{k}[P]
(resp.
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
) is written as a polynomial
(resp. a formal power series) in
x
as

\displaystyle f=\sum_{m\in P}c_{m}x^{m},\quad(c_{m}\in\mathbbm{k}).

(0.5.17)

Remark 0.5.10
.

In
[
GHKK18
]
the notation
z^{m}
is used.
We use
x^{m}
because of the forthcoming identification with
x
-variables.
This notation is also compatible with the one in Example
0.5.4
.

Example 0.5.11

(continued.)
.

Under the identification (
0.5.12
),

\mathbbm{k}[P]
is the polynomial algebra
\mathbbm{k}[\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}]
.
Also,

\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
is the algebra of formal power series
\mathbbm{k}[[\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}]]
.

Definition 0.5.12

(Normalized automorphism)
.

For
n\in N^{+}
and
f=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}x^{kp^{*}(n)}\in\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
,
we define an automorphism
\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}
of
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}(x^{m})=x^{m}f^{\langle\delta(n)n,m\rangle},\quad(m\in P),

(0.5.18)

where
\delta(n)
is the smallest positive rational number
such that
\delta(n)n\in N^{\circ}
.
We call
\delta(n)
the
normalization factor
of
n

and
\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}
the
normalized automorphism
by
n
and
f
.

Example 0.5.13

(continued.)
.

Let us identify
N\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
M^{\circ}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

(a). Let
n=\mathbf{e}_{i}
, where
p^{*}(n)=B\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathbf{b}_{i}
.
Then, we have
\delta(\mathbf{e}_{i})=\delta_{i}
.
Let
f=1+x^{\mathbf{b}_{i}}=1+\hat{y}_{i}
.
Then, the automorphism
\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}(x^{\mathbf{m}})=x^{\mathbf{m}}(1+\hat{y}_{i})^{(\delta_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{m})_{D}}.\quad(\mathbf{m}\in P\subset M^{\circ}).

(0.5.19)

(b).
Let
n=\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}
,
where
p^{*}(n)=B\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}=\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{+}
.
We first claim that

\displaystyle\delta(\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+})=\delta_{i}.

(0.5.20)

We prove it by the induction on
t
along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
starting from
t_{0}
.
For
t=t_{0}
, this is the case (a) above.
Assume that (
0.5.20
) holds for some
t
.
Let
t^{\prime}
be the vertex that is
k
-adjacent to
t
.
By (
0.2.6
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{t^{\prime}}D^{-1}&=C_{t}(J_{k}+[\varepsilon_{k;t}B_{t}]_{+}^{k\bullet})D^{-1}\\
&=C_{t}D^{-1}(J_{k}+[-\varepsilon_{k;t}(B_{t})^{T}]_{+}^{k\bullet}),\end{split}

(0.5.21)

where (
0.1.13
) is used for the last equality.
Since
|J_{k}+[-\varepsilon_{k;t}(B_{t})^{T}]_{+}^{k\bullet}|=-1
by Lemma

0.1.10
, each column vector
\delta_{i}\mathbf{c}_{i;t^{\prime}}^{+}
belongs to
N_{0}
.
Furthermore,
\delta_{i}
is the smallest
positive rational number satisfying this property
because
\delta_{1}\mathbf{c}_{1;t^{\prime}}^{+}
, …,
\delta_{n}\mathbf{c}_{n;t^{\prime}}^{+}
are a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
of
N^{\circ}
.
This proves (
0.5.20
).
Now, let
f=1+x^{\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{+}}=1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}(x^{\mathbf{m}})=x^{\mathbf{m}}(1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}})^{(\delta_{i}\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+},\mathbf{m})_{D}},\quad(\mathbf{m}\in P\subset M^{\circ}).

(0.5.22)

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}^{-\varepsilon_{i;t}}=\mathfrak{q}_{i;t},

(0.5.23)

where
\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}^{-1}
is the inverse of
\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}
,
and
\mathfrak{q}_{i;t}
is the one in (
0.4.26
)
viewed as
an automorphism of

\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
.
The factor
-\varepsilon_{i;t}
in (
0.5.23
)
is important for the integrity of the whole picture.

For any
n\in N^{+}
, we define
a hyperplane in
M_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle n^{\perp}:=\{m\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle n,m\rangle=0\}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}.

(0.5.24)

We say that
n\in N^{+}
is
primitive
if
there is no pair
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>1}
and
n^{\prime}\in N^{+}

such that
n=jn^{\prime}
.
Let
N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
be the set of all primitive elements
in
N
.

Now we introduce the fundamental ingredient of scattering diagrams.

Definition 0.5.14

(Wall)
.

A
wall
for a seed
\mathfrak{s}

is a triplet
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},f)_{n}
,
where

•

n\in N^{+}_{\rm pr}
,

•

\mathfrak{d}\subset n^{\perp}
is a (not necessarily strongly convex) cone in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
of codimension one,

•

f
is an element of
\mathbbm{k}[[x^{p^{*}(n)}]]\subset\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
with
constant term 1,

\displaystyle f=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}x^{kp^{*}(n)}.

(0.5.25)

The cone
\mathfrak{d}
is called the
support
of a wall.
No names were explicitly given for
f
and
n
in
[
GHKK18
]
.
Since this is inconvenient, here we call them the
wall function

and the
normal vector
of a wall.
We say that a wall
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},f)_{n}
is
incoming
if

\displaystyle p^{*}(n)\in\mathfrak{d}.

(0.5.26)

Otherwise, we say it is
outgoing
.

Remark 0.5.15
.

Since
\{n,n\}=0
by skew-symmetry, we have

\displaystyle p^{*}(n)\in n^{\perp}.

(0.5.27)

Example 0.5.16

(continued)
.

Let us identify
N\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
M^{\circ}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

(a).
Let
n=\mathbf{e}_{i}
,
\mathfrak{d}=\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\perp}
, and
f=1+\hat{y}_{i}
.
Then,
\mathbf{b}_{i}=B\mathbf{e}_{i}\in\mathfrak{d}
, and
\mathbf{w}_{i;t_{0}}=(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\perp},1+\hat{y}_{i})_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
is an
incoming
wall.

(b).
Let
n=\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}=\varepsilon_{i;t}\mathbf{c}_{i;t}
,

\mathfrak{d}=\sigma_{i}(G_{t})
, and
f=1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}}
.
Firstly,
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}
is primitive due to the unimodularity
(
0.2.3
) of
C
-matrices.
Secondly,
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}
is a normal vector of
\sigma_{i}(G_{t})

thanks to the duality in Proposition
0.2.16
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{w}_{i;t}=(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}),1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}})_{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}}
is a wall.
Moreover, due to
Proposition
0.2.32
,
if
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}\neq\mathbf{e}_{\ell}
for any
\ell
, it is an
outgoing
wall.
Note that, if
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
are
k
-adjacent,
then two walls
\mathbf{w}_{k;t}
and
\mathbf{w}_{k;t^{\prime}}

are identical.

0.5.3
Scattering diagrams

Let
\widehat{J}
be the maximal ideal of
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]

generated by
P\setminus\{0\}
.
We say that
f=1+\sum_{m\in P,m\neq 0}c_{m}x^{m}\in\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
is
trivial modulo
\widehat{J}^{\ell}
if its image in
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]/\widehat{J}^{\ell}

is 1.

Example 0.5.17

(continued)
.

Under the identification
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]=\mathbbm{k}[[\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}]]
,

f
is trivial modulo
\widehat{J}^{\ell}
if and only if

f-1
contains no monomial in
\hat{y}_{1}
, …,
\hat{y}_{n}
whose total degree is
less than
\ell
.

Now we can give the definition of a scattering diagram.

Definition 0.5.18

(Scattering diagram)
.

A
scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}
for
a seed
\mathfrak{s}
is a collection
of walls
\{\mathbf{w}_{\lambda}=(\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},f_{{\lambda}})_{n_{\lambda}}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}

with respect to
a seed
\mathfrak{s}
,
where
\Lambda
is a finite or countably infinite index set,
satisfying the following
finiteness condition
:

•

For each positive integer
\ell
, there are only finitely many
walls whose wall functions are not trivial modulo
\widehat{J}^{\ell}
.

Definition 0.5.19

(Support and singular locus)
.

For a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}=\{(\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},f_{{\lambda}})_{n_{\lambda}}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
,
the
support
and the
singular locus
of
\mathfrak{D}
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},

(0.5.28)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\partial\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cup\bigcup_{\scriptstyle\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda\atop\scriptstyle\dim\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda^{\prime}}=n-2}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda^{\prime}}.

(0.5.29)

Example 0.5.20

(continued)
.

Let us identify
N\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
M^{\circ}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
For the cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}}
,
we define a set of walls

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}}):=\{\mathbf{w}_{i;t}=(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}),1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}})_{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}}\mid i=1,\dots,n;\,t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}\},

(0.5.30)

where we discard all duplicate walls so that
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
consists
of mutually distinct walls.
We may take a different label
(i;t)
freely for identical walls.
The collection of walls
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})

is not necessarily a scattering diagram, because the finiteness condition
is not guaranteed.
In fact, there are examples where
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})

does not satisfy the finiteness condition.
See Remark
0.6.15
.

0.5.4
Wall-crossing automorphisms

Definition 0.5.21

(Admissible curve)
.

A curve
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}

is
admissible
for a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

if it satisfies the following properties:

•

\gamma
does not intersect
\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
.

•

The end points of
\gamma
are in
M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
.

•

It is a smooth curve, and it intersects
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
transversally.

Definition 0.5.22

(Wall-crossing automorphism)
.

For any scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
and any admissible curve
\gamma
,
we define the associated
wall-crossing automorphism along
\gamma
,
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbbm{k}[[P]])
as follows:
For each degree
\ell
, take all walls whose wall functions are not trivial modulo
\widehat{J}^{\ell}
.
There are only finitely many such walls.
Let
(\mathfrak{d}_{s},f_{s})_{n_{s}}
(
s=1,\dots,r
)
be the walls among them which
\gamma
intersects.
We may assume that the curve
\gamma(t)
intersects
\mathfrak{d}_{s}
at time
t=t_{s}

such that

\displaystyle 0<t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\dots\leq t_{r}<1.

(0.5.31)

For each
s
, we define the
intersection sign

\displaystyle\epsilon_{s}=\begin{cases}1&\langle n_{s},\gamma^{\prime}(t_{s})\rangle<0,\\
-1&\langle n_{s},\gamma^{\prime}(t_{s})\rangle>0,\\
\end{cases}

(0.5.32)

where
\gamma^{\prime}(t)
is the velocity vector of
\gamma(t)
at
t
.
Then, we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{p}_{n_{r},f_{r}}^{\epsilon_{r}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{p}_{n_{2},f_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}\circ\mathfrak{p}_{n_{1},f_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}},

(0.5.33)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}^{\ell}.

(0.5.34)

Note that
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
only depends on the homotopy class
of
\gamma
in
M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
.

Remark 0.5.23
.

In
[
GHKK18
]
,

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
is called a
path-ordered product
of
automorphisms
\mathfrak{p}_{n_{s},f_{s}}^{\epsilon_{s}}
.

Example 0.5.24

(continued)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
be the collection of walls
in Example
0.5.20
.
Even though
the finiteness condition
does not hold in general,
the wall-crossing automorphism is still well-defined
for a curve crossing only finitely many walls.
Let
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
, …,
t_{r+1}=t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be a sequence of vertices such that
they are sequentially adjacent with edges labeled by
k_{0}
,
k_{1}
,…,
k_{r}
.
Accordingly, we consider an admissible curve
\gamma_{t}^{t_{0}}
further satisfying
the following conditions:

•

It is in the interior of the support
|\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})|

of the
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
.

•

It starts in
\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t_{r+1}})
.

•

It sequentially intersects walls

\displaystyle(\sigma_{k_{r}}(G_{t_{r}}),1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{k_{r};t_{r}}^{+}})_{\mathbf{c}_{k_{r};t_{r}}^{+}},\dots,(\sigma_{k_{0}}(G_{t_{0}}),1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{k_{0};t_{0}}^{+}})_{\mathbf{c}_{k_{0};t_{0}}^{+}}.

(0.5.35)

•

It ends in
\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t_{0}})
.

By Proposition
0.2.16
,
for each
s=0,\dots,r
,
the
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{k_{s};t_{s}}

is inward for the cone
\sigma_{k_{s}}(G_{t_{s}})
.
Thus,
when
\gamma_{t}^{t_{0}}
crosses

\sigma_{k_{s}}(G_{t_{s}})
to
get into

\sigma(G_{t_{s}})
,
the velocity vector
\gamma^{\prime}
and the
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{k_{s};t_{s}}

are always in the
same
direction with respect to
\sigma_{k_{s}}(G_{t_{s}})
.
Since
the normal vector of the wall is
n_{s}=\varepsilon_{k_{s};t_{s}}\mathbf{c}_{k_{s};t_{s}}
,
the factor in (
0.5.32
)
is given by
-\varepsilon_{k_{s};t_{s}}
.
Then, by (
0.4.40
) and (
0.5.23
),
the wall-crossing automorphism along
\gamma_{t}^{t_{0}}
is
identified with
\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}
in (
0.4.40
)
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{t}^{t_{0}}:=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{t}^{t_{0}},\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})}&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{c}_{k_{0};t_{0}}^{+},1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{k_{0};t_{0}}^{+}}}^{-\varepsilon_{k_{0},t_{0}}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{c}_{k_{r};t_{r}}^{+},1+\hat{y}^{\mathbf{c}_{k_{r};t_{r}}^{+}}}^{-\varepsilon_{k_{r},t_{r}}}\\
&=\mathfrak{q}_{k_{0};t_{0}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{q}_{k_{r};t_{r}}\\
&=\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}.\end{split}

(0.5.36)

Note that the factor
-\varepsilon_{i;t}
in (
0.5.23
) is absorbed
in coordination with the factor in (
0.5.32
).

Definition 0.5.25

(Equivalence/Consistency)
.

•

Two scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
with a common initial seed
\mathfrak{s}
are

equivalent
if, for any curve
\gamma
that is admissible for both
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
, the equality

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}}
holds.

•

A scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is
consistent
if for any admissible curve
\gamma
,
the associated wall-crossing automorphism
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
depends
only on
the end points of
\gamma
.

Remark 0.5.26
.

For a given scattering diagram,
one obtains equivalent scattering diagrams
by repeating the following procedures:

•

To join a pair of walls

(\mathfrak{d}_{1},f)_{n}
and
(\mathfrak{d}_{2},f)_{n}

intersecting in their common face
\mathfrak{d}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{2}
of codimension two
into a wall
(\mathfrak{d}_{1}\cup\mathfrak{d}_{2},f)_{n}
,
or to split it conversely.

•

To join a pair of walls
(\mathfrak{d},f_{1})_{n}
and
(\mathfrak{d},f_{2})_{n}
into a wall
(\mathfrak{d},f_{1}f_{2})_{n}
,
or to split it conversely. One may join infinitely many walls
with a common support as well
by taking the infinite product of wall functions, or split it conversely.

•

To add or to remove a trivial wall
(\mathfrak{d},1)
.

Applying these operations,
and especially removing all trivial walls,
one can obtain a scattering diagram
whose support is minimal among other equivalent diagrams.

The following is the first fundamental theorem
on scattering diagrams
in view of the application to cluster algebra theory.

Theorem 0.5.27

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorems 1.12 &1.28]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be any initial seed for
\Gamma
.
Then, there exists a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

satisfying the following properties:

(a).

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in},\mathfrak{s}}:=\{(e_{i}^{\perp},1+\hat{y}_{i})_{e_{i}}\mid i=1,\dots,n\}\subset\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.

(b).

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in},\mathfrak{s}}
consists only of outgoing walls, and their normal vectors are not equal to
e_{i}
for any
i
.

(c).

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is consistent.

Moreover, such a scattering diagram is unique up to the equivalence.

We call
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
a
cluster scattering diagram
(CSD for short) for
\mathfrak{s}
.

Remark 0.5.28
.

The second statement of (b) is taken from

[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.28, Remark 1.29]
,
while
all other statements are from
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.12]
.

From now on we assume that
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
has the minimal support
among other equivalent diagrams.
Under the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we define
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}}):=\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Now we see that the properties of the support diagram
\mathfrak{S}(B_{t_{0}})
given in
Proposition
0.3.1
follow from Definition
0.5.14
and
Theorem
0.5.27
.

Remark 0.5.29
.

There is some redundancy of fixed data,
because we do not require the coprimeness of

\delta_{1}
, …,
\delta_{n}
here.
Suppose that the exchange matrix
B

in (
0.5.7
) is indecomposable.
For a fixed data
\Gamma
,
another fixed data
\Gamma^{\prime}
is called a
rescaling
of
\Gamma

if there is some rational number
\lambda>0
such that
the following relations hold:

\displaystyle N^{\prime}

\displaystyle=N,

(0.5.37)

\displaystyle\{n_{1},n_{2}\}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lambda\{n_{1},n_{2}\},

(0.5.38)

\displaystyle(N^{\circ})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}N^{\circ},

(0.5.39)

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\delta_{i},

(0.5.40)

\displaystyle(M^{\circ})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lambda M^{\circ}.

(0.5.41)

For such a pair,
a seed
\mathfrak{s}
for
\Gamma
is also regarded as a seed for
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Let
\phi_{\lambda}:\mathbbm{k}[[P]]\rightarrow{\mathbbm{k}[[P^{\prime}]]}
,
x^{m}\mapsto x^{\lambda m}

be the induced algebra isomorphism.
Then, we have, for
m\in P
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{n,\phi_{\lambda}(f)}\circ\phi_{\lambda})(x^{m})&=\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{n,\phi_{\lambda}(f)}(x^{\lambda m})=x^{\lambda m}\phi_{\lambda}(f)^{\langle\delta^{\prime}_{0}(n)n,\lambda m\rangle}\\
&=x^{\lambda m}\phi_{\lambda}(f)^{\langle\delta(n)n,m\rangle}=(\phi_{\lambda}\circ\mathfrak{p}_{n,f})(x^{m}).\end{split}

(0.5.42)

Thus, cluster scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{s}}

for
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
, respectively, are identified under the above correspondence.
Moreover,
for
x_{i}
,
y_{i}
,
b_{ij}

and

x^{\prime}_{i}
,
y^{\prime}_{i}
,
b^{\prime}_{ij}
in
(
0.5.7
) for
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
, respectively,
the following relations hold:

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}^{\lambda},\quad y^{\prime}_{i}=y_{i},\quad b^{\prime}_{ij}=b_{ij}.

(0.5.43)

In particular, we see that a cluster scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
depends only on
the matrix
B=(b_{ij})
as expected.
In general, when
B
is not indecomposable, we may apply the above rescaling to
each indecomposable component of
B
.

0.5.5
Rank 2 examples of consistent scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

Let us present the scattering diagrams

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
in Theorem
0.5.27

in the rank 2 case
under the identification
N\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
following
[
GHKK18
]
.
Using the equivalence in Remark
0.5.29
,
we may assume that
\{e_{2},e_{1}\}=1
without loosing generality.
We use the same convention and notations in Section
0.2.5
.

(I). Finite type.
The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is equivalent to

\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
constructed in
Example
0.5.20
.
In other words,
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
itself is a consistent
scattering diagram.

(a). Type
A_{2}
. Let

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}=1;\ \hat{y}_{1}=x_{2},\ \hat{y}_{2}=x_{1}^{-1}.

(0.5.44)

The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})

consists of five distinct walls of the form in (
0.5.30
).
They are rearranged up to the equivalence of scattering diagrams as
two incoming walls and one outgoing walls as

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},1+\hat{y}_{1})_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\ (\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},1+\hat{y}_{2})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},\ (\sigma((1,-1)),1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2})_{(1,1)}.

(0.5.45)

This is the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
which is
depicted in Figure
3
.
The only property to be checked is the following consistency condition

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{2},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}

(0.5.46)

for the admissible curves
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
in
Figure
3
.
One can check it easily by the direct calculation as follows:

1+\hat{y}_{1}
1+\hat{y}_{2}
1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}
\textstyle{\gamma_{1}}
\textstyle{\gamma_{2}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Figure 3:
Consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
of Type
A_{2}
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{1})&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{2},1+\hat{y}_{2}}(\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{1},1+\hat{y}_{1}}(x_{1}))\\
&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{2},1+\hat{y}_{2}}(x_{1}(1+\hat{y}_{1}))\\
&=x_{1}(1+\hat{y}_{1}(1+\hat{y}_{2}))\\
&=x_{1}(1+\hat{y}_{1}+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}).\end{split}

(0.5.47)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{2},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{1})&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{1},1+\hat{y}_{1}}(\mathfrak{p}_{(1,1),1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}}(\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{2},1+\hat{y}_{2}}(x_{1})))\\
&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{1},1+\hat{y}_{1}}(\mathfrak{p}_{(1,1),1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}}(x_{1}))\\
&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{1},1+\hat{y}_{1}}(x_{1}(1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}))\\
&=x_{1}(1+\hat{y}_{1})(1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}(1+\hat{y}_{1})^{-1})\\
&=x_{1}(1+\hat{y}_{1}+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}).\end{split}

(0.5.48)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{2})&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{2},1+\hat{y}_{2}}(\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{1},1+\hat{y}_{1}}(x_{2}))\\
&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{2},1+\hat{y}_{2}}(x_{2})\\
&=x_{2}(1+\hat{y}_{2}).\end{split}

(0.5.49)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{2},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{2})&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{1},1+\hat{y}_{1}}(\mathfrak{p}_{(1,1),1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}}(\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{2},1+\hat{y}_{2}}(x_{2})))\\
&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{1},1+\hat{y}_{1}}(\mathfrak{p}_{(1,1),1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}}(x_{2}(1+\hat{y}_{2})))\\
&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{1},1+\hat{y}_{1}}(x_{2}(1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2})(1+\hat{y}_{2}(1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2})^{-1}))\\
&=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{e}_{1},1+\hat{y}_{1}}(x_{2}(1+\hat{y}_{2}+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}))\\
&=x_{2}(1+\hat{y}_{2}(1+\hat{y}_{1})^{-1}+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}(1+\hat{y}_{1})^{-1})\\
&=x_{2}(1+\hat{y}_{2}).\end{split}

(0.5.50)

It is also instructive to give an alternative derivation of
(
0.5.46
)
in view of Proposition
0.4.8
.
Recall the action of a permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}

in (
0.4.49
)–(
0.4.53
).
We know from (
0.2.52
) that

\displaystyle G_{t_{2}}=s_{12}G_{t_{-3}},

(0.5.51)

where
s_{12}
is the transposition of 1 and 2.
Also, the following property (the
pentagon periodicity
) is the first thing we learn in cluster algebra theory.

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t_{2}}=s_{12}\mathbf{x}_{t_{-3}}.

(0.5.52)

Therefore, by Proposition
0.4.8
, we have (for
x
-variables)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{t_{2}}^{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{q}_{t_{-3}}^{t_{0}}.

(0.5.53)

Thus, by (
0.5.36
),

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{t_{2}}^{t_{0}}=\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{t_{-3}}^{t_{0}}.

(0.5.54)

Since

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}=(\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{t_{2}}^{t_{0}})^{-1}

and

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{2},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}=(\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{t_{-3}}^{t_{0}})^{-1}
,
we have
(
0.5.46
).
Moreover, it clarifies the meaning of the consistency condition
(
0.5.46
);
namely,
it guarantees the detropicalization of
x
-variables
.

(b). Type
B_{2}
.
Let

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=1,\ \delta_{2}=2;\ \hat{y}_{1}=x_{2}^{2},\ \hat{y}_{2}=x_{1}^{-1}.

(0.5.55)

The situation is the same as type
A_{2}
,
and the outgoing walls are

\displaystyle\left(\sigma((1,-2)),1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}\right)_{(1,1)},\ \left(\sigma((1,-1)),1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}^{2}\right)_{(1,2)}.

(0.5.56)

(c). Type
G_{2}
.
Let

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=1,\ \delta_{2}=3;\ \hat{y}_{1}=x_{2}^{3},\ \hat{y}_{2}=x_{1}^{-1}.

(0.5.57)

The situation is the same as type
A_{2}
,
and the outgoing walls are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(\sigma((1,-3)),1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}\right)_{(1,1)},\ \left(\sigma((1,-2)),1+\hat{y}_{1}^{2}\hat{y}_{2}^{3}\right)_{(2,3)},\\
\left(\sigma((2,-3)),1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}^{2}\right)_{(1,2)},\ \left(\sigma((1,-1)),1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}^{3}\right)_{(1,3)}.\end{split}

(0.5.58)

(II). Infinite type.
The consistent scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

is an extension of
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})

such that
the supports of additional walls are in the complement
of
|\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})|
.
In particular,
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
is a scattering diagram but it is not consistent.

(d). Type
A_{1}^{(1)}
.
Let

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}=2;\ \hat{y}_{1}=x_{2}^{2},\ \hat{y}_{2}=x_{1}^{-2}.

(0.5.59)

There is one additional wall to
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})

[
GHKK18
, Example 1.15]
, which is

\displaystyle\Biggl(\sigma((1,-1)),\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\hat{y}_{1}^{k}\hat{y}_{2}^{k}\right)^{2}\Biggr)_{(1,1)}.

(0.5.60)

(e). Type
A_{2}^{(2)}
.
Let

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=1,\ \delta_{2}=4;\ \hat{y}_{1}=x_{2}^{4},\ \hat{y}_{2}=x_{1}^{-1}.

(0.5.61)

There is one additional wall to
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})

[
Rea20a
, Theorem 3.4.]
, which is

\displaystyle\Biggl(\sigma((1,-2)),(1+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}^{2})\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\hat{y}_{1}^{k}\hat{y}_{2}^{2k}\right)^{2}\Biggr)_{(1,2)}.

(0.5.62)

(f). Non-affine type. There are infinitely many additional walls
inside the irrational cone spanned by
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}

in (
0.2.68
).
The region is informally called the
Badlands
. We may also call it the
dark side

from the viewpoint of cluster patterns.
The explicit description of walls therein is not yet known.
However, for the skew-symmetric case
\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}
,
the existence of walls for every rational slope was proved in
[
DM21
, Example 7.10]
.

0.6
Scattering diagrams and
F
-polynomials

In this section we show how
F
-polynomials and
x
-variables are related with
the consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
The Laurent positivity
follows from the positivity of theta functions in
[
GHKK18
]
.

0.6.1
Mutations of scattering diagrams

We continue to assume that a fixed data
\Gamma
satisfies Injectivity Assumption
throughout the section.

The following definition originates in
[
FG09
, §2.1]
.

Definition 0.6.1

(Seed mutation)
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
be any seed for a fixed data
\Gamma
.
For any
k=1,\dots,n
,
we define a new seed
\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{s})=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=(e_{1}^{\prime},\dots,e_{n}^{\prime})

by

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}-e_{k}&i=k,\\
e_{i}+[b_{ki}]_{+}e_{k}&i\neq k,\end{cases}\quad b_{ij}=\{\delta_{i}e_{i},e_{j}\},

(0.6.1)

which is called the
mutation
of
\mathfrak{s}
in direction
k
.

The transformation (
0.6.1
) is written by a matrix
form
P
in Lemma
0.1.10
.
Thus,
e_{1}^{\prime},\dots,e_{n}^{\prime}
are certainly a basis of
N
.
Also,
under the mutation (
0.6.1
),
The basis
\delta_{1}e_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}e_{n}
of
N^{\circ}
mutates as

\displaystyle\delta_{i}e^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}-\delta_{k}e_{k}&i=k,\\
\delta_{i}e_{i}+[-b_{ik}]_{+}\delta_{k}e_{k}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(0.6.2)

where we used the skew-symmetry
b_{ki}\delta_{i}=-b_{ik}\delta_{k}
.
Thus,
\delta_{1}e_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\delta_{n}e_{n}^{\prime}
are a basis of
N^{\circ}

by the same reason as above.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is certainly a seed in the sense of Definition

0.5.3
.

On can easily confirm that the mutation (
0.6.2
)
induces the following mutation for the basis
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
of
M^{\circ}
,
which are dual to
\delta_{1}e_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}e_{n}
:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle-f_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}[-b_{jk}]_{+}f_{j}&i=k,\\
f_{i}&i\neq k.\end{cases}

(0.6.3)

Also, we define the matrix
B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}_{ij})
,
b^{\prime}_{ij}=\{\delta_{i}e^{\prime}_{i},e^{\prime}_{j}\}

as in (
0.5.7
).
Then,
B^{\prime}
coincides with the matrix mutation of
B
in direction

k
by (
0.1.12
) with
\varepsilon=1
.

Remark 0.6.2
.

The transformation (
0.6.3
) already appeared in
(
0.2.78
)
under the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Remark 0.6.3
.

For
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{s})
,

\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})=\mathfrak{s}
does not hold,
because
b^{\prime}_{ki}=-b_{ki}
.
Namely,
\mu_{k}
is
not
an involution on the set of all seeds for a fixed data
\Gamma
.
This causes a technical problem, for example, in the proof of
the forthcoming Theorem
0.6.13
.
To remedy the situation,
we also define another mutation of a seed
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=\mu_{k}^{-}(\mathfrak{s})
by

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}-e_{k}&i=k,\\
e_{i}+[-b_{ki}]_{+}e_{k}&i\neq k.\end{cases}

(0.6.4)

Again, we define the matrix
B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}_{ij})
,
b^{\prime}_{ij}=\{\delta_{i}e^{\prime}_{i},e^{\prime}_{j}\}
.
Then,
B^{\prime}
coincides with the matrix mutation of
B
in direction

k
by (
0.1.12
) with
\varepsilon=-1
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{k}^{-}(\mu^{+}_{k}(\mathfrak{s}))=\mu_{k}^{+}(\mu_{k}^{-}(\mathfrak{s}))=\mathfrak{s},

(0.6.5)

where
\mu_{k}^{+}=\mu_{k}
. We call
\mu_{k}^{\pm}
the
signed mutations
of
\mathfrak{s}
.
They appeared, for example, in
[
IN14
]
.

For any seed
\mathfrak{s}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
and
k=1,\dots,n
,
we define a linear map

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}S_{k;\mathfrak{s}}:&M_{\mathbb{R}}&\rightarrow&M_{\mathbb{R}}\\
&m&\mapsto&m+\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle p^{*}(e_{k}).\end{matrix}

(0.6.6)
