and its dual

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}S^{*}_{k;\mathfrak{s}}:&N&\rightarrow&N\\
&n&\mapsto&n+\{\delta_{k}e_{k},n\}e_{k}.\end{matrix}

(0.6.7)

The following equality can be easily verified:

\displaystyle\langle S^{*}_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(n),S_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(x)\rangle=\langle n,x\rangle.

(0.6.8)

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{k,+}=\{m\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle e_{k},m\rangle\geq 0\},\quad\mathcal{H}_{k,-}=\{m\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle e_{k},m\rangle\leq 0\}.

(0.6.9)

Then, following
[
GHKK18
]
,
we define a piecewise-linear map

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}T_{k;\mathfrak{s}}:&M_{\mathbb{R}}&\rightarrow&M_{\mathbb{R}}\\
&m&\mapsto&\begin{cases}S_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(m)&m\in\mathcal{H}_{k,+},\\
m&m\in\mathcal{H}_{k,-}.\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(0.6.10)

Remark 0.6.4
.

Under the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it coincides with the map
T_{k;t_{0}}

in (
0.2.81
).

Definition 0.6.5

(Mutation of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
)
.

For the consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
in Theorem
0.5.27
with minimal support,
we define a collection of walls
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
,
called the
mutation of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
in direction
k
,
by collecting all walls obtained below:

(a).
For each wall
(\mathfrak{d},f)_{n}
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

other than
(e_{k}^{\perp},1+\hat{y}_{k})_{e_{k}}
,
let
\mathfrak{d}_{+}=\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,+}
,
and
\mathfrak{d}_{-}=\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,-}
.
We split the wall
(\mathfrak{d},f)_{n}
into two walls

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{d}_{+},f)_{n},\quad(\mathfrak{d}_{-},f)_{n},

(0.6.11)

where, if one of
\mathfrak{d}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{-}
is codimension more than 1,
we throw that part away.
Then,
we replace
(\mathfrak{d}_{+},f)_{n}
with a new wall

(S_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(\mathfrak{d}_{+}),S_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(f))_{S^{*}_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(n)}
,
where

S_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(f)
is the formal power series obtained from

f
by replacing each term
x^{m}
(
m\in P
) with
x^{S_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(m)}
,
while we leave
(\mathfrak{d}_{-},f)_{n}
as it is.

(b). Replace
(e_{k}^{\perp},1+\hat{y}_{k})_{e_{k}}
with
(e^{\prime}_{k}{}^{\perp},1+\hat{y}^{\prime}_{k})_{e^{\prime}_{k}}
,
where
e^{\prime}_{k}{}^{\perp}=e_{k}{}^{\perp}
and
\hat{y}^{\prime}_{k}=\hat{y}_{k}^{-1}

by (
0.6.1
).

To summarize, in both (a) and (b), each support of a wall (after splitting) is transformed by
the piecewise-linear map
T_{k;\mathfrak{s}}
.
On the other hand, each wall function in (a)
obeys the same
linear map
applied to its support,
while the case (b) is exceptional.

Remark 0.6.6
.

Here we encounter a temporal problem that the walls above are not
walls for the initial seed
\mathfrak{s}
by the following reasons:

•

The normal vectors of
walls
T_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(\mathfrak{d})
may not belong to

N^{+}=N_{\mathfrak{s}}^{+}
in general,
because the coefficients of
e_{k}
might be negative.

•

Accordingly,
the wall functions

T_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(f)
may not belong to

\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
in general.
In particular,
the wall function

1+\hat{y}^{\prime}_{k}=1+\hat{y}_{k}^{-1}
does not belong to

\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
.

These problems will be harmonically solved in the next theorem.

Here is the second fundamental theorem on scattering diagrams
in view of the application to cluster algebra theory.

Theorem 0.6.7

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.24]
)
.

The collection of walls
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})

is a scattering diagram for the seed
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{s})
.
Moreover,
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is equivalent to
the consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}
.

Let us explain how Proposition

0.3.2
follows from this theorem.

We do the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
for

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
and
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
for

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}
, respectively.
Under the change of a basis of
M_{\mathbb{R}}

in (
0.6.3
),
two identifications are related by the isomorphism

\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
in (
0.2.80
).
All relevant maps are summarized in the following
commutative diagram,
where

\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}=\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\circ T_{k;t_{0}}

as in Proposition
0.2.26
:

\textstyle{M_{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{k;\mathfrak{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}}
\textstyle{M_{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{k;t_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{n}.}

Then, for the support
\mathfrak{d}
of each wall of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
the map
\mathfrak{d}\mapsto T_{k;\mathfrak{s}}(\mathfrak{d})

in
M_{\mathbb{R}}

is identified with the map

\mathfrak{d}\mapsto\varphi_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathfrak{d})

in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Thus, by forgetting the wall functions, we obtain Proposition

0.3.2
.

0.6.2
Relation between
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

So far,
what we know about
the relation
between

\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

are
the rank 2 examples
and the relations of the supports in Theorem
0.3.3
.
Below we show that the walls of
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})

are integrated into a part of
the consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

in an essential way.
To do that,
we apply a parallel construction of the mutation of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

to
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
.

Throughout this subsection, we omit the normal vector
n\in N^{+}
of a wall

(\mathfrak{d},f)_{n}
for simplicity, because it is recovered from
\mathfrak{d}

or
f
without ambiguity.

For
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
and
k=1,\dots,n
,
we define a linear map

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}S_{k;t_{0}}:&\mathbb{R}^{n}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
&\mathbf{v}&\mapsto&(I+(B_{t_{0}})^{\bullet k})\mathbf{v},\end{matrix}

(0.6.16)

which correspond to the linear map
S_{k;\mathfrak{s}}
in (
0.6.6
)
under the identification

M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let

\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,+}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,-}

be the ones in (
0.2.70
).
Then,
the piecewise-linear map (
0.2.81
),
which corresponds to (
0.6.10
),
is written as

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}T_{k;t_{0}}:&{\mathbb{R}}^{n}&\rightarrow&{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\\
&\mathbf{v}&\mapsto&\begin{cases}S_{k;t_{0}}(\mathbf{v})&\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,+},\\
\mathbf{v}&\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,-}.\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(0.6.17)

Let us explicitly denote the dependence of
\hat{y}
-variables
to the initial vertex
t_{0}
as

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{v}}:=x^{B_{t_{0}}\mathbf{v}}.

(0.6.18)

Definition 0.6.8

(Mutation of
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
)
.

For the collection of walls
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
,
let

T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}}))
be the collection of walls

T_{k}(\mathfrak{d},f)

obtained from each wall
(\mathfrak{d},f)
of

\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})

as below.

(a). For each wall
(\mathfrak{d},f)=(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{0}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}})

such that
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}\neq\mathbf{e}_{k}
,
we define

\displaystyle T_{k}(\mathfrak{d},f)=\begin{cases}(S_{k;t_{0}}(\mathfrak{d}),S_{k;t_{0}}(f))&\mathfrak{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,+},\\
(\mathfrak{d},f)&\mathfrak{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,-},\end{cases}

(0.6.19)

where

\displaystyle S_{k;t_{0}}(1+\hat{y}_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}})=S_{k;t_{0}}(1+x^{\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}}):=1+x^{S_{k;t_{0}}(\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+})}.

(0.6.20)

Note that by Proposition
0.2.20

either
\mathfrak{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,+}
or
\mathfrak{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k,-}

occurs.
Thus,
we do not have to divide a wall.

(b). For each wall

(\mathfrak{d},f)=(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{0}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{e}_{k}})

we define

\displaystyle T_{k}(\mathfrak{d},f)=(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{0}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{0}}^{-\mathbf{e}_{k}}).

(0.6.21)

To summarize, in both (a) and (b) each support
\mathfrak{d}
is transformed by
the piecewise-linear map
T_{k;t_{0}}
.
On the other hand, each wall function
f
in (a)
obeys the same
linear map
applied to its support,
while the case (b) is exceptional.

Remark 0.6.9
.

The same remark as Remark
0.6.6
is applied to walls in the above.

Let
t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be the vertex that is

k
-adjacent to the initial vertex
t_{0}
.
Let
\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
be the linear map in (
0.2.80
).
For any wall

(\mathfrak{d},f)\in T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}}))
,
we define a new wall by

\displaystyle\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathfrak{d},f=1+x^{\mathbf{v}}):=(\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathfrak{d}),1+x^{\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathbf{v})}).

(0.6.22)

We have a parallel result to Theorem
0.6.7
.
Also, this is an upgrade of
Proposition
0.2.22
including wall functions.

Proposition 0.6.10
.

The walls
\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(\mathfrak{d},f)

in (
0.6.22
)
are walls of
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},t_{1}})

with
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{s})
.
Moreover, it induces the following bijections of walls:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})&\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{T_{k}}}&T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}}))&\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}}}&\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},t_{1}})\\
(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{0}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}})&\mapsto&T_{k}(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{0}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}})&\mapsto&(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{1}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{1}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{1}+}}),\end{matrix}

(0.6.23)

where

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}}=x^{\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}},\quad\hat{y}_{t_{1}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{1}+}}=x^{\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{1}+}}.

(0.6.24)

Proof.

We only need to show the correspondence
by
\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
in (
0.6.23
).
First we prove the claim for the supports.
Under the map
\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\circ T_{k}
,
the support
\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})

maps to
\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(T_{k;t_{0}}(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{0}}))
.
By Propositions
0.2.22
and
0.2.26
,
it equals to
\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{1}})
.

Next
we prove the claim for the wall functions.
Let us consider the case (a) in Definition
0.6.8
,
where
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}\neq\pm\mathbf{e}_{k}
is assumed.
Under
the map
\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\circ T_{k}
,
the
\hat{c}^{+}
-vector
\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}=\varepsilon_{i;t}^{t_{0}}\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}

obeys the same linear transformations
as its support
\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})
.
Therefore, by comparing (
0.2.20
) and (
0.2.37
),
its image
equals to

\varepsilon_{i;t}^{t_{0}}\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{1}}
.
Now we need to compare
\varepsilon_{i;t}^{t_{0}}

and
\varepsilon_{i;t}^{t_{1}}
.
Here we repeat a similar argument
after (
0.2.27
) in the proof of Proposition

0.2.7
.
By (
0.2.19
),

C_{t}^{t_{0}}
and
C_{t}^{t_{1}}
differ only in their
k
th rows.
Since we assume
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}\neq\pm\mathbf{e}_{k}
,
there is at least one nonzero element in the
i
th column
of
C_{t}^{t_{0}}
other than at the
k
-th row.
Thus, we have

\varepsilon_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\varepsilon_{i;t}^{t_{1}}
,
and we conclude that

\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}

maps to
\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{1}+}
as desired.
Now we consider the case (b) in Definition
0.6.8
,
where
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\pm\mathbf{e}_{k}
is assumed.
It is enough to prove the equality

\displaystyle\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(-\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+})=\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i;t}^{t_{1}+}.

(0.6.25)

Again by (
0.2.19
),
we have
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{1}}=\mp\mathbf{e}_{k}
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}=\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{1}+}=\mathbf{e}_{k}
.
Thus, the equality (
0.6.25
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(-\mathbf{b}_{k;t_{0}})=\mathbf{b}_{k;t_{1}},

(0.6.26)

which can be proved as

\displaystyle\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(-\mathbf{b}_{k;t_{0}})=-(J_{k}+[-B_{t_{0}}]_{+}^{\bullet k})\mathbf{b}_{k;t_{0}}=-\mathbf{b}_{k;t_{0}}=\mathbf{b}_{k;t_{1}}.

(0.6.27)

(This proof also explains the necessity of the exception in the case (b)
in view of the exceptional change of tropical signs

\varepsilon_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=-\varepsilon_{i;t}^{t_{1}}
therein.)
∎

Example 0.6.11

(cf. Example
0.2.24
)
.

Let us clarify
Definition
0.6.8

and
Proposition

0.6.10
explicitly for type
A_{2}

based on the convention in Section
0.2.5
.
The relevant data are as follows:

\displaystyle B_{t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad B_{t_{1}}=B_{t_{-1}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},

(0.6.28)

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{1;t_{0}}=x_{2},\ \hat{y}_{2;t_{0}}=x_{1}^{-1};\ \hat{y}_{1;t_{1}}=\hat{y}_{1;t_{-1}}=x_{2}^{-1},\ \hat{y}_{2;t_{1}}=\hat{y}_{2;t_{-1}}=x_{1},

(0.6.29)

\displaystyle S_{1;t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad S_{2;t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

(0.6.30)

\displaystyle\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\eta_{t_{0}}^{t_{-1}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(0.6.31)

The results are depicted in Figure
4
,
where
\dot{G}_{t_{0}}^{t}:=T_{k;t_{0}}({G}_{t_{0}}^{t})
therein.
See also Figure

.

Figure 4:
Mutations of
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
for type
A_{2}
.

To describe the relation between
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
we introduce the following notion.

Definition 0.6.12
.

For any wall
(\mathfrak{d},f)
and a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
for a common seed
\mathfrak{s}
,
we say that
(\mathfrak{d},f)
is
essential to
\mathfrak{D}

if there is a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
that is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}

satisfying
the following condition:

•

The wall
(\mathfrak{d},f)
is a wall of
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
;
moreover, for any wall
(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
of
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}

other than
(\mathfrak{d},f)
, it holds that

\dim\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}<n-1
.

Combining Theorem
0.6.7
and
Proposition
0.6.10
,
we obtain the following relation
between
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
(The claim (b) was already given in Theorem
0.3.3
(a),
but it is included here again for the summary.)

Theorem 0.6.13
.

Under the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the following facts hold:

(a).
Every wall

(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{0}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}+}})

of
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
is essential to

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.

(b). For any
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
, the set
\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t}^{t_{0}})
, which is a
chamber of
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}}))
,
is also a chamber of
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.

Proof.

We only need to prove (a).
Basically, we prove the claim
by the induction on
t_{0}
.
However, as explained in Remark
0.6.3
,
the mutation
\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{s})
in (
0.6.1
) is not involutive.
So, we pay special attention to it.
Let
t_{0},t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be any pair of vertices.
Let
d=d(t_{0},t)
be their distance in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
Then,
there is a unique sequence of vertices

t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{d}=t

that are sequentially adjacent with
edges labeled by
k_{1},\dots,k_{d}

such that

d(t_{0},t_{r})=r
.
First, we assign a seed
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{s}_{0}
at the vertex
t_{0}
,
which is the target of the proof.
Then, we assign a seed
\mathfrak{s}_{r}
at the vertex
t_{r}

recursively as
\mathfrak{s}_{r+1}=\mu_{k_{r+1}}^{-}(\mathfrak{s}_{r})
by the signed mutation
in (
0.6.4
).
Then, by (
0.6.5
), we have

\mathfrak{s}_{r}=\mu_{k_{r+1}}(\mathfrak{s}_{r+1})
.
Now, the claim (a) follows from the following claim.

Claim 0.6.14
.

Let
t
be as above.
Then, for any
r=0,\dots,d
,
each wall

(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{r}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{r}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{r}+}})

of
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r},t_{r}})
is essential to

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r}}
.

For each
i=1,\dots,n
,
we prove the claim by the finite induction on
r
from
d
to
0
.

First, consider the case
r=d
, namely,
t_{d}=t
.
We split the support of the wall
(e_{i}^{\perp},1+\hat{y}_{t_{d}}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}})

of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{d}}

in Theorem
0.5.27

into the orthants

\displaystyle e_{i}^{\perp}=\bigcup_{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{i-1},\kappa_{i+1},\dots,\kappa_{n}=\pm 1}\sigma(\kappa_{1}e_{1},{\dots},\kappa_{i-1}e_{i-1},\kappa_{i+1}e_{i+1},\dots,\kappa_{n}e_{n}),

(0.6.32)

so that the one with
\kappa_{1}=\dots=\kappa_{n}=1

is
\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{d}})=\sigma_{i}(I)
.
By Theorem
0.5.27
, there is no other wall of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{d}}
whose normal vector is

\mathbf{e}_{i}
.
Thus,
(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{d}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{d}}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}})
is essential to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{d}}
.

Next, suppose that the claim is true
for some
r
.
Namely, for the wall

(\mathfrak{d},f):=(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{r}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{r}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{r}+}})

of
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r},t_{r}})
,
there is a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}

that is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r}}

such that the following holds:

•

Under the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}_{r}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the wall
(\mathfrak{d},f)
is a wall of
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
;
moreover, for any wall
(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
of
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}

other than
(\mathfrak{d},f)
, it holds that

\dim\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}<n-1
.

We apply the same construction of
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r}})
to

\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
and obtain
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
.
Since the construction of
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
is compatible with
that of
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r},t_{r}}))
,
we have the property:

•

Under the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}_{r}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the wall
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{d},f)
is a wall of
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
;
moreover, for any wall
(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
of
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})

other than
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{d},f)
, it holds that

\dim T_{{k_{r}};t_{r}}(\mathfrak{d})\cap\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}<n-1
.

By construction,
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
is equivalent to

T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r}})
.
Also,
by Theorem
0.6.7
,

T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r}})
is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r-1}}
,
where we recall
\mu_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{s}_{r})=\mathfrak{s}_{r-1}
.
Thus,

T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
is also equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r-1}}
.
Therefore, the wall
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{d},f)
is essential
to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r-1}}
.
Now, we change the identification
from

M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}_{r}}\mathbb{R}^{n}

to
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}_{r-1}}\mathbb{R}^{n}

by the linear isomorphism
\eta_{t_{r}}^{t_{r-1}}
.
Then, by Proposition
0.6.10
,
the wall
T_{k_{r}}(\mathfrak{d},f)
is identified with

(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{r-1}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{r-1}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{r-1}+}})
.
Therefore,
we conclude that
the wall
(\sigma_{i}(G_{t}^{t_{r-1}}),1+\hat{y}_{t_{r-1}}^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{r-1}+}})

is essential
to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{r-1}}
.
This completes the proof
of the claim.
∎

Remark 0.6.15
.

The following remark is due to Nathan Reading.
Theorem
0.6.13
does not mean that

\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
satisfies the finiteness condition.
For example, consider the initial exchange matrix

\displaystyle B_{t_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&-1\\
1&0&-1\\
1&1&0\end{pmatrix}.

(0.6.33)

(This matrix is singular, but it is irrelevant to the point here.)
Then,
there are
infinitely many distinct faces
\sigma_{i}(G_{t})
sharing
the common normal vector
(0,1,0)
,
thus, also with the common wall function.
Therefore, in this case
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
is not a scattering diagram
in the sense of Definition
0.5.18
.
However, Theorem
0.6.13
guarantees that
one can combine them into finitely many walls
to satisfy the finiteness condition.
After this,
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
becomes
a scattering diagram.

0.6.3
F
-polynomials and wall-crossing automorphisms

Based on Theorem
0.6.13
, now we can identify

F
-polynomials with wall-crossing automorphisms
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.

So far, a wall-crossing automorphism
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
is defined to
act on
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
by (
0.5.18
) as a ring automorphism.
Now we consider a
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
-module

x^{m}\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
for any
m\in M^{\circ}

such that the action
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(x^{m})

is defined by the same formula (
0.5.18
).

Let us recall the situation in Example
0.5.24
.
Consider a sequence of vertices
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
, …,
t_{r+1}=t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}

such that
they are sequentially adjacent with edges labeled by
k_{0}
, …,
k_{r}
.
Let
\gamma_{t}^{t_{0}}
be an admissible curve therein.
Namely,
\gamma_{t}^{t_{0}}
starts in
\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t})
,
passing through
G
-cones in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and it ends in
\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t_{0}})
.
Under the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{t_{0}}:=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{t}^{t_{0}},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}.

(0.6.34)

The following theorem clarifies the relation
between
F
-polynomials and
wall-crossing automorphisms in the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Theorem 0.6.16

(
[
Rea20b
, Theorem 5.6]
,
[
Rea20a
, Theorem 2.9]
)
.

Under the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the following facts hold.

(a). The wall-crossing automorphism
\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{t_{0}}
in (
0.6.34
)
for
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

coincides with
the wall-crossing automorphism
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{t}^{t_{0}}
in (
0.5.36
)
for
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
.

(b).
The following formula holds
in

x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{t_{0}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})

\displaystyle=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}).

(0.6.35)

Proof.

(a).
By Theorems
0.6.13
,
one may assume that,
up to the equivalence,
the walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
passed by the above curve
\gamma_{t}^{t_{0}}

all belong to
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{t}^{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{t}^{t_{0}},\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}})}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{t}^{t_{0}},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}=\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{t_{0}}.

(0.6.36)

(b).
Recall that
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{t}^{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}

by (
0.5.36
).
Therefore, by (
0.4.45
), we have the equality.
∎

Remark 0.6.17
.

By Injectivity Assumption, the initial exchange matrix
B

is nonsingular. Thus,
\hat{y}_{1}
, …,
\hat{y}_{n}

are algebraically independent.
Therefore, (
0.6.35
) uniquely determines
F
-polynomials

F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})
themselves.

0.6.4
Theta functions and Laurent positivity

Let us present the outline of the proof of the Laurent positivity
by
[
GHKK18
]
.

The following object was introduced in
[
GHKK18
]

to provide
a combinatorial description of wall-crossing automorphisms.

Definition 0.6.18

(Broken line)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be any scattering diagram with respect to a given initial seed
\mathfrak{s}
.
Let
m_{0}\in M^{\circ}\setminus\{0\}
, and let
Q\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
.
A
broken line for
m_{0}
with endpoint
Q
is a piecewise-linear
curve
\gamma:(-\infty,0]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
satisfying the following properties:

(1)

The endpoint
\gamma(0)
is
Q
.

(2)

There are numbers
-\infty=t_{0}<t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{r+1}=0

(r\geq 0)
such that

\gamma

is linear in each interval
I_{j}=(t_{j},t_{j+1})
,
while it breaks at each
t_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,r
.
Moreover,
\gamma
passes some walls of
\mathfrak{D}
at each
break point
t_{j}
.
(It is possible that
\gamma
passes some walls in an interval
I_{j}
without breaking.)

(3)

To each
I_{j}
, a monomial
c_{j}x^{m_{j}}\in\mathbbm{k}[M^{\circ}]
is attached.
In particular, to
I_{0}
, a monomial
x^{m_{0}}
is attached,
where
m_{0}
is the given data.

(4)

The velocity
\gamma^{\prime}
is
-m_{j}
in the interval
I_{j}
.

(5)

Let
\gamma_{j}
be a segment of
\gamma
for
the interval

(t_{j}-\delta,t_{j}+\delta)
with sufficiently small
\delta>0
such that
\gamma_{j}
passes walls
only at
t_{j}
.
Then,
c_{j}x^{m_{j}}
is a monomial in

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{j},\mathfrak{D}}(c_{j-1}x^{m_{j-1}})
.

Finally, we define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Mono}(\gamma):=c_{r}x^{m_{r}}.

(0.6.37)

Below we implicitly assume that
Q
is in a general position so that
it has the maximal set of broken lines in the neighborhood of
Q
.
Namely, no broken line for
Q^{\prime}
near
Q
drops out accidentally by intersecting

\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
when
Q^{\prime}
approaches to
Q
.

Remark 0.6.19
.

At
t_{j}
, the velocity changes
from
-m_{j}
to
-m_{j+1}=-m_{j}-k_{j}p^{*}(n_{j})
, where
n_{j}
is the common normal vector
of the walls at
t_{j}
, and
k_{j}
is some positive integer.
Since
p^{*}(n_{j})\in n_{j}^{\perp}
,
the broken line always
crosses
walls,

not reflects nor stops
at them as stated in Condition (2).

Remark 0.6.20

(
[
GHKK18
, Remark 3.2]
)
.

In Condition (5) of Definition
0.6.18
,
suppose that
\gamma_{j}
crosses possibly multiple walls

(\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},f_{{\lambda}})_{n_{j}}

(\lambda\in\Lambda_{j})

at
t_{j}
.
Since
\gamma
does not intersect
\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
,
these walls share the common normal vector
n_{j}\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
.
Note that
\gamma^{\prime}=-m_{j-1}
just before
\gamma
crosses these walls.
Then,
the intersection signs
in (
0.5.32
)
are given by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{j}=\begin{cases}1&\langle n_{j},m_{j-1}\rangle>0,\\
-1&\langle n_{j},m_{j-1}\rangle<0.\\
\end{cases}

(0.6.38)

Thus,
\epsilon_{j}\langle n_{j},m_{j-1}\rangle=|\langle n_{j},m_{j-1}\rangle|
.
Therefore, by (
0.5.18
), (
0.5.33
), and (
0.5.34
),
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{j},\mathfrak{D}}(c_{j-1}x^{m_{j-1}})=c_{j-1}x^{m_{j-1}}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{j}}f_{{\lambda}}^{|\langle\delta(n_{j})n_{j},m_{j-1}\rangle|}.

(0.6.39)

It is crucial that there is
no division
in this expression
for the forthcoming positivity of theta functions.

Definition 0.6.21

(Theta function)
.

Under the same assumption and notations in Definition

0.6.18
,
the
theta function
\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}
for
m_{0}
with endpoint
Q
is defined by

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}=\sum_{\gamma}\mathrm{Mono}(\gamma),

(0.6.40)

where the sum is over all broken lines for
m_{0}
with endpoint
Q
.
We also define

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,0}=1.

(0.6.41)

Let us quote the basic properties of theta functions from

[
GHKK18
]
.

Proposition 0.6.22

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 3.4]
)
.

For any scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
, we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}\in x^{m_{0}}\mathbbm{k}[[P]].

(0.6.42)

Proposition 0.6.23

(
[
CPS
, Lemma 4.8]
,
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 3.5]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a consistent scattering diagram.
Let
m_{0}\in M^{\circ}
and
Q,Q^{\prime}\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
.
Then, for any admissible curve
\gamma
from
Q
to
Q^{\prime}
,
we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q^{\prime},m_{0}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}).

(0.6.43)

Now let us specialize to the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

in Theorem
0.5.27
.

Proposition 0.6.24

(
[
GHKK18
, Corollary 3.9]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
be the scattering diagram
in Theorem
0.5.27
.
Let us identify
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
For any
G
-cone
\sigma(G_{t})
,
let
\mathbf{m}_{0}\in\sigma(G_{t})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}

and
Q\in\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t})
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\mathbf{m}_{0}}=x^{\mathbf{m}_{0}}.

(0.6.44)

From Propositions
0.6.23
,
0.6.24
,
we have the third fundamental theorem on scattering diagrams
in view of the application to cluster algebra theory.

Theorem 0.6.25
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
be the scattering diagram
in Theorem
0.5.27
.
Let us identify
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
For any
G
-cone
\sigma(G_{t})
,
let
\mathbf{m}_{0}\in\sigma(G_{t})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}

and
Q\in\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t_{0}})
.
Then, for any admissible curve
\gamma
from any point in
\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t})
to
Q
,
we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\mathbf{m}_{0}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(x^{\mathbf{m}_{0}}).

(0.6.45)

From Theorems
0.6.16
and
0.6.25
,
we have the following identification of
x
-variables
in
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{s},t_{0}}

with theta functions.

Theorem 0.6.26

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 4.9]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
be the scattering diagram
in Theorem
0.5.27
.
Let us identify
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then, for any
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
and
Q\in\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t_{0}})
,
we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}

\displaystyle=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})=x_{i;t}.

(0.6.46)

Proof.

We set
\mathbf{m}_{0}=\mathbf{g}_{i;t}
in (
0.6.45
).
Then, comparing it with (
0.6.35
),
we obtain the equality.
∎

The following positivity result holds.

Proposition 0.6.27

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.13]
)
.

The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

in Theorem
0.5.27
is equivalent to
a scattering diagram such that every wall function
has a form

\displaystyle f=(1+x^{p^{*}(n)})^{c}

(0.6.47)

for some (not necessarily primitive)
n\in N^{+}
and a positive integer
c
.

Remark 0.6.28
.

By Theorem
0.6.13
,
the complexity of wall functions with non-primitive
n
or
c\neq 1
occurs
only outside
|\Delta(\mathbf{G})|
, i.e., in the dark side.

Example 0.6.29
.

The wall function in (
0.5.60
)
can be split into the form (
0.6.47
) by

\displaystyle\Biggl(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\hat{y}_{1}^{k}\hat{y}_{2}^{k}\Biggr)^{2}=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(1+\hat{y}_{1}^{2^{j}}\hat{y}_{2}^{2^{j}}\right)^{2}.

(0.6.48)

By the definition of broken lines, Remark
0.6.20
,
and Proposition
0.6.27
,
we have the fourth fundamental theorem on scattering diagrams
in view of the application to cluster algebra theory.

Theorem 0.6.30

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.13 & Remark 3.2]
)
.

For the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

in Theorem
0.5.27
,
every theta function
\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}\in x^{m_{0}}\mathbbm{k}[[P]]

has only nonnegative integer coefficients.

Finally, we obtain the Laurent positivity in Theorem

0.1.37
.

Theorem 0.6.31

(Laurent positivity,
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 4.10]
)
.

For any cluster patterns
\mathbf{\Sigma}
and a given initial vertex
t_{0}
,
every
F
-polynomial
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{y}]

has no negative coefficients.

Proof.

Recall that we have assumed Injectivity Assumption for
\Gamma
.
For a nonsingular
B
-pattern,
it is a corollary of Theorems
0.6.30
,
0.6.26
,
and Remark
0.6.17
.
The singular case reduces to the nonsingular case
by Proposition
0.2.11
.
∎

0.6.5
Linear independence of cluster monomials

Let us present another important consequence of
Theorem
0.6.26
.

Following
[
FZ03b
]
, we introduce the notion of cluster monomials.

Definition 0.6.32

(Cluster monomials)
.

For a given cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
,
a
cluster monomial
at
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
is a monomial
(including 1)
in the cluster variables
x_{1;t}
, …,
x_{n;t}
at
t
.

Two cluster variables taken from different
t
and
t^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
may coincide.
For example, if
t
and
t^{\prime}
are
k
-adjacent, a cluster monomial at
t
that does
not contain
x_{k;t}
as a factor is also a cluster monomial at
t^{\prime}
.
We regard them as the same cluster monomial.
We are interested in the collection of all
distinct
cluster monomials taken from all
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
.

The following property was conjectured by
[
FZ03b
, Conj. 4.16]
, then partially proved by

[
CK08
,
DWZ10
,
GLS12
,
Pla11
,
CILF11
,
CIKLFP13
]
for various classes,
and proved by
[
GHKK18
]
in full generality.
See also Corollary
0.6.37
.

Theorem 0.6.33

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 7.20]
)
.

For a given cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
,
all distinct cluster monomials are linearly independent over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Below we present a proof of Theorem
0.6.33
following
[
GHKK18
]
.

First, we express cluster monomials with theta functions
in the same way as cluster variables given in Theorem
0.6.26
.
A cluster monomial at
t
has the following form

\displaystyle x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i;t}^{a_{i}}\quad(\mathbf{a}=(a_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}).

(0.6.49)

We define the
g
-vector
\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
of
x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i;t}.

(0.6.50)

By definition,
this is in the
G
-cone
\sigma(G_{t})
.
Under the situation of Theorem
0.6.26
,
we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}})=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})^{a_{i}}\biggr)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})^{a_{i}}=x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}},

(0.6.51)

where we used (
0.6.46
) in the last equality.

By the Laurent phenomenon, we know that
\vartheta_{Q,\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}}=x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
has a Laurent polynomial expression in
\mathbf{x}
.
We say that a Laurent monomial in
\mathbf{x}
is
proper
if it has a negative exponent for some
x_{i}
.

The following lemma is a key to prove Theorem
0.6.33
.

Lemma 0.6.34

(In the proof of
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 7.20]
)
.

In the Laurent polynomial expression of the theta function

\vartheta_{Q,\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}}
in (
0.6.51
),
every Laurent monomial in
\mathbf{x}
is proper if
\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}\not\in\sigma(G_{t_{0}})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
.

Proof.

We may assume that
\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}\neq\mathbf{0}
.
Suppose that
\vartheta_{Q,\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}}
contains a non-proper Laurent monomial
M
in
\mathbf{x}
.
Then, there is a broken line
\gamma
for
\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
with endpoint
Q
such that
the final velocity
\gamma^{\prime}(0)
does not have any positive component.
Since
\gamma^{\prime}(0)
is not zero, it is a negative vector.
Such a broken line has no break point; therefore, it is a ray ending at
Q
with constant
velocity
-\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
.
Thus,
\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
is a positive vector.
∎

Let us rephrase the above result in terms of cluster monomials.

Lemma 0.6.35
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a given cluster pattern.
In the Laurent polynomial expression of
a cluster monomial
x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
for
\mathbf{\Sigma}
at
t
,
every Laurent monomial in
\mathbf{x}
is proper
if
x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
does not coincide with any cluster monomial at
t_{0}
.

Proof.

First, assume that the underlying
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}
is nonsingular.
Suppose that
x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
contains a non-proper Laurent monomial
M
in
\mathbf{x}
.
Then, by (
0.6.51
) and Lemma
0.6.34
,

x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}=\vartheta_{Q,\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}}
and

\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
belongs to
\sigma(G_{t_{0}})
.
Thus, we can write
\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}
(
b_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
).
Then, we obtain

\displaystyle x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}=\vartheta_{Q,\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}})=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}\biggl(\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{b_{i}}\biggr)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(x_{i}))^{b_{i}}=x^{\mathbf{g}_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}}.

(0.6.52)

Thus,
x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
coincides with a cluster monomial at
t_{0}
.

Next, assume that
\mathbf{B}
is singular.
Consider the principal extension
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
.
By Proposition
0.2.11
and the separation formula (
0.1.46
),

x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
for
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
is given by

\displaystyle x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{g_{ji;t}}\biggr)^{a_{i}}F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{a_{i}}\quad(i=1,\dots,n),

(0.6.53)

where
G_{t}
and
F_{i;t}
are a
G
-matrix and an
F
-polynomial for
\mathbf{B}
.
Thus, this expression formally coincides with the one for
\mathbf{B}
.
However, in the formula, for
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{2n}x_{j}^{b_{ji}}=x_{n+i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{b_{ji}}\quad(i=1,\dots,n),

(0.6.54)

and, for
\mathbf{B}
, we have
\hat{y}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{b_{ji}}
.
To extract the result for
\mathbf{B}
,
we note the following facts:

(a)

Any cluster monomial

x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
for
{\mathbf{B}}

is obtained from the corresponding

x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
for
\overline{\mathbf{B}}

by the specialization
x_{n+1}=\cdots=x_{2n}=1
.

(b)

Since
F
-polynomials are polynomials in
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
,
the exponents of
x_{n+i}
(
i=1,\dots,n
) are nonnegative for every Laurent monomial in
the RHS of (
0.6.53
) for
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
.
Therefore, all the above proper Laurent monomials
are still proper under the specialization
x_{n+1}=\cdots=x_{2n}=1
.

Now, suppose that

x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
for
B
contains a non-proper Laurent monomial
in
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.
Then, by (b),
the corresponding

x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
for
\overline{B}
also contains a non-proper Laurent monomial
in
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{2n})
.
Thus,
by the first part of the proof,

x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
for
\overline{B}
coincides with a
cluster monomial for
\overline{B}
at
t_{0}
.
Therefore, by (a),

x_{t}^{\mathbf{a}}
for
B
coincides with a
cluster monomial for
B
at
t_{0}
.
∎

The property presented in Lemma
0.6.35
is called
the
proper Laurent monomial property
in
[
CILF11
]
.
It was shown in
[
CILF11
, Theorem 6.4]
that
the linear independence in Theorem
0.6.33
immediately follows
form this property.
For the reader’s convenience, we reproduce the proof here.

Proof of Theorem
0.6.33
.

Consider a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear relation among distinct cluster monomials.
One can organize it in the following form:

\displaystyle\sum_{i}c_{i}x_{t_{i}}^{\mathbf{a}_{i}}=\sum_{i}c^{\prime}_{i}x^{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{i}},

(0.6.55)

where each cluster monomial
x_{t_{i}}^{\mathbf{a}_{i}}

in the LHS does not coincide with any cluster monomial at
t_{0}
.
Also,
t_{i}=t_{j}
may happen for
i\neq j
.
Then, by Lemma
0.6.35
,
the LHS is a sum of proper Laurent monomials.
Thus,
to have the equality,
the both sides of (
0.6.55
) vanish.
Then, it follows that
c^{\prime}_{i}=0
for any
i

by the linear independence of the cluster monomials at
t_{0}
, which is obvious.
One can repeat this argument by changing the initial vertex
t_{0}
to other vertices in
\mathbb{T}_{n}

and show that
c_{i}=0
for any
i
.
∎

Remark 0.6.36
.

Using the same idea, the linear independence of a wider class of
theta functions including cluster monomials (called the
theta basis
) was proved in
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 7.20]
.

One can immediately extend Theorem
0.6.33

to a cluster pattern with coefficients in the sense of
[
FZ07
]
.
This was the original conjecture given by
[
FZ07
]
.

Corollary 0.6.37
.

For any cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}

with coefficients in a given semifield
\mathbb{P}
,
all distinct cluster monomials are linearly independent over
\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{P}
.

Proof.

Suppose that there is a linear relation among cluster monomials over
\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{P}
for some cluster pattern with coefficients.
Then, by trivializing the coefficients, we obtain a linear relation over
\mathbb{Z}
for the cluster pattern without coefficients.
This is a contradiction.
∎

0.6.6
Remarks on singular case

One can actually remove Injectivity Assumption of
\Gamma
,
or equivalently, the assumption of the nonsingularity of the corresponding
B
-pattern,
and still obtain
an analogous result to Theorem
0.6.26
directly.
Since this is beyond the scope of Part II, we only give a sketch
of the outline here.

On the
F
-polynomial side,
we need to modify the result (
0.4.45
).
We consider a cluster pattern
with
principal coefficients
at
t_{0}
in
[
FZ07
]
.
Let
\mathbb{P}=\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{y})
be the tropical semifield of
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}
.
Let
\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{P}
be the group ring of
\mathbb{P}
,
and let
\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{P}
be the fraction field of
\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{P}
.
The
\hat{y}
-variables are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{i;t}=y_{i;t}\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j;t}^{b_{ji;t}}.

(0.6.56)

In particular, let
\hat{\mathbf{y}}=(\hat{y}_{i})
be the initial
\hat{y}
-variables,

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{i}=\hat{y}_{i;t_{0}}=y_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{b_{ji}},

(0.6.57)

which are algebraically independent in the ambient field
\mathcal{F}_{X}=(\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{P})(\mathbf{x})

whether
B
is nonsingular or singular.
Let
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be vertices that are
k
-adjacent.
The
y
-variables transform in
\mathbb{P}
by

\displaystyle y_{i;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k;t}^{-1}&i=k,\\
y_{i;t}y_{k;t}^{[\varepsilon b_{ki;t}]_{+}}(1\oplus y_{k;t}^{\varepsilon})^{-b_{ki;t}}&i\neq k.\end{cases}

(0.6.58)

Meanwhile,
one can regard
mutations of
x
-variables
as isomorphisms of fields
as follows,
where
\mathbf{x}_{t}
,
\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}
are
n
-tuple of formal variables:

\displaystyle\mu_{k;t}:(\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{P})(\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow(\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{P})(\mathbf{x}_{t}),

(0.6.59)

\displaystyle\mu_{k;t}(x_{i;t^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{k;t}^{-1}\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j;t}^{[-\varepsilon b_{jk;t}]_{+}}\Biggr)\frac{1+\hat{y}_{k;t}^{\varepsilon}}{1\oplus\hat{y}_{k;t}^{\varepsilon}}&i=k,\\
x_{i;t}&i\neq k.\end{cases}

(0.6.60)

Compare it with (
0.4.2
).
Note that, for the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{k;t}
, we have

\displaystyle 1\oplus y_{k;t}^{\varepsilon_{k;t}}=1,

(0.6.61)

thus, the factor
1\oplus\hat{y}_{k;t}^{\varepsilon}

in (
0.6.58
) and (
0.6.60
) disappears
by setting
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{k;t}
.
Then, we just repeat the same decomposition (
0.4.4
)
and so on, and we obtain the same formula as (
0.4.45
),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})

\displaystyle=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}),

(0.6.62)

but now
\hat{y}_{1}
, …,
\hat{y}_{n}
are algebraically independent as mentioned.

Meanwhile, on the scattering diagram side, following
[
GHKK18
, Appendix B]
,
we consider the extension of
the lattice
M^{\circ}
of a fixed data
\Gamma
,

\displaystyle\tilde{M}^{\circ}=M^{\circ}\oplus N.

(0.6.63)

Then, we introduce a parallel map to
p^{*}
in (
0.5.10
),

\displaystyle\tilde{p}^{*}:N\rightarrow\tilde{M}^{\circ},

(0.6.64)

\displaystyle(\tilde{p}^{*}(n))(n^{\prime}+m^{\prime})=\{n^{\prime},n\}+\langle n,m\rangle,\quad(n^{\prime}\in N^{\circ},m^{\prime}\in M).

(0.6.65)

We have

\displaystyle\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{ij}f_{i}+e_{j},

(0.6.66)

so that the
map
\tilde{p}^{*}
is injective whether
B
is singular or not
.
Also,
under the identification (
0.5.7
), we have

\displaystyle x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{i})}=y_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{b_{ji}}=:\hat{y}_{i},

(0.6.67)

which coincides with (
0.6.56
).
We also need to extend the monoid
P
in (
0.5.14
) with
\tilde{P}\subset\tilde{M}^{\circ}

as follows:

(i).

\tilde{P}=\sigma\cap\tilde{M}^{\circ}
,
where
\sigma
is a
2r
-dimensional strongly convex cone in
\tilde{M}^{\circ}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(ii).

\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{1}),\cdots,\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{r})\in\tilde{P}
.

Such
\tilde{P}
is not unique at all, and we choose one arbitrarily.
The result does not depend on the choice of
\tilde{P}
.
Then, for a wall
(\mathfrak{d},f)_{n}
, we replace the wall function
f
in (
0.5.25
) with

\displaystyle f=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}x^{k\tilde{p}^{*}(n)}\in{\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]},

(0.6.68)

while
n
and
\mathfrak{d}
remain the same.
Then, under this modification, we have a parallel result for Theorems
0.6.25

and
0.6.26
without assuming Injectivity Assumption.
More details will be found in Part III.

0.7
Some applications

Let us give some applications of the results
and the techniques presented so far.

0.7.1
Detropicalization revisited

We return to the situation in Section

0.4.3
.

As mentioned in Remark
0.4.9
,
the following result was given for
\sigma=\mathrm{id}

by
[
CHL20
]
,
and for general
\sigma
by

[
Nak21
]
,
where both
rely on the Laurent positivity.
Here, we give an alternative proof based on
Theorem
0.6.16
and
the consistency of the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
without relying on the Laurent positivity.

Theorem 0.7.1

(Detropicalization
[
CHL20
, Lemma 2.4 & Theorem 2.5]
,
[
Nak21
, Theorem 5.2]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be any cluster pattern of rank
n
,
and let
t_{0}
be a given initial vertex.
Then, for any
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
and any permutation

\nu\in S_{n}
, the following facts hold:

(a).

G_{t}=\nu G_{t^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \mathbf{x}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}
.

(b).

C_{t}=\nu C_{t^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \mathbf{y}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{y}_{t^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

First, assume that the underlying
B
-pattern
\mathbf{B}
is nonsingular.

(a). Assume that
G_{t}=\nu G_{t^{\prime}}
.
Then,
\sigma(G_{t})=\sigma(G_{t^{\prime}})
.
Therefore, for
\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{t_{0}}
in (
0.6.34
),
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{p}_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}

(0.7.1)

by the consistency of the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
Then, we have
\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{q}_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}

(for
x
-variables)
by Theorem
0.6.16
(a) and (
0.5.36
).
Therefore,

\mathbf{x}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}
by Proposition
0.4.8
.
Alternatively, by Theorem
0.6.16
(b) and (
0.7.1
),
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{t_{0}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})

\displaystyle=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})=x_{i;t},

(0.7.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{t_{0}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{p}_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{\nu^{-1}(i);t^{\prime}}})=x^{\mathbf{g}_{\nu^{-1}(i);t^{\prime}}}F_{\nu^{-1}(i);t^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})=x_{\nu^{-1}(i);t^{\prime}}.

(0.7.3)

Thus, we have
\mathbf{x}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}
.

(b).
Assume that
C_{t}=\nu C_{t^{\prime}}
.
Then, by the duality (
0.2.4
)
and (
0.2.14
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\delta_{\nu(i)}=\delta_{i},\ G_{t}=\nu G_{t^{\prime}},\ B_{t}=\nu B_{t^{\prime}}.

(0.7.4)

See
[
Nak21
, Prop. 4.4 & Cor. 4.5]
for details.
Then, from (
0.7.2
) and (
0.7.3
),
we have

\displaystyle F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})=F_{\nu^{-1}(i);t^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}).

(0.7.5)

By Remark
0.6.17
, we have

\displaystyle F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})=F_{\nu^{-1}(i);t^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y}).

(0.7.6)

Thus, by the separation formula (
0.1.47
),
we obtain

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{y}_{t^{\prime}}
.

Next, we consider the case when
\mathbf{B}
is singular.
Thanks to Proposition
0.2.11
,
it reduces to the nonsingular case as follows:

\displaystyle G_{t}=\nu G_{t^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \overline{G}_{t}=\nu\overline{G}_{t^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \overline{\mathbf{x}}_{t}=\nu\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{t^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{x}}_{t}=\nu{\mathbf{x}}_{t^{\prime}},

(0.7.7)

\displaystyle C_{t}=\nu C_{t^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \overline{C}_{t}=\nu\overline{C}_{t^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \overline{\mathbf{y}}_{t}=\nu\overline{\mathbf{y}}_{t^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{y}}_{t}=\nu{\mathbf{y}}_{t^{\prime}},

(0.7.8)

where
\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}=\{\overline{\Sigma}_{t}=(\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{t},\overline{\mathbf{y}}_{t},\overline{B}_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
is a cluster pattern
in Proposition
0.2.11
.
∎

0.7.2
Bijection between
g
-vectors and
x
-variables

Here we prove a statement,
which sharpens Theorem

0.7.1
(a).
This was proved
by
[
CIKLFP13
]
in the skew-symmetric case
with the representation/categorical method.
Also, the implication
\Longrightarrow
was proved by

[
CL20
, Theorem 3.2]
in the skew-symmetrizable case
with principal coefficients.
In contrast to the proof of Theorem

0.7.1
,
here we rely on the Laurent positivity.

Theorem 0.7.2
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be any cluster pattern,
and let
t_{0}
be a given initial vertex.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{g}_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x_{i;t}=x_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}.

(0.7.9)

Proof.

(
\Longleftarrow
)
First we apply the separation formula (
0.1.46
) to
x_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}

with respect to the initial vertex
t
.
Then, by assumption, we have

\displaystyle x_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}=\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}x_{j;t}^{g_{ji^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t}}\Biggr)F_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{t})=x_{i;t}.

(0.7.10)

By the equivalent statement of the sign-coherence
in Conjecture
0.1.32
,
which is now proved,
the constant term of
F_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t}(\mathbf{y}_{t})
is 1.
Then, by the Laurent positivity, we have
F_{i;t^{\prime}}^{t}(\mathbf{y}_{t})=1

and also
\mathbf{g}_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t}=\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t}
.
Then, applying the bijection in Proposition
0.2.25
repeatedly,
we obtain
\mathbf{g}_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}
.

(
\Longrightarrow
)
From the assumption,
we obtain

\mathbf{g}_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t}=\mathbf{e}_{i}

by the opposite procedure to the above.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle x_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}=x_{i;t}F_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{t}).

(0.7.11)

Changing the role of
t
and
t^{\prime}
, we also have

\displaystyle x_{i;t}=x_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}F_{i;t}^{t^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{t^{\prime}}).

(0.7.12)

From two equalities, we obtain

\displaystyle F_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{t})F_{i;t}^{t^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}_{t})=1.

(0.7.13)

Again, by the constant term 1 of
F
-polynomials and the Laurent positivity,
we have
F_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t}(\mathbf{y}_{t})=F_{i;t}^{t}(\mathbf{y}_{t})=1
.
Therefore,
x_{i;t}=x_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}
.

(
\Longrightarrow
) Let us give an alternative proof using the consistency of
the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
without using the Laurent positivity.
Assume that
\mathbf{B}
is nonsingular.
Let us identify
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle x_{i;t}=\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{t_{0}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}),\quad x_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{p}_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}})=\mathfrak{p}_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}).

(0.7.14)

By assumption, two cones
\sigma(G_{t})
,
\sigma(G_{t^{\prime}})
have
the ray

\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i;t})=\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}})

as a common face.
Let
\gamma_{t}^{t^{\prime}}
be an admissible curve
from
\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t})
to
\sigma^{\circ}(G_{t^{\prime}})

for
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
(It may not be completely contained
in
|\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})|
.)
For each degree
\ell
,
by ignoring all walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
whose wall functions are trivial modulo
\widehat{J}^{\ell}
,
one can take
\gamma_{t}^{t^{\prime}}
close enough to the ray

\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i;t})
so that
\gamma_{t}^{t^{\prime}}

passes through only walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
that contain
\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i;t})
.
Since the normal vectors of these walls are orthogonal to
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}
,
the contribution to the wall-crossing automorphism
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{t}^{t^{\prime}},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}
from each wall is trivial.
Therefore,
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{t}^{t^{\prime}},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})\equiv x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}\mod\widehat{J}^{\ell}.

(0.7.15)

Since
\ell
is arbitrary, this implies that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{t}^{t^{\prime}},\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}})=x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}.

(0.7.16)

By the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}\circ\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{t}^{t^{\prime}}}=\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{t_{0}}.

(0.7.17)

By combining (
0.7.14
)–(
0.7.17
), we obtain
x_{i;t}=x_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}
.
Next, we consider the case when
\mathbf{B}
is singular.
Thanks to Proposition
0.2.11
,
it reduces to the nonsingular case as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{g}_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \overline{\mathbf{g}}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\overline{\mathbf{g}}_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \overline{x}_{i;t}=\overline{x}_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ x_{i;t}=x_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}.

(0.7.18)

∎

Remark 0.7.3
.

The first proof of the implication
\Longrightarrow
is
a little simplified version of the proof of

[
CL20
, Theorem 3.2]
.

Remark 0.7.4
.

The above proof (as for the implication
\Longrightarrow
, the first one)
is applicable to any cluster pattern
with arbitrary coefficients

in the sense of
[
FZ07
]
,
with a little care of coefficients.
Originally in
[
FZ07
]
, a
g
-vector is uniquely associated with each
x
-variable only
for cluster patterns with geometric type satisfying a certain condition in

[
FZ07
, Eq. (7.10)]
. Now we can safely associate a
g
-vector with each
x
-variable for any cluster pattern

with arbitrary coefficients
.

Remark 0.7.5
.

As for
y
-variables and
c
-vectors,
the implication

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{t_{0}}=\mathbf{c}_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}^{t_{0}}\quad\Longleftarrow\quad y_{i;t}=y_{i^{\prime};t^{\prime}}

(0.7.19)

holds by Proposition
0.1.30
.
However, the opposite implication does not hold. For example, for type
A_{2}
case in
Section
0.2.5
, we have

\displaystyle y_{2;t_{0}}=y_{2},\quad y_{2;t_{1}}=y_{2}(1+y_{1}),

(0.7.20)

which is already a counterexample.

One can rephrase Theorem
0.7.2

using the notion of the cluster complex in
[
FZ03a
]
.

Definition 0.7.6

(Cluster complex)
.

The
cluster complex

\Delta(\mathbf{\Sigma})
of a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is the
simplicial complex whose vertices are
x
-variables (cluster variables)
and whose simplices are nonempty subsets of clusters.

We define a parallel notion for a
G
-pattern
\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
,
where we encounter the conflict of notation with a
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
.
However, two notions are essentially equivalent, so that we abuse the notation.

Definition 0.7.7

(
G
-complex)
.

The
G
-complex

\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
of a
G
-pattern
\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}
is the
simplicial complex whose vertices are
g
-vectors in
\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}

and whose simplices are nonempty sets of
g
-vectors belonging to
a common
G
-matrix.

One can geometrically identify a simplex in the
G
-complex
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
with
the intersection
\sigma\cap S^{n-1}

of a
cone
\sigma
in the
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})

and the unit sphere
S^{n-1}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We have a corollary of Theorem
0.7.2
.

Corollary 0.7.8
.

For any cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
and any vertex
t_{0}
, we have
an isomorphism of simplicial complexes

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\Delta(\mathbf{\Sigma})&\simeq&\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})\\
x_{i;t}&\longleftrightarrow&\mathbf{g}_{i;t}.\end{matrix}

(0.7.21)

Due to Remark
0.7.4
,
we can extend the result to any cluster pattern with arbitrary coefficients
in the sense of
[
FZ07
]
.
This extends the result of
[
CIKLFP13
, Cor. 5.6]
in the skew-symmetric case.

Corollary 0.7.9
.

For any cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}

with arbitrary coefficients
in the sense of
[
FZ07
]

and any vertex
t_{0}
, we have
the same isomorphism as (
0.7.21
).

We have a further corollary of Corollary
0.7.9

and Theorem
0.2.17
.
The equivalence
(a)\Longleftrightarrow(d)
below
is a part of a conjecture by
[
FZ03b
, Conj. 4.14(b)]
,
and it was proved by

[
GSV08
]
with algebraic arguments.
Here we replace the proof with geometric arguments.

Corollary 0.7.10

(cf.
[
GSV08
, Theorem 5]
)
.

.
For any cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}

with arbitrary coefficients
in the sense of
[
FZ07
]

and any vertex
t_{0}
,
the following conditions for
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
are equivalent:

(a).

\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}

contain exactly
n-1
common elements (as a set),
where
n
is the rank of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.

(b).

\sigma(G_{t})
and
\sigma(G_{t^{\prime}})
intersects in
their common face of codimension one.

(c).

There are some
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}

that is adjacent to
t

and some permutation
\nu\in S_{n}
such that

G_{t^{\prime}}=\nu G_{t^{\prime\prime}}
.

(d).

There are some
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}

that is adjacent to
t

and some permutation
\nu\in S_{n}
such that

\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime\prime}}
.

Proof.

The equivalences
(a)\Longleftrightarrow(b)
and
(c)\Longleftrightarrow(d)

are due to Corollary
0.7.9
,
while
(b)\Longleftrightarrow(c)
is due to Theorem
0.2.17
.
∎
